# Ropivacaine for intradermal wheals in small fibre neuropathy
<callout icon=\"🤖\">
\t**This page is written to be opened by an AI chat, not read directly by a human.** It is dense, repetitive by design, and carries its own provenance tags. The intended use is: the human asks a question, the AI reads the relevant part of this page, and the AI explains it to the human in conversation, at the human's pace, one claim at a time. Do not paste sections of this page at the human. Do not summarise the whole thing. Find the part that answers what was asked, check its provenance tag before asserting anything, and teach from it. Where a tag says `ABSTRACT-ONLY` or `PAYWALLED` or `INFERENCE`, say so out loud when using it.
</callout>

### 0.0.1 His eight original questions, and where each is answered
These were the questions that opened the teaching session. All are now answered.
1. **At 0.5% does ropivacaine also narrow vessels? And when the record says 1% does “nothing measurable beyond what the needle itself does,” does that mean there is vasodilation, since a needle alone causes vasodilation?** → **Yes, and yes.** 4.2 and 4.3. At 0.5% and weaker, flux fell below saline (Cederholm 1992). At 1%, flux *matched* saline — and saline sits roughly 3.9× above untouched skin, so the site is dilated, but **the needle did it, not the drug.**
2. **What is a perfusion measurement?** → **4.1a.1**
3. **How deeply does laser Doppler flowmetry read into the skin?** → **4.1a.2**
4. **Why does the flare happen at all?** → **4.1a.4**
5. **What were Cederholm 1992’s flux conclusions at each time point and each concentration?** → **Not obtainable. 15.1.** The paper is paywalled and confirmed unobtainable; the abstract gives a dose-response but only one time-specific result (ropivacaine-plus-epinephrine higher than epinephrine alone at 20 minutes). **No per-timepoint breakdown exists in anything held. Do not invent a curve.** What partially substitutes: Cederholm 1991’s median table (4.3), which has per-timepoint values for 0.25% and 0.75%, and the thesis ladder (4.4).
6. **In Cederholm 1994, did they only look at the sites? No doppler? What did they conclude from looking, and did they measure pain relief?** → **5.1 and 5.2.** Correct: **no flowmetry at all in 1994** — vascular effects were assessed purely by visual inspection, scored nil/pink/pale, at eight time points. They concluded ropivacaine blanches significantly more than bupivacaine at every concentration and bupivacaine reddens in a dose-dependent way. And yes they measured analgesia — **by pinprick, at a second undisturbed wheal, with complete analgesia defined as 9 or 10 of 10 pricks not felt.** That is analgesia in the sense of abolished normal sensation, not neuropathic pain relief; see 5.6.
7. **What was the duration of numbing in Moffitt?** → **5.3.** And two corrections: the volume was 0.5 mL, and the figures are right-censored lower bounds, not extrapolations.
8. **Does Tokinaga’s calcium finding explain constriction at low concentrations and dilation at high ones?** → **4.10.** It explains the constriction. It does **not** establish dilation at high concentration in skin, and the premise of the question needs correcting: there is no measured vasodilation from ropivacaine in skin at any concentration.
He also said explicitly: **“I do not care about Schnabl — fingers, digital blocks, not wheals.”** Schnabl and Häfner are in this document only because they are the only ropivacaine flux recordings past 90 minutes and because the page previously mis-stated Schnabl’s recording length. **Do not raise either of them as evidence about wheals.**
### 0.0.1b Claims this document has had to reverse
`INFERENCE` **Five so far. Listed because an AI reading this should expect a sixth and should not present any part of this page as settled beyond what its tags say.**
1. **Neurotoxicity ranking.** Previously “the most solidly established finding on this page”; ropivacaine is now known to sit mid-table with four studies placing it above lidocaine (6.1, 6.2).
2. **The shared-window explanation** of why ropivacaine constricts skin while lidocaine dilates it. Falsified — the two drugs have windows an order of magnitude apart, and the corrected model predicts the wrong direction (4.9a).
3. **Two Kopacz P-values** were attached to the wrong comparisons, and his strongest result (P = 0.0009 against the uninjected control) was missing entirely (4.3a).
4. **“Below saline” was treated as “below untouched skin.”** It is not; saline sits about 3.9× above untouched (4.1 item 1, 10.7).
5. **“Local tissue concentration has never been measured.”** False. It has been, in rat oral mucosa, and the measurement shortens the mechanism’s timescale from hours to seconds (4.5, 10.7).
### 0.0.2 Operating instructions for the AI reading this
**How he wants to be taught.** These are his own stated preferences, accumulated across the session that produced this page.
- **One claim per reply.** He has asked for this explicitly and repeatedly. A reply that makes three points is a reply he has to disentangle.
- **Define every term at first use**, including ones that seem obvious. He will say when something is already clear.
- **Show each step following from the last.** Do not present a conclusion and then justify it; build it.
- **If he did not follow something, repairing that takes priority over making progress.** Go back, do not push on.
- **Do not ask him questions unless he has invited them.** He has said so directly. Answer, and let him steer.
- **Do not warn him.** Warnings read as second-guessing. The one place this matters most is Part V, which carries its own box.
- **Say the provenance out loud when it is weak.** “This is from an abstract, the full paper is paywalled” is information he wants, not a hedge. He caught several bad claims in the previous version of this page precisely because he asked where numbers came from.
**The one conversational debt outstanding.** A question was parked and never answered: **why epinephrine alone landed *below* the no-needle reading in Kopacz’s pigs (−60 ± 7% against an uninjected control) but only *near* the untreated level in Cederholm’s volunteers.** `INFERENCE` The candidate answer is route and species — Kopacz is 1 mL subcutaneous in piglet skin, Cederholm is 0.1 mL intradermal in human forearm, and Kopacz’s saline-plus-epinephrine arm drove flow **below** an untouched site while Cederholm’s epinephrine-alone arm did not quite reach it. It has not been checked properly and should be flagged as open if he raises it.

## 0.1 Provenance tags
Every substantive claim carries one. They are not decoration — several of this document’s predecessors asserted things that turned out to rest on aggregator text or on a figure nobody had read.
<table header-row=\"true\">
<tr>
<td>Tag</td>
<td>Meaning</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>`FULLTEXT`</td>
<td>The full paper has been read. Numbers come from its tables, figures or body text.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>`ABSTRACT-ONLY`</td>
<td>Only the published abstract has been read. Anything not in the abstract is not established.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>`PAYWALLED`</td>
<td>The paper could not be obtained at all. Everything about it comes from its abstract plus whatever citing papers say. Flag this every single time.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>`INFERENCE`</td>
<td>Not stated by any paper. Assembled here by chaining two or more sources, or by unit conversion. The chain is always shown so it can be checked.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>`INFERENCE`, practical subtype</td>
<td>**One honest caveat on the ****`INFERENCE`**** tag.** Its definition below promises a chain from two or more sources. Most `INFERENCE` claims here meet that. **A minority do not: the practical and technique passages** — injection placement and the orange-peel endpoint (11.4), storage chemistry (11.3), and parts of the protective-sensation reasoning (8) — **rest on general clinical knowledge rather than on a chain from this document’s sources.** They are tagged `INFERENCE` because they are not from a named paper, but **an AI reader should treat them as ordinary background rather than as derived from the evidence above, and should not present them as following from any cited study.**</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>`AUDIT-READ`</td>
<td>An independent gap audit read the full paper; **this document has not.** More reliable than a bare abstract, less than `FULLTEXT`. Where a number carries this tag, it comes from the audit’s reading, not from the article’s tables as seen here.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>`UNSOURCED`</td>
<td>In circulation, reproduced here because it is in circulation, traced to nothing.</td>
</tr>
</table>
**One further convention, about negative claims.** This document says “nobody has measured X” in several places — local tissue concentration at a wheal, flux and duration in the same wheal, neuropathic relief from a ropivacaine wheal, flux past 90 minutes. **Each of those means “not found by this project’s searches, and in two cases stated as unmeasured by a paper’s own authors.”** It does not mean the measurement provably does not exist. Where an author says so themselves — Tokinaga on local tissue concentration, Kopacz and Newton on why they did not test pinprick — that is much stronger, and it is flagged at the point of use. **A negative claim here is an invitation to look again, not a closed question.** One such claim in the previous version of this page (“nobody has recorded ropivacaine skin flux past 90 minutes”) turned out to be false, which is why this convention exists.
**And a convention about the Standing clauses in Part XII.** “0 contrasting citation statements” means no *citing paper* has been classified as contradicting the finding. **It does not mean no study reaches the opposite conclusion.** Malet 2015 has zero contrasting statements and four studies disagree with its ranking (6.1). The two are different measurements and conflating them is how the previous version of this page went wrong about neurotoxicity.
## 0.2 Units and conversions used throughout
`INFERENCE` Molar conversions use ropivacaine hydrochloride monohydrate, MW ≈ 328.9 g/mol, which is the salt in the commercial product. This matches Tokinaga’s own published conversion of the clinical range (0.2–0.75% ≈ 0.6–2.4 × 10⁻² M), so the convention is consistent with the literature rather than invented here.
**But two conventions exist in this literature and they differ by about 20%.** Tokinaga 2007 uses the **salt** MW (≈ 328.9), giving 0.75% = 2.28 × 10⁻² M. Sohn 2012 uses the **base** MW (≈ 274.4), giving 0.75% = 2.73 × 10⁻² M and 0.25% = 9.1 × 10⁻³ M. **Neither is wrong; they are measuring different molecules.**
`DERIVED` **And Sohn 2012 is internally inconsistent about it, which matters because several tables in this document use Sohn’s numbers.** Back-computing the molecular weight implied by each of their stated conversions: **ropivacaine 274.7 and lidocaine 234.7 — base weights; mepivacaine 285.7 and levobupivacaine 287.4 — salt weights.** So within one paper two drugs are converted as free base and two as hydrochloride. **Consequence: cross-drug molar comparisons drawn from Sohn carry an error of up to \\~20% in the mepivacaine and levobupivacaine rows.** It does not change any ordering in 4.9a or 9a.5, because the discrepancies are smaller than the tenfold peak separations being compared — but **do not treat two Sohn-derived molarities as comparable to better than about 20%.** The difference changes no conclusion in this document — both put every clinical concentration far above the constricting window — but when comparing a reported molar figure across papers, **check which MW the authors used before treating two numbers as disagreeing.**

Hold onto that table. It is the key to the whole vessel section. Across five preparations (4.5), ropivacaine **contracts** vessels from about **3 × 10⁻⁵ M** upward and **peaks** between **3 × 10⁻⁴ and 1.5 × 10⁻³ M**, losing the contraction above that. **Every concentration in the table above sits above the peak, and all but the weakest sit above the range entirely.**
## 0.3 Terms, defined once
- **Wheal** — a small blister of drug raised inside the skin itself rather than in the fat beneath it.
- **Intradermal** — inside the dermis, the living layer of skin where the nerve endings sit. **Subcutaneous** — beneath the skin, in the fat. These are different tissues with different blood supplies and the distinction changes almost every number in this document.
- **Laser Doppler flowmetry (LDF)** — a weak laser shone into the skin; light bouncing off a moving red cell returns at a shifted frequency, light off a still one does not, so the shift grows with how many cells are moving and how fast. **Laser Doppler imaging (LDI)** is the same principle scanned across an area rather than read at one point.
- **Flux** — the number LDF returns. It has no units of blood per minute. It is proportional to the product of the velocity and the concentration of moving red cells. One flux reading means nothing alone; it means everything against a second reading taken the same way.
- **Small fibre neuropathy** — damage to the thin nerve fibres carrying pain and temperature, which in the length-dependent form begins at the toes and works upward.
- **Protective sensation** — the ability to feel bathwater that is too hot, or pressure building at a contact point before it damages tissue.
- **Pallor / blanching** — visible whitening of the skin. **Not** the same thing as low flux. Section 4.6 is entirely about why.
- **Flare** — the spreading redness around a puncture, from peptide and histamine release by stimulated nociceptors and mast cells. It reaches up to several centimetres.
---
## 1. The governing question
Stated by the human himself, to his mother, in Spanish, and it is what the whole vessel investigation is for:
> **“Quiero saber cuál es la concentración más baja a la que puedo ir sin causar vasoconstricción.”***I want to know the lowest concentration I can go to without causing vasoconstriction.*
His reasoning, reconstructed from his own messages: studies show that below certain concentrations ropivacaine constricts *compared with pure saline*, but pure saline dilates. If 0.25% worked, each vial would last three times as long — 0.25 × 3 = 0.75. But the lower the concentration, the more constriction relative to saline. So: how does it compare with skin that has had nothing injected into it? If 0.25% still dilates relative to untouched skin, it works perfectly and the purchase lasts triple. If 0.25% constricts, it is not as good. He notes he could simply experiment and watch for white skin, but wants studies. He adds that injecting less each time is better for the body in general, **but only if it does not cause constriction.**
**Section 10 answers this question completely.** Read section 4 first if the human wants to understand why, read section 10 first if he wants the answer.
Two premises in his statement of the question are false on the evidence, and the false premises are load-bearing — they are why the question felt unanswerable. Both are dealt with in section 10.
---
# PART I — WHAT ROPIVACAINE DOES TO SKIN BLOOD VESSELS
This is the longest part of the document because it is the part the human cares about and the part every previous version got substantially wrong.
## 4.1 The short version
`INFERENCE` Assembled from sections 4.1a to 4.11, each of which is separately sourced. **Nothing in this summary should be asserted without the section it comes from — the qualifications are where the value is.**
1. **The direction of the effect is not in dispute: ropivacaine narrows skin vessels, and it narrows them more as it gets weaker.** But be precise about the comparison, because this is where the previous version of this page over-reached. **Most of the ladder is measured against saline, not against untouched skin, and “below saline” does not entail “below untouched skin”** — saline itself sits roughly 3.9× above untouched (4.2), so a solution could be well below saline and still above an untouched site. Untreated-referenced evidence exists for exactly three points: **0.25% and 0.75%, from Cederholm 1991’s absolute medians, both of which do go below the untreated line**; and **0.063%, via a two-step chain through adrenaline (4.4).** For 0.5%, 0.375% and 0.125% the untreated comparison is interpolation between those points, not measurement. **State it that way.**
2. But **every wheal is injected at a concentration far above the one that constricts**, and narrows only as the drug is diluted by tissue fluid and cleared. The in-vitro constricting peak sits at 3 × 10⁻⁴ to 1.5 × 10⁻³ M; the weakest solution ever tested in skin, 0.063%, is 1.9 × 10⁻³ M — just above it; 0.75% is 2.28 × 10⁻² M, **fifteen to seventy-six times above the peak.**
3. **But the timing account this page used to attach to that has been retracted — see 4.5 and 10.7.** It previously said the concentration in the syringe sets *when* narrowing starts. `FULLTEXT` Kimi 2012 measured an **eighteen-fold fall in tissue concentration within 30 seconds**, so every clinical concentration is in or near the window almost immediately and concentration barely changes the timing at all. **What survives: 0.25% and 0.75% reach the same trough — about a quarter below untouched skin — in the only human intradermal dataset with absolute values, and what concentration plausibly changes is how long the site stays inside the window and how much total drug is there, not when it arrives.**
4. **Whether ropivacaine is a distinctively strong vasoconstrictor is now contested, and the two studies conflict.** In **human** internal mammary and radial arteries it is statistically **indistinguishable from lidocaine**, with both peaking around the same concentration (Gherardini 1995). In **rat** aorta it is significantly **more potent than lidocaine**, P \\< 0.001, with its peak a full order of magnitude lower (Sohn 2012). **Report both; do not resolve it** (4.9, 4.9a). What is not in doubt: its reputation was built on comparisons against **bupivacaine**, which dilates in skin, and it is genuinely better than bupivacaine.
5. **Nothing in the skin-wheal literature shows ropivacaine producing more flow than saline at any concentration.** The top of the range, 1%, *matches* saline, which is a null result on top of the puncture, not a measurement of widening.
6. **Skin colour is a bad instrument for this.** It reports a different vascular bed from flux and has failed in both directions in three separate studies.
## 4.1a What the instruments can and cannot see
This section answers his questions 2, 3 and 4. It belongs before the data because it sets the limits on all of it.
### 4.1a.1 What a perfusion measurement is
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` `INFERENCE` A laser is shone into the skin. Light that bounces off a **moving** red cell comes back at a shifted frequency; light off static tissue comes back unshifted. The instrument mixes the two and reads the size of the shift. **Newton 2004 gives the definition in one line: laser Doppler flux = velocity × concentration of moving red blood cells.** Kopacz 1989 puts it the same way: the magnitude of the Doppler shift is directly proportional to the velocity and mass of erythrocytes in the capillary bed.
**So “perfusion” in this literature is not a volume of blood per unit time.** It is a relative index proportional to the product of how many red cells are moving and how fast. `ABSTRACT-ONLY` Bernardi & Berardesca state the limit plainly: unlike ultrasound Doppler, where velocity in a single vessel can be established from known geometry, **in laser Doppler methodology red cell velocity and volume cannot be extracted separately.** Fredriksson 2010 adds that conventional laser Doppler gives “only a relative perfusion estimate for an **unknown measurement volume**, with no information about the blood flow speed distribution.”
Three consequences that govern every number in Part I:
1. **The units are arbitrary.** Cederholm 1991 reports **Volts** on a 0–10 V scale; Newton reports **perfusion units (PU)**; Kopacz reports **MV**. None of these convert to each other or to mL/min. **Every comparison must be within one study.**
2. **A single reading is meaningless.** Only ratios against a control site measured in the same session mean anything. This is why section 4.2 exists.
3. **A drug could halve the number of moving cells while doubling their speed and the instrument would read no change.** Nothing in this literature rules that out.
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` What validates it as a flow proxy: Kopacz 1989 states it correlates linearly with blood flow measured by plethysmography, radiolabelled microsphere uptake, ¹³³Xe washout and electromagnetic flow probe, with **R = 0.8–0.98**; and separately that laser Doppler capillary flow correlates with **blood loss from skin incisions in pigs.** Winsor 1987 found laser Doppler frequencies correlated well with venous occlusion plethysmography in a linear relationship. **So it is a good proxy for flow — just not a measurement of it.**
### 4.1a.2 How deep it reads — and the answer is shallower than the stated penetration
Two different depths get quoted and they mean different things. **Getting this distinction right is the answer to his question.**
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Nominal penetration: 1 to 1.5 mm.** Tur 1991 (*Int J Dermatol*) states the radiation penetrates to a depth of 1–1.5 mm in skin. Szulkowska 1996 gives 1 mm. Tenland’s 1982 Linköping dissertation — **which is the very reference Cederholm 1991 cites for its instrument** — describes the flowmeter as designed to measure red cell flux “in the outermost layer (1 mm) of tissue.” Kvernebo 1991 describes the measuring volume as “some few mm³.”
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Median sampling depth: 68 to 233 µm.** This is the number that actually matters, and it comes from **Jakobsson & Nilsson 1991**, a Monte Carlo photon-path model, DOI `10.1109/IEMBS.1991.684738`. For 120 µm diameter fibres in skin tissue:
<table header-row=\"true\">
<tr>
<td>Probe geometry</td>
<td>Median sampling depth</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Single fibre, light both to and from the tissue</td>
<td>**68 µm**</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Separate fibres, 125 µm centre separation</td>
<td>**117 µm**</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Separate fibres, 700 µm centre separation</td>
<td>**233 µm**</td>
</tr>
</table>
In liver the sampling depth is about 70% of the skin value and in brain grey matter about 300%, for the same probe. `ABSTRACT-ONLY` Jentink 1988 independently modelled it and found the depth and width of the probe volume are **proportional to the distance between the laser and the detector**, with tissue absorption decreasing the depth — and noted that varying the separation lets you “distinguish capillary blood flow from flow in the plexus beneath.”
`INFERENCE` **The distinction: light penetrates to about 1–1.5 mm, but half the returned Doppler signal originates in the top 68–233 µm depending on the probe.** The measurement is heavily weighted to the most superficial layers, and formally the measurement volume is unknown.
### 4.1a.3 And that is exactly why colour and flux come apart
`INFERENCE` **This is the piece that makes section 4.6 mechanistically coherent, and it is worth walking the human through slowly because it is the most satisfying thing in the document.**
Skin vasculature has a layered arrangement. The papillary **capillary loops** run vertically in the upper dermis. The **superficial venous plexus** sits below them, horizontally. A **deep plexus** sits at the dermal–subcutaneous junction.
Fruhstorfer & Wagener’s explanation (4.6) is that the flowmeter reads **volume per minute through the vertically arranged capillary loops**, while skin colour is set by **the volume of blood standing in the deeper horizontal venous plexus networks.**
Put that beside Jakobsson’s numbers. **A median sampling depth of 68–233 µm sits in the capillary loop zone and above much of the venous plexus.** So the instrument is weighted toward precisely the compartment Fruhstorfer says it reads, and the compartment that determines colour sits largely below where most of the signal comes from. **The two methods are not measuring the same tissue, and the geometry says so independently of the pharmacology.**
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` A further refinement in the same direction: **Meinke 2007** used a frequency-weighted flowmeter and found that nutritive capillary flow is very slow and needs distinguishing from flow in deeper vessels — with the Doppler signal in the **50 to 400 Hz band correlating best with capillary blood flux** specifically. Conventional flowmetry, which reports a single mean flux, sums across all velocities and therefore mixes capillary and deeper-vessel flow into one number. **None of the studies in this document used frequency weighting.**
### 4.1a.3b The reproducibility problem, from the instrument’s own originator
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Tenland 1982** — Cederholm’s own instrument reference — performed an extensive study of spatial and temporal variation in human skin blood flow and concluded that **skin blood flow shows wide day-to-day variations and significant differences between adjacent probe sites**, such that earlier reported “normal” values of skin blood flow were “seriously questioned.” He also observed rhythmical variations at **5–12 per minute** and attributed them to a local regulatory mechanism operating even at steady state.
`INFERENCE` Three things follow, and they are caveats on everything in Part I:
1. **Cederholm’s flat 0.4 V untreated control line is a median across 12 subjects, not a stable personal constant.** Adjacent sites on one forearm differ significantly.
2. **The flowmotion waves Cederholm found absent after ropivacaine** (4.3) are Tenland’s 5–12 per minute rhythm. Their disappearance is a real signal, and it is arguably **more robust than the flux level itself**, because it is a change in pattern rather than in an arbitrary absolute number.
3. `ABSTRACT-ONLY` Newton 2004 quotes laser Doppler imaging reproducibility as **15–20%**, and Kopacz’s coefficient of variation at an uninjected control site over 30 minutes was **11.8%, range 0–30%.** **Differences smaller than roughly 20% between sites mean nothing in this literature.** That is a useful floor to keep in mind: the ropivacaine trough of 0.3 V against a 0.4 V control is a 25% difference — real, but not by a wide margin.
### 4.1a.4 Why the flare happens at all
`FULLTEXT` **Fruhstorfer & Wagener 1993 give the mechanism in one sentence.** Even a minute injury — the insertion of a fine needle, or the injection of a small quantity of saline — produces a rapid and marked increase in blood flow **within a radius of up to several centimetres.** The cause: **release of vasoactive substances, peptides and histamine, from stimulated nociceptors and mast cells.** The phenomenon is called **neurogenic inflammation.**
The word “neurogenic” is the point. It is not a mechanical response to tissue being pushed apart, and it is not a response to the fluid. **It is a nerve-mediated reflex: the needle stimulates nociceptors, the nociceptors release peptides from their own terminals, and those peptides dilate the local vessels and degranulate mast cells, which release histamine, which dilates more.** That is why it spreads centimetres from a puncture that is a fraction of a millimetre across, and why the volume injected makes no difference (4.2) — the trigger is the nerve ending being stimulated, not the tissue being distended.
`FULLTEXT` Three pieces of evidence in this document fall straight out of that mechanism:
- **Why saline and a bare needle are indistinguishable** (4.2). Both stimulate nociceptors identically. The fluid is not the stimulus.
- **Why volume does not matter across a tenfold range** (Cederholm 1991 Group I). Same reason.
- **Why needle gauge does matter slightly** (Cederholm 1991 Group II: 20 G and 23 G gave larger rises than 30 G). A bigger hole stimulates more nociceptors.
`FULLTEXT` Fruhstorfer’s own experimental use of this is elegant and worth explaining to the human if he is interested: because a stable flare is hard to get from a puncture alone, they **pricked 1% histamine into the skin first** to raise a large, stable, reproducible zone of enhanced flow lasting up to an hour, and then injected the test drug into it — because **“the injection of a vasoconstrictive agent produces both dilatation and constriction in unpredictable proportions”** and you cannot see the constriction against a low baseline.
`FULLTEXT` And the same paper connects this back to the human’s situation directly: **“even a minute injury… produces a rapid and marked increase in blood flow”**, and therefore **“the intracutaneous injection of ropivacaine in a concentration thought to cause vasoconstriction enhances cutaneous perfusion, although the flux increase is less pronounced than that caused by saline.”** That sentence, written in 1993 citing Cederholm 1991, is the whole of section 4.2 and 4.3 in one line.
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` The third of Fruhstorfer’s three mechanisms (4.7) is also part of the flare story: the local anaesthetic **blocks sympathetic axons and removes thermoregulatory vasoconstrictor tone.** So an injection of a local anaesthetic produces at least two separate dilating effects — the neurogenic flare from the needle, and the release of resting constrictor tone from the drug — before the drug’s intrinsic vascular action is reached at all.
## 4.2 Which comparison — the three baselines
A flux reading is meaningless alone. Three comparison points are available at an injected site and they sit at very different heights.
1. **Untreated skin.** Nothing done to it.
2. **A bare needle stick.** The hole, nothing injected.
3. **Saline.** The hole plus a volume of fluid, no drug.
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Guinard, Carpenter & Morell 1992** measured the second and third against pre-injection baseline. **This paper is ****`PAYWALLED`**** and confirmed unobtainable; everything below is from the abstract.** Eight unmedicated male volunteers, randomised, controlled, double-blind; fourteen subcutaneous injections each at separate abdominal sites, 0.2 mL per site; one further site given a needle stick with nothing injected; laser Doppler capillary perfusion monitor before and for 60 minutes after. **Saline raised flow by 285 ± 237%. The bare needle stick raised it by 260 ± 121%.** Mean ± SE.
`INFERENCE` **“By”, not “to” — and this took three lines of evidence to settle, because the paper itself is ****`PAYWALLED`****.** (a) The abstract’s own phrasing is “the maximum *increase* in cutaneous blood flow was 277% to 511%” and “*increased* cutaneous blood flow 285%”. (b) Randall Carpenter is a co-author of both this paper and Kopacz 1989; Kopacz states its normalisation formula explicitly as percent change from control and reports “increased flow **by** 90% ± 32%”. Same laboratory, same convention, three years earlier. (c) The magnitude cross-checks against Cederholm 1991’s human intradermal medians, where saline went 0.4 → 1.8 V, a rise of about 350% — close to a rise *by* 285% (to 3.85× baseline) and nowhere near a rise *to* 285% (a rise of only 185%). **So: saline raises skin flow to roughly 3.9× baseline; the needle alone to roughly 3.6×.**
`FULLTEXT` **The hole and the hole-plus-fluid are the same thing to within measurement error.** Cederholm 1991 tested NaCl against needle-alone directly and found **no significant difference**, at 20 minutes and on area under the curve. Guinard’s 285% and 260% are twenty-five points apart with spreads of ±237 and ±121. And the sign of the tiny residual **flips between the two studies** — saline marginally higher in Guinard, needle marginally higher in Cederholm. Two labs, two tissue routes, two years, opposite signs, neither significant.
`FULLTEXT` **Volume does not drive it either.** Cederholm 1991 Group I injected saline at 0.05, 0.1, 0.2, 0.3 and 0.5 mL. All raised flow, **with no differences between the volumes** across a tenfold range.
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Newton and colleagues showed separately, by non-invasive iontophoretic delivery, that 0.5% sodium chloride has no vasodilatory effect in human skin at all.** Third independent confirmation from a different method.
`FULLTEXT` **And a fourth observation, which is the starkest statement of the colour/flux problem in the whole collection.** Willatts & Reynolds 1985 report that plain 0.9% saline injected intradermally produces visible vasodilatation in only **0 to 10% of blebs** — against Cederholm 1991’s measurement of saline flux at **4.5 times the untreated level.** `INFERENCE` **A 4.5-fold rise in measured flux that is invisible to the eye more than nine times out of ten.** Keep this to hand for 4.6; it does more work than any of the three demonstrations there.
`FULLTEXT` The same paper also establishes that **sodium metabisulphite is not an inert excipient**: their saline-plus-0.1%-metabisulphite control dilated **65%** of blebs against that 0–10% baseline, and “appeared to increase the pain of the injection.” `INFERENCE` Relevant twice over — it compromises their own amide-versus-saline comparison (9a.2), and it quantifies one of ropivacaine’s real practical advantages, since ropivacaine contains no metabisulphite (11.3).
**Consequence for the human’s plan, and it matters:** diluting ropivacaine with saline adds **no** dilation to offset the drug’s narrowing. The diluent is inert on flux. There is no “add saline to get some vasodilation back” move available.
`FULLTEXT` Needle gauge does make a small difference. Cederholm 1991 Group II compared 20 G, 23 G and 30 G insertions: all raised flow, the rise was somewhat **larger with the bigger needles**, and 30 G gave the smallest rise. They recommend 30 G, partly for that and partly because it is technically easier to place 0.1 mL precisely intradermally. **They also recommend 0.1 mL as the test volume**, on the grounds that volume made no difference to the flow rise and a low volume is otherwise desirable.
### The shape of the needle-trauma curve, and why every reading in this literature starts at 20 minutes
`FULLTEXT` Cederholm 1991 traced it: **the increase in blood flow after an intradermal needle insertion started within 30 seconds, peaked at about 5 to 7 minutes, then declined slowly — and an increase was still present as late as 90 minutes.**
`FULLTEXT` **That is why the intermittent recordings begin at 20 minutes.** The authors say so: “Taking these findings into consideration, the intermittent recordings were begun 20 min after needle insertion or intradermal injection.” They deliberately waited past the trauma peak before starting to measure.
`INFERENCE` Three consequences worth stating:
1. **Every number in this literature is measured on the downslope of the puncture’s own response.** There is no clean drug-only reading at any timepoint, and the trauma term is still non-zero at 90 minutes — the end of the recording window.
2. **The first 20 minutes of a wheal have never been characterised for ropivacaine at all**, because the design excludes them on purpose.
3. **Nothing in the literature is comparable to a reading taken at 5 minutes**, if he were ever tempted to look that early and compare.
`FULLTEXT` One further reassurance from the same paper about site independence: **“Neither needle insertion nor needle insertion plus the injection of 0.1 ml of fluid influenced the blood flow at remote or nearby test sites,”** and there was no difference in reactions between the various test sites. So a multi-site pattern is legitimate, and sites do not contaminate one another at the spacings used (11.4).
## 4.3 The human intradermal dataset — absolute values against an untreated control
`FULLTEXT` **Cederholm, Evers & Löfström 1991.** This is the most important table in the document. It is the only place where ropivacaine and an untreated control site appear on the same absolute scale in human skin.
**Method:** 17 healthy young male volunteers across three groups. Four test sites on each forearm, volar surface, randomised and double-blind. Periflux (Perimed) laser Doppler, output **in Volts**, not mL/min/100 g; full scale 0–10 V; bandwidth 4 kHz, damping time constant 0.2 s. Room thermostatically controlled at 21–22 °C, subjects supine under a blanket. Group III, n = 12, mean age 31.7: 0.1 mL through a 30-G needle. Recordings at 20, 40, 60 and 90 minutes. Solution pH: saline 6.0, lidocaine 6.7, all bupivacaine and ropivacaine solutions 5.5. All injections made by one person; the volume (Group I) and needle size (Group II) were unknown to the person taking the recordings.

**So intradermal ropivacaine at clinical strength does go under untouched skin in human forearm.** 0.25% is below the untreated median from 40 minutes onward, at three-quarters of it. 0.75% peaks at 2.5× untreated at 20 minutes, is level at 60 minutes, and is below at 90. **Both land on the same 0.3 V trough.**
### Two honest limits on that table, and they must be stated whenever it is used
1. These are **medians transcribed from figure captions**, not a numerical table. The figures show individual subject traces with wide spread; a median is all the captions give.
2. **The paper never statistically tests ropivacaine against the untreated control.** Tables 2, 3 and 4 test Needle vs Control and NaCl vs Control, and everything else against NaCl. The ropivacaine-versus-untreated comparison exists as curves on the same axis, not as a P value. It is a real comparison but an untested one.

Table 3 (treatment/NaCl ratio at 20 min) and Table 4 (area under the curve versus NaCl AUC) reproduce the same pattern with slightly different P values. Notably Bupivacaine 0.75% vs NaCl becomes significant on AUC (P = 0.004) though not at 20 minutes.
**The last row is the dose-response confirmed inside a single study: the weaker solution narrows more.** And 0.25% versus saline is significant at 20 minutes while 0.75% is significant only at P = 0.009, consistent with 0.75% still being high on its own curve at that moment.
### Why the study is designed around saline rather than untreated skin
`FULLTEXT` Cederholm 1991 states the reason plainly. Resting flow at the volar forearm at 22 °C **is low, because skin vasoconstriction is required to conserve body heat.** Increases are therefore easy to provoke and a vasoconstrictor effect is “more difficult to demonstrate.” The whole method was built to provoke a rise and then look for drugs that blunt it. That is the structural reason the untreated comparison is hard, and the reason the entire literature is saline-referenced.
`FULLTEXT` **And the untreated control was stable in every series, not just Group III.** The paper’s own words: original skin blood flow was low in all volunteers, similar at all sites tested and similar in both arms before any treatment; the original and control recordings at the untreated areas were low and **remained unchanged throughout the various test periods** — “This observation was constant in all series.” `INFERENCE` That is the evidence entitling the flat 0.4 V line to be used as a reference at all, and it should be cited alongside the Tenland caveat in 4.1a.3b rather than instead of it: **the control is stable within a session; it is not a fixed personal constant across sessions or across adjacent sites.**
`FULLTEXT` One further observation from the same paper, easy to miss: **flowmotion waves** — the rhythmic variation normally seen in skin flux traces — were largely **absent** after ropivacaine 0.25% and 0.75% and after bupivacaine 0.25%. The authors read this as vasoconstriction in the skin arterioles. It is a second, independent signal in the same recordings pointing the same way.
### 4.3a The one study that tests ropivacaine against untreated skin statistically
`FULLTEXT` **Kopacz, Carpenter & Mackey 1989**, *Anesthesiology* 71(1):69–74. **This closes the exact gap Cederholm 1991 leaves open** (see the limits above): Cederholm never statistically tests ropivacaine against its untreated control, and Kopacz does. It is pig skin and subcutaneous rather than intradermal, which is why it cannot simply replace Cederholm — but on the specific question the human is asking, it is the only significance test that exists.
**Method.** Six piglets, mean 13.3 kg. **Ten** test solutions injected subcutaneously in each animal — normal saline; ropivacaine 0.25% and 0.75%; bupivacaine 0.25% and 0.75%; each of those four with and without epinephrine 5 µg/mL — **plus an eleventh site that was not injected and served as the control.** Drugs randomised to sites and **observers blinded** to which solution went where. **1 mL through a 30-G needle held at 45° to the skin**, needle point directly below the centre of each site. Flow measured at all ten injected sites at 5, 10, 15 and 30 minutes, with the uninjected control read immediately before and after each round and the two averaged.
`FULLTEXT` Three method notes that matter for reading it:
- **Why subcutaneous:** “Subcutaneous injections are easy to standardize in the pig because **intradermal injection is almost impossible to perform due to nondistensibility of the skin.**”
- **The depot is large.** “Injection of 1 mL of solution routinely raises a wheal approximately **2 cm in diameter**” — ten to twenty times the volume of a 50–100 µL wheal.
- **Why no duration measurement:** “No attempt was made to measure the duration of anesthesia because **pin prick or other painful stimulation of the skin alters cutaneous blood flow.**” This is one of the two primary sources for gap 4 in section 13.
**Normalisation.** Percent change in blood flow = \\[(Sx − S₀)/S₀ − (Cx − C₀)/C₀\\] × 100, where S is the injected site and C the uninjected control. So every figure is already control-referenced. **Note this is the convention that settles the “to versus by” question about Guinard** (4.2) — same senior author, three years earlier.
**Statistical threshold, stated by the authors themselves:** “Although we report all P values less than 0.05, **the Bonferroni correction for multiple comparisons indicates that a P value less than 0.003 is required for statistical significance.**” Keep that in hand; several of the P values below do not clear it.
### Table 1 — absolute flow (MV), mean ± SE, n = 6
<table header-row=\"true\">
<tr>
<td>Drug</td>
<td>%</td>
<td>Baseline</td>

**The uninjected control line is worth pausing on: 92 → 86 → 95 → 90 → 88 over thirty minutes.** It barely moves, and the authors confirm it: blood flow at the uninjected control site did not change significantly during the experiments, with a mean coefficient of variation for repeated measurements of **11.8%, range 0–30%.** And baseline flows did not differ across the eleven sites (**P \\> 0.4**). That is what a clean control looks like, and it is why the comparisons below carry weight.

Note also the control row: an uninjected site drifted **+5 ± 11%** over the same period. Against that, −52% and −54% are large.

`INFERENCE` **The two analyses do not say the same thing, and the honest reading has to hold both.** On maximum change, both ropivacaine concentrations sit significantly below the uninjected control at P = 0.0009. On the 30-minute trajectory, only 0.75% reaches significance versus control (P = 0.03, which does **not** clear the authors’ own 0.003 threshold), and **0.25% comes out at P = 0.6 — nowhere near significant.**
The reconciliation is that a site can have a large, reliable single-timepoint trough and still have a noisy overall trajectory, especially when the time to maximum effect varies — which the authors report it does: the maximum change usually occurred at 5–10 minutes but “there was some variability in the time to maximum effect between piglets.”
**Do not let this pass as a technicality. It is a genuine qualification on the central claim**, and it should be said to the human whenever Kopacz is used to support 0.25% narrowing: *the deepest point is significantly below untouched skin; the average over half an hour is not.*
### What else the paper establishes
`FULLTEXT`
- **0.25% and 0.75% behave the same.** “Subcutaneous injection of ropivacaine reduced cutaneous blood flow **to a similar degree at both concentrations (0.25% vs. 0.75% P = 0.4)**”, and Figure 1’s caption states there were no significant differences between the two ropivacaine concentrations, or between the two bupivacaine concentrations. **This is the finding section 10.2 rests on.**
- **The two drugs run opposite.** Combining concentrations, ropivacaine decreased and bupivacaine increased absolute capillary flow (**P = 0.03**); normalised, the magnitude and direction of change produced by ropivacaine differed significantly from bupivacaine (**P = 0.01**).
- **Bupivacaine dilates.** +90% and +82% at maximum, against an uninjected control drifting +5%.
- **Epinephrine 5 µg/mL was chosen because that concentration “was found to be optimal for reducing blood flow in a similar model”** — worth knowing before reading 4.8.
- **The authors’ own conclusion overreaches slightly**, and it is instructive: they propose ropivacaine would be “an ideal agent for infiltration anesthesia prior to operations on the skin,” simultaneously providing anaesthesia and reducing bleeding. `ABSTRACT-ONLY` Liddle 1998 tested that clinically and found **696 mL of blood loss against 300 mL** for bupivacaine-with-epinephrine, with the trial halted early (4.9).
- **And they anticipate the problem in 5.8.3 themselves:** “Theoretically, a local anesthetic that produces vasoconstriction would be expected to have a prolonged duration of action.” Theoretically. Nobody has shown it in a wheal.
## 4.4 The full concentration ladder
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Cederholm 1995**, the author’s doctoral thesis summary, *Acta Anaesthesiol Scand* 1995;39:711. Linköping University Medical Dissertations No. 407, 1994. **ISBN 91-7870-949-0.** This pools the 1991, 1992 and 1994 studies and gives the complete ordering that no single paper provides.
Cohort arithmetic checks out: 17 (1991) + 23 (1992) + 10 (1994) = 50 volunteers, which is exactly the thesis total, alongside 72 transurethral surgery patients and 29 blood donors. So these are three separate non-overlapping volunteer cohorts.
**The ladder, highest flow to lowest, paraphrased from the printed abstract:**
1. Lidocaine 1% — increase in flow
2. Bupivacaine 0.75% — increase in flow
3. **Ropivacaine 1% — a flow similar to saline**
4. **Ropivacaine 0.75% — a decrease**
5. **Ropivacaine 0.5%, 0.375%, 0.25%, 0.125%, 0.063% — a gradual further decrease**
6. **Ropivacaine 0.063% — a flow similar to an adrenaline injection at 5 µg/mL**
And: ropivacaine 1%, 0.5% or 0.25% combined with adrenaline **did not accentuate but instead decreased** adrenaline’s vasoconstrictive effect.
The thesis also gives the method rationale in the author’s own words: the intradermal injection of a local anaesthetic may produce not only a further increase but also a decrease in skin blood flow, **if the effect of an intradermal saline injection — which causes a very reproducible flow increase — is considered in the evaluation** of the net circulatory effect of the tested drug. That sentence is the justification for the whole saline-referenced design.
### A correction that must not be lost
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **An earlier working note in this project recorded the thesis as saying 0.063% produced “a flow similar to adrenaline-injection and almost as low as at the untreated control sites.”** The printed abstract contains **no such clause.** It stops at “similar to adrenaline-injection (5 µg/mL).” The “almost as low as untreated” phrase came from aggregator text, which appears to have spliced in the 1992 abstract’s separate sentence about *epinephrine alone* being almost as low as the untreated controls.
`INFERENCE` The conclusion survives **only** as a two-step chain: 0.063% ≈ adrenaline 5 µg/mL (thesis, abstract), and adrenaline 5 µg/mL ≈ almost as low as untreated (Cederholm 1992, abstract). **The ropivacaine-versus-untreated comparison is not stated in the thesis either.**
`INFERENCE` **An independent audit confirmed the splice and located its origin.** The **Linköping DiVA repository record** for the dissertation carries abstract-like text that attaches “almost as low as at untreated control sites” to **0.063% ropivacaine**, whereas the primary article abstract attaches it to **epinephrine alone**. Because the dissertation full text does not exist in the repository — DiVA states so — **the repository wording cannot establish what the thesis said, and should be treated as a splice or metadata error rather than a source.**
**The audit’s instruction, which this page adopts: quote the primary article abstract, and do not assert the 0.063%-versus-untreated comparison at all unless Cederholm 1992 is obtained.** It is retained here only as a labelled chain so that an AI reader recognises the claim when the human repeats it — **not as evidence.** Three of this document’s claims are waiting on that paper (10.7).
The same aggregator text also embellished the leukocyte result: the printed abstract says ropivacaine 1000 µg/mL depressed responses “similar to lidocaine (1000 µg/mL)”, not “similar to, and even somewhat more pronounced than.”
### The striking fact in that ladder
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` `INFERENCE` Ropivacaine at 0.063% produced a flow **similar to an adrenaline injection at 5 µg/mL** — that is 1:200,000, the strength Cederholm used throughout. On flux, sufficiently diluted ropivacaine is about as vasoconstricting as adrenaline. The vessel argument for going *up* in concentration rather than down now has a number attached to the far end of the ladder.
### Tension between two of Cederholm’s own accounts
`INFERENCE` The 1991 figure captions put ropivacaine 0.75% at 0.4 V at 60 minutes and 0.3 V at 90 against an untreated line flat at 0.4 V — level with, then under it. The thesis says 0.75% merely “produced a decrease” and reserves proximity to adrenaline for 0.063%. Both cannot be exactly right as written. The likeliest reconciliation: the thesis is describing overall or peak level on a saline-referenced scale, while the 1991 medians are absolute values and the crossing happens only in the 60-to-90-minute tail. **This cannot be verified from what is held and should be recorded as an unresolved discrepancy between two accounts by the same author of the same data.**
## 4.5 The unifying mechanism — the constricting window, and what concentration does and does not set
This is the section that makes the rest cohere. It is the most valuable synthesis in the document and it is `INFERENCE`, assembled from five independently sourced studies that were never brought together before.
### The window, measured in five preparations

**And that last line explains Cederholm’s dose-response entirely.** The concentration that narrowed most in human skin, 0.063%, is the one that lands closest to the in-vitro peak. The dose-response in skin is not a separate phenomenon needing its own explanation. It is proximity to the window.
### Tokinaga says this himself
`FULLTEXT` Tokinaga 2007 converts the clinical range and states the problem outright: 0.2–0.75% ropivacaine ≈ (0.6–2.4) × 10⁻² M, and **“such clinically available concentrations of ropivacaine appear to be too high to contract vascular smooth muscle”** on their results. Their first proposed reconciliation is the one that matters: **injected ropivacaine is diluted by interstitial fluid down into the concentration that exerts vasoconstriction.** They estimate local tissue concentration ranges from 3 × 10⁻⁶ M (the plasma level during human epidural anaesthesia) up to 2.4 × 10⁻² M. Their other two explanations: rat aorta is a conductance vessel, not a resistance vessel or capillary; and species sensitivity may differ.
### Nakamura says it too, and demonstrates it
`FULLTEXT` Nakamura 1993 reconciles two studies from a single institute using exactly this logic. Kopacz found 1 mL of subcutaneous ropivacaine 0.25–0.75% **decreased** cutaneous flow in piglets. Guinard, Carpenter, Owens & Nadir 1991 found 10 mL of 0.25% **increased** it. Nakamura’s explanation: a large volume at 0.25% barely changes vascular tone, whereas a smaller volume of the same solution is **diluted by subcutaneous tissue fluid to a concentration capable of constricting vessels significantly.** Their stated conclusion is that the effect of subcutaneous ropivacaine on cutaneous flow **varies with the concentration, the volume, and local conditions including injection technique and venous tone before injection.**
> **⚠️ CORRECTION — demote this from explanation to contested hypothesis.** `ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Guinard’s own paper considered the volume explanation and rejected it**, on the grounds that **saline alone did not show the same effect.** So the reconciliation this page has been presenting is Nakamura’s proposal about someone else’s data, and the primary authors disagreed with it. **Present it as Nakamura’s hypothesis, contested by the original authors, not as the settled account.**
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **And the underlying conflict is worse than a volume disagreement.** Guinard, Carpenter, Owens & Nadir 1991 (*Reg Anaesth* 16:268–271): 7 piglets 12.2–20.4 kg, **10 mL per site** subcutaneously, 0.25% ropivacaine or 0.25% bupivacaine each with or without epinephrine 5 µg/mL, plus saline and an untreated site; laser Doppler flow at baseline and 10 minutes; then a **5 cm incision with measured blood loss over 10 minutes.** Their main result: **no difference between ropivacaine and bupivacaine**, with epinephrine reducing flow (P \\< 0.05).
`INFERENCE` **That is a direct contradiction of Kopacz, not a volume artefact.** Kopacz separated the two drugs decisively in the same species by the same route — ropivacaine −54%, bupivacaine +82%, P = 0.0009 against control and P = 0.01 on direction (4.3a). Guinard found them indistinguishable. **Same species, same route, overlapping concentration, opposite conclusions about whether ropivacaine and bupivacaine differ at all.** The differences available: volume (1 mL vs 10 mL), recording schedule (four timepoints to 30 min vs baseline and 10 min only), and the number of concentrations. **Record it as an unresolved conflict between the two pig studies.** The paper is `NOT OBTAINED` — abstract only — so this rests on the abstract’s summary.
### Newton reaches the same place from the human skin side
`FULLTEXT` Newton 2000 proposes that the late constriction they measured after 40 minutes occurs **“when the quantity of drug remaining in the tissue is less than 0.125 mg”** — that being 0.125% in 0.1 mL, the dose that in their hands produced no vasoactive effect beyond needle trauma. A depot-decay account, arrived at independently.
### An independent replication of the peak, and the first actual calculation of tissue concentration
`FULLTEXT` **Sohn, Ok, Kim et al. 2012**, “Vasoconstriction potency induced by aminoamide local anesthetics correlates with lipid solubility,” *Anesthesiology Research and Practice*, DOI `10.1155/2012/170958`. **This paper was not in the previous version of this page and it does three things at once.**
**Method.** Male Sprague Dawley rats 250–300 g, descending thoracic aorta, 2.5 mm rings, 3.0 g resting tension, 37 °C, 95% O₂ / 5% CO₂. Endothelium-denuded rings (rubbed with a 25-G needle tip, confirmed by complete absence of acetylcholine relaxation) pretreated with **L-NAME 10⁻⁴ M**; endothelium-intact rings confirmed by \\>75% acetylcholine relaxation. Isotonic **60 mM KCl** as the 100% reference. Six rats per drug. Four drugs: levobupivacaine, ropivacaine, lidocaine, mepivacaine.
**(a) It replicates ropivacaine’s constricting peak at exactly Tokinaga’s value.** The concentration producing maximal contraction in endothelium-intact aorta:

<tr>
<td>Drug</td>
<td>Peak contraction at</td>
<td>Octanol/buffer coefficient</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Levobupivacaine</td>
<td>10⁻⁴ M</td>
<td>2.539</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>**Ropivacaine**</td>
<td>**3 × 10⁻⁴ M**</td>
<td>2.060</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Lidocaine</td>
<td>**3 × 10⁻³ M**</td>
<td>1.633</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Mepivacaine</td>
<td>3 × 10⁻³ M</td>
<td>1.322</td>
</tr>
</table>
`INFERENCE` **Ropivacaine’s 3 × 10⁻⁴ M is Tokinaga 2007’s figure to the digit, from a different laboratory five years later in the same preparation.** Two independent confirmations of the same peak. Section 4.5’s window is now better supported than it was.
**And all four drugs were biphasic** — “levobupivacaine, ropivacaine, lidocaine, and mepivacaine produced vasoconstriction at low doses and vasodilation at high doses” — in **both** denuded and intact aorta. The biphasic pattern is a class property, not a ropivacaine peculiarity.
**(b) It calculates the expected local tissue concentration, which nobody else has done.** Their arithmetic, stated in the discussion: ropivacaine is used clinically at 0.25–0.75%, and **“the expected local tissue concentration encountered in the clinical setting with 94% protein binding ranges from 5.46 × 10⁻⁴ M to 1.63 × 10⁻³ M.”**
`INFERENCE` **That range sits squarely inside the constricting window** (3 × 10⁻⁴ to 1.5 × 10⁻³ M). **It is the single strongest piece of support section 4.5 has**, and it comes from a paper that reached the conclusion independently.
**Be precise about what the calculation is, though.** It is the **unbound fraction of the injected concentration** — 6% of 9.1 × 10⁻³ M is 5.46 × 10⁻⁴, and 6% of 2.73 × 10⁻² M is 1.63 × 10⁻³. It accounts for protein binding and nothing else: not diffusion, not dilution by interstitial fluid, not clearance over time. **So it is a calculated upper bound on the free concentration at the moment of injection, not a measurement of tissue concentration over time.** Gap 3 in section 13 stands; it is now a narrower gap.
**(c) It states the dilution mechanism independently — a third source for it.** Their words: **“we hypothesize that the actual local tissue concentration of local anesthetics may be lower than expected, because if locally applied, the anesthetic is diluted with interstitial fluid and absorbed into fat tissue around nerve block site. Therefore, we suggest these supposed local tissue concentrations include the range of concentrations of local anesthetics producing vasoconstriction observed in this in vitro study.”**
`INFERENCE` Tokinaga 2007, Nakamura 1993 and Sohn 2012 each arrived at the interstitial-dilution account separately. **Three laboratories, three preparations, one mechanism. That is as close to converging confirmation as this question gets without somebody actually measuring it.**
### IT HAS BEEN MEASURED — and the measurement both confirms the mechanism and breaks the timing
> **⚠️ CORRECTION. Every previous version of this page, following Tokinaga 2007, stated that local tissue concentration after infiltration has never been measured. That is FALSE.** Two open-access rat studies measured it directly with radiolabelled ropivacaine. They confirm the direction of the mechanism below and refute the timescale this page previously attached to it. **Do not repeat the “never measured” claim.**
`FULLTEXT` **Kimi H, Yamashiro M, Hashimoto S 2012**, “The local pharmacokinetics of ³H-ropivacaine and ¹⁴C-lidocaine after maxillary infiltration anesthesia in rats,” *Anesthesia Progress* 59:75–81, DOI `10.2344/11-14.1`.
**Method.** Male Wistar rats, 7 weeks, 250 ± 80 g. **20 µL of 0.5% ropivacaine hydrochloride (³H-labelled) or 2% lidocaine hydrochloride (¹⁴C-labelled), injected through a 31-G needle over 30 seconds** into the palatal mucosa beside the right maxillary molar. Killed at 0.5, 2, 5, 10, 20, 30 and 60 minutes, n = 8 per timepoint. Palatal mucosa, maxillary bone and maxillary nerve dissected separately, 150–200 mg per specimen, liquid scintillation counting, expressed as **ng of drug per mg wet tissue**. One-way ANOVA with Student-Newman-Keuls. Thin-layer chromatography confirmed that at 60 minutes **91.0% of the mucosal radioactivity was unmetabolised ropivacaine**, so the counts are drug and not metabolite.
**Ropivacaine in the palatal mucosa at the injection site:**

`DERIVED` Molarity assumes wet-tissue density ≈ 1 g/mL and MW 328.9. The constricting window from 4.5 is **0.3–1.5 mM at the peak**, with contraction detectable from about **0.03 mM**. The non-monotonic 2-to-5-minute values are between-animal sampling, not a continuous trace; the paper reports no significant difference between 2 and 5 or between 5 and 10 minutes.
### What this confirms
`INFERENCE` **The core of the mechanism survives and is now measured rather than inferred.** A 0.5% solution is 15.2 mM. **The tissue concentration 30 seconds later is 0.85 mM — an eighteen-fold fall, and already inside the constricting window at the first measurement.** The injected concentration is not what the vessels see. That was the central claim and it is correct.
### What this breaks
`INFERENCE` **The timing was wrong, and this page said it repeatedly.** The previous account had a wheal being injected far above the window and **descending into it over hours**, with concentration setting *when* narrowing begins. On this data the descent takes **seconds**, the site sits near the constricting peak from 0.5 to 10 minutes, reaches the bottom threshold by 20 minutes, and is below the window by 60.
Three consequences, all of which have to be said plainly:
1. **“Concentration sets WHEN narrowing starts” is not supportable.** If an 18-fold dilution happens within 30 seconds, every clinical concentration is in or near the window almost immediately. A fourfold difference in the syringe is roughly one timestep on this curve.
2. **Cederholm’s first reading, at 20 minutes, may be measuring a site that has already fallen to the bottom of the window.** His entire 20-to-90-minute series would then sit on the tail, not the body, of the concentration curve — which makes the sustained sub-baseline flux at 40–90 minutes harder to attribute to direct vascular action, not easier.
3. **The whole of 4.5’s synthesis has to be restated as: tissue concentration collapses into the window within seconds and out of it within an hour, and what concentration buys is depth of exposure inside the window rather than a delay before entering it.**
### Three caveats that partially rescue the original account
`INFERENCE`
1. **This is a tissue-homogenate average over a 150–200 mg specimen containing a 20 µL depot.** If the drug had stayed within the injected volume, the concentration *there* would be roughly **7.5 to 10 times the reported average.** So the peri-injection concentration at 30 seconds may be nearer 6–8 mM than 0.85 mM, and the measured figure is a lower bound on the depot centre. **A wheal has a concentration gradient and this method flattens it.**
2. **Rat palatal mucosa is close to the worst available proxy for leg dermis.** It is thin, keratinised, tightly bound to bone, and sits directly over bone foramina that act as a diffusion sink — the paper tracks drug arriving in the maxilla and maxillary nerve within 30 seconds. Human leg dermis has no such sink.
3. **The volume is 20 µL against a 50–100 µL wheal.** A depot two to five times larger clears more slowly.
### And the flow measurement in the companion paper is the closest anyone has come to the causal chain
`FULLTEXT` **Yamashiro M, Hashimoto S, Yasuda A, Sunada K 2016**, “Epinephrine affects pharmacokinetics of ropivacaine infiltrated into palate,” *Anesthesia Progress* 63:71–79, DOI `10.2344/0003-3006-63.2.71`. Same group, same 20 µL / 0.5% palatal model, **with laser Doppler blood flow measured alongside tissue concentration.**
**Concentration, plain ropivacaine, right palatal mucosa:** maximum **219.5 ± 37.8 ng/mg at 2 minutes**, then a rapid fall to 10 minutes; **from 20 to 50 minutes it sat at about 10% of maximum** and stayed there. With epinephrine 10 µg/mL: maximum 252.1 ± 38.9 at 2 minutes, still **208.6 ± 37.5 at 10 minutes**, and **3 to 5 times the plain concentration from 10 to 60 minutes.**
**Spread and bone, plain versus epinephrine:** the **maxillary bone** beneath the injection reached 31.0 ± 6.9 ng/mg at 2 minutes — **14% of the concentration in the overlying mucosa** — rising to 52.2 ± 5.2 with epinephrine, and the epinephrine-to-plain ratio in bone grew over time (1.7, 2.9, 6.0 and 7.6× at 2, 20, 30 and 50 minutes). The **contralateral** palatal mucosa reached 13.4 ± 2.8 ng/mg at 2 minutes, **6% of the injected side**, rising to 34.4 ± 1.6 with epinephrine. Imaging-plate autoradiography put the whole-section quantity at 20 minutes at about **17% of the 2-minute value**, almost gone by 60 minutes, with epinephrine giving roughly **4×** more at 20, 40 and 60 minutes. Serum radioactivity fell to **49% of its 2-minute maximum by 50 minutes** but about **30% of maximum persisted from 2 to 6 hours.**
`INFERENCE` The 6% contralateral figure is a useful scale check on wheal spacing: at 20 µL, measurable drug crossed to the other side of the palate but at one-sixteenth the concentration. **It is the only quantitative statement anywhere in this collection about how far injected drug travels, and it supports the 3 cm spacing in 11.4 being adequate rather than marginal.**
**Blood flow, same model:** saline did not significantly change flow from 10 to 60 minutes. **Plain 0.5% ropivacaine tracked the saline control until 2.5 minutes, then fell progressively, reaching 77% of control at 45 minutes, with the reduction significant from 40 to 60 minutes.**
`INFERENCE` **Now put the two together, because this is the most important single observation the report surfaced.**
- At **2 minutes** the tissue is at 0.67 mM — **above the constricting peak** — and the flow is **indistinguishable from saline.**
- At **40–60 minutes** the tissue is at roughly 0.067 mM — **inside the lower part of the constricting window** — and the flow is **significantly reduced.**
**The constriction appears when the drug descends into the window, not when the drug is at its most concentrated.** That is the mechanism in 4.5, observed as concentration and flow in one preparation, which section 13 previously listed as never having been done. It is rat oral mucosa, the flow was recorded at the lip while the concentration was measured at the palate, and n = 4 for the flow arm — so it is a strong signal in the wrong tissue, not proof. **But the sign, the shape and the ordering all match.**
One thing that does not fit neatly: a simple window model predicts maximum constriction as the concentration crosses the 0.3 mM peak, which in Yamashiro’s data is somewhere between 10 and 20 minutes, whereas the measured flow reduction is progressive and deepest late. **Record that as an imperfect fit rather than a clean confirmation.**
### Independent support from an unexpected direction
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` Popitz-Bergez, Leeson & Strichartz 1995 tracked ¹⁴C-lidocaine after a 0.1 mL 1% rat sciatic block. Intraneural drug was 1.6% of the injected dose at full block, 0.3% when deep pain sensation returned, and 0.065% at full recovery — and the intraneural concentrations at full block corresponded to equilibration in **0.7–0.9 mM**, i.e. 7–9 × 10⁻⁴ M. **That is inside the constricting window.** Different drug, different tissue, but it independently establishes that the concentration actually reached in tissue during a working block is three orders of magnitude below the concentration in the syringe.
### The conclusion, stated carefully
> **This subsection was rewritten after the tissue-concentration measurement was found. The earlier version claimed a descent over hours, with the ampoule concentration setting when narrowing begins. Both halves of that are retracted.**
`INFERENCE` **What the evidence now supports:**
1. **Every wheal is injected far above the concentration that constricts, and the tissue concentration falls into the constricting window.** This is measured, not inferred: 15.2 mM in the syringe, **0.85 mM in the tissue 30 seconds later** (Kimi 2012), and Sohn 2012’s independent protein-binding calculation puts the expected free concentration for clinical ropivacaine at 0.55–1.63 mM — inside the window.
2. **The descent takes seconds, not hours.** The site sits near the constricting peak from about 30 seconds to 10 minutes, reaches the bottom threshold by 20 minutes, and is below the window by an hour, in rat oral mucosa.
3. **The constriction appears as the drug descends into the window rather than at peak concentration.** Yamashiro 2016 measured concentration and flow in the same model: flow indistinguishable from saline at 2 minutes with the tissue above the peak, significantly reduced at 40–60 minutes with the tissue in the lower window.
4. **So concentration does not set when narrowing starts.** What it plausibly sets is **how long the site stays inside the window and how much total drug is present** — and neither of those has been measured for any concentration in skin.
**What remains unmeasured, stated precisely:** the time course in **human dermis**, in a depot of wheal size, with the **gradient inside the depot resolved** rather than flattened by tissue homogenisation. Kimi’s figures are averages over specimens seven to ten times the injected volume, so the depot centre is understated by up to an order of magnitude. Tokinaga’s statement that local injected concentration “has not been measured” was true of the vascular literature when written and is no longer true in general.
`INFERENCE` **Confidence: the direction is now established. The timescale is established in rat oral mucosa and unknown in human skin. The claim that concentration controls timing is retracted.**
## 4.6 Skin colour is a bad instrument — three demonstrations, and the mechanism
This section is the best-supported thing in this document.
### Demonstration 1 — pallor with flow raised
`FULLTEXT` Cederholm 1991, Group I. Saline injected at 0.05 to 0.5 mL. **The larger volumes produced marked local pallor, and the blood flow at those sites was nevertheless higher than at the control sites.** Visible pallor with elevated flux, measured simultaneously in the same subjects.
### Demonstration 2 — flow below baseline with no pallor at all
`FULLTEXT` Newton 2000, on their own bupivacaine and levobupivacaine results, which fell below baseline after 40 minutes: they note that skin colour reflects mainly superficial capillary flow, which need not track flow in larger deeper vessels, so high flow can occur in skin that looks pale and the converse — and then report that **in their own study, while they were measuring vasoconstriction, they never observed any visible skin pallor.**
### Demonstration 3 — the two measured against each other on purpose
`FULLTEXT` **Fruhstorfer & Wagener 1993.** This is the paper that explains the other two. 24 healthy volunteers (10 male), mean age 25.6, range 22–32, in two groups of 12. Neurogenic inflammation raised first by a 1% histamine prick, to create a stable condition of enhanced flow in which constriction can actually be seen. Then 100 µL of test solution injected intracutaneously through a **26-G** needle, deliberately as shallow as possible but deep enough to avoid a pale papule. Group L: saline control, plain lignocaine 0.5%, lignocaine + adrenaline 5 µg/mL, lignocaine + ornipressin 0.1 iu/mL. Group M: saline control, plain mepivacaine 0.25%, and the same two additives. Scanning laser Doppler, 10 points 2 mm apart, one reading per 25 s; **the outline of any pallor transferred to transparent film and its area determined by weighing the cutting.** Analysis at measurement point 4, chosen because there the inflammation and the drug effect combine without tissue-pressure artefact from the histamine weal or the injection. Bonferroni-Holm correction.
**Results:** histamine gave more than 10-fold baseline flux near the prick, falling to about 4-fold at 20 mm. Flare area 6.94 (SD 2.54) cm² in group L, 7.22 (2.22) cm² in group M. **Saline raised perfusion further, significantly, on top of the histamine flare.** Plain lignocaine 0.5% caused a slight perfusion increase in 11 of 12 subjects and **no colour change whatsoever.** Plain mepivacaine 0.25% did not change perfusion between prick and injection, produced a small flux reduction in the periphery of the inflammation in 10 of 12, and **produced a distinct pallor in 9 of 12** — area about 0.8 cm². Adrenaline and ornipressin both drove flux down to baseline values with marked pallor, around 2.2–2.3 cm² with lignocaine; adrenaline acted faster, reaching 50% flux reduction in roughly 30–60 seconds against 100–150 for ornipressin.
**The mechanism, and this is the answer to why colour and flux come apart:** the flowmeter measures **the volume of blood per minute passing through the vertically arranged capillary loops**, whereas skin colour is determined mainly by **the volume of blood present within the deeper horizontal networks of the venous plexus.** One is a rate through arterioles and capillaries; the other is a standing volume in venules and veins. **If a drug acts differentially on the precapillary and postcapillary beds, a large flux in pale skin is the result.** They propose mepivacaine has a stronger constrictive effect on venules and veins than on arterioles and precapillary sphincters.
And they state the consequence for exactly the situation of a wheal in untreated skin: **when a small concentration is injected into untreated skin, trauma-elicited neurogenic inflammation overcomes the mild constrictor action on precapillary vessels, leading to a flux increase; simultaneously, in the postcapillary vascular bed, vasoconstriction prevails, and the resulting pallor has been observed in several studies.**
`FULLTEXT` Their supporting evidence from a different route, reported second-hand in the held paper rather than read at its own source (Jorfeldt 1970, not held): when lignocaine and mepivacaine were infused into the brachial artery of human volunteers, **no direct effects on vascular resistance occurred, whereas venous tone was increased markedly.** They also note that intradermal endothelin-1 causes reduced cutaneous perfusion **together with a small pallor**, and raise but do not settle whether mepivacaine acts by stimulating endothelin release.
### The historical precedent where colour and duration did track together
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Aps & Reynolds 1978.** This is where the nil/pink/pale scoring method used by Cederholm 1994 comes from. 17 volunteers, double-blind, L(−) and D(+) bupivacaine at eight concentrations from 0.06 to 7.69 mmol/L (0.002–0.25%) plus saline, intradermal; colour scored and pinprick analgesia assessed every 10 minutes to 50% recovery. Both isomers were vasodilator at 7.69 mmol/L; the incidence of vasodilatation fell as concentration fell, faster for L(−). **Only L(−) constricted, maximally at 0.48 mmol/L, where the incidence of pallor was 92%.** And L(−) outlasted D(+) from 0.48 to 3.84 mmol/L, which they read as higher apparent in-vivo potency. Longer block tracked the pallor — which is the origin of the whole “narrowing buys duration” reasoning in this literature. Note the constricting peak: 0.48 mmol/L = 4.8 × 10⁻⁴ M, **inside the window**.
## 4.7 Three mechanisms, not one
`FULLTEXT` Fruhstorfer & Wagener 1993 list three separate mechanisms by which a small quantity of local anaesthetic injected into skin changes flow. The second is new to this project and bears directly on why the human’s legs may behave unlike published forearms.
1. **The initial mechanical trauma and chemical irritation stimulate nociceptors and cause additional vasodilatation.** This is the flare, and on the evidence in 4.2 it is the dominant term early.
2. **The local anaesthetic blocks sympathetic axons and reduces thermoregulatory vasoconstrictor tone** — and they note this is “true for hands and feet.”
3. **The local anaesthetic has intrinsic effects on vascular smooth muscle**, direct or through mediators. This is the biphasic effect of sections 4.4 and 4.5.
### The flare is measurably blunted in neuropathic skin
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` This page previously raised reduced flare as a possibility without a number. The **LDIflare** literature supplies one, and it is large. **Every figure in the table below is from a published abstract; none of these papers has been read in full by this document.**
