Krishnan 2009 also found intraepidermal nerve fibre densities of 307.6 ± 164.5 (painful), 205.8 ± 165.3 (painless) and 424.9 ± 176.3 fibres/mm² (controls), with flare area and fibre density correlated at **r = 0.57, P \\< 0.0001.** `ABSTRACT-ONLY` Abraham 2016 reports LDIflare sensitivity of 64% in clinically suspected small fibre neuropathy, 64% in mixed neuropathy and 86% in diabetic mixed neuropathy — a useful but imperfect marker.
`INFERENCE` **So neuropathic skin mounts roughly a third of the axon-reflex flare that healthy skin does**, and painful and painless neuropathy are indistinguishable on this measure. One consequence:
1. **Note that a local anaesthetic blunts the flare about as much as neuropathy does** — 5.2 cm² to 1.62 cm² under topical anaesthesia. `INFERENCE` So a ropivacaine wheal suppresses the very flare its own needle creates, which is a third term nobody has separated.
`INFERENCE` **What the LDIflare data do NOT show:** that ropivacaine constricts earlier or more deeply in neuropathic skin. **No study has injected a local anaesthetic into neuropathic human skin and measured the vascular response against untreated, needle-only and saline controls.** That remains gap 5 in section 13 and it is the single most directly relevant missing experiment for him.
`INFERENCE` **Mechanism 2 predicts that the size of the dilating response depends on how much resting constrictor tone there is to release.** A cold limb with high tone dilates more on injection than a warm one. And **a leg with small fibre neuropathy may have less sympathetic vasoconstrictor tone left to release.** Cederholm 1991 established that resting volar forearm flow at 22 °C is low *because* thermoregulatory vasoconstriction is active. If that tone is already diminished in neuropathic leg skin, the injection has less to un-do, the flare term is smaller, and the drug’s intrinsic effect would show up sooner and more clearly. That would predict *earlier* pallor in his legs.
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` Also relevant to mechanism 2, from a different route: **Cederholm, Anskär & Bengtsson 1994** — a different paper from the intradermal one, and abstract-only — found the majority of patients had **marked or complete sympathetic blockade in the lower limbs** after 20 mL of 0.5% or 0.75% ropivacaine epidurally, assessed by skin resistance level and response, skin temperature and laser Doppler flux. The drug does produce sympathetic block when it reaches the relevant fibres.
## 4.8 Epinephrine, properly
This section exists because the page previously carried an unsourced duration figure, and because ropivacaine’s interaction with epinephrine is a mechanistic clue.
### What epinephrine actually does in skin, and how fast
`FULLTEXT` **Newton, McLeod, Khan & Belch 2004.** This is the definitive study for intradermal skin. 10 healthy males 18–35, non-smokers, 22 °C, arms at heart level. **28 test solutions**, 0.1 mL each, 27-G, intradermal, volar forearm, across **four visits** at least a week apart, seven injections per visit, sites 3 cm apart, randomised and blinded to both participant and investigator. Epinephrine at 1.25, 2.5 and 5 µg/mL; bupivacaine and levobupivacaine at 0.125, 0.25 and 0.75%; every anaesthetic dose crossed with every epinephrine concentration; plus saline. moorLDI imaging at 0, 10, 20, 40 and 60 minutes, median flux in a 1 cm circle, AUC referenced to **baseline** flow. Technique reproducibility 15–20%.
**The paper states the conversions: 1.25 µg/mL = 1:800,000, and 2.5 µg/mL = 1:400,000.**
**Epinephrine alone, AUC mean ± SD:** saline **+1.11 ± 1.31**; epinephrine 1.25 µg/mL **−1.05 ± 0.84**; 2.5 µg/mL **−0.87 ± 0.79**; 5 µg/mL **−1.16 ± 1.04**. All approximately 200% smaller than saline, P \\< .001. **No significant difference between the three epinephrine concentrations, P = .586.**
**Timing.** At 2 minutes, epinephrine alone gave a small increase of about **25%**, against **180% for saline** at the same moment — so epinephrine suppresses the needle flare almost from the start. **Epinephrine alone reached maximal vasoconstriction by 10 minutes; combined with an anaesthetic it took closer to 20 minutes.** The authors draw the practical conclusion that a few minutes’ wait after administration is rewarded with the maximum vasoconstrictor effect.
**With anaesthetic.** All three epinephrine concentrations significantly and markedly reduced the responses to both drugs (P \\< .001), completely abolishing the vasodilation and in most cases producing a **net vasoconstriction from baseline by 20 minutes.** The only detectable concentration effect anywhere in the study: 1.25 µg/mL was approximately **10% less effective** than the other two against bupivacaine 0.75% (P = .001) — which the authors call statistically significant but physiologically small.
`FULLTEXT` Newton 2005 reproduces this, finding that epinephrine 2.5 µg/mL added to the five highest anaesthetic concentrations put **every** response below saline, P \\< .001 for both drugs.
`INFERENCE` **In skin, epinephrine has effectively no dose-response across the clinical range.** From 1:800,000 up to 1:200,000 the effect is the same, maximal by 10 minutes alone, and it suppresses the flare within 2 minutes.
### Supporting literature collected by Newton 2004
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` O’Malley 1995 found 2.5 µg/mL as constricting as 5 in human neck skin. Siegel & Vistnes 1972 concluded even 1.25 µg/mL was as effective at reducing bleeding in rabbit skin injected with lidocaine 1%. Niemi & Breivik 2003 found 1.5 µg/mL optimal for prolonging epidural bupivacaine. Against those, Gessler 2001 found 5 µg/mL as effective as higher concentrations in ear surgery, Scott 1972 found 5 µg/mL as effective as higher for limiting lidocaine plasma levels after vaginal injection whereas half that dose had no effect, and Liu 1995 found 5 µg/mL as effective as higher doses with lidocaine and bupivacaine in human skin.
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Liu 1995 is the only paper in this whole collection reporting a direct correlation between the magnitude of epinephrine-induced vasoconstriction and the duration of analgesia.** Newton 2004 cites it for exactly that. It concerns epinephrine, not ropivacaine — see 5.8 on why that matters.
### The conflicting subcutaneous study
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Larrabee, Lanier & Miekle 1987**, subcutaneous infiltration, laser Doppler: epinephrine at 1:200,000 and higher gave good vasoconstriction in both magnitude and duration, **and 1:400,000 differed significantly from all others (P = 0.0001)**, while 1:200,000, 1:100,000 and 1:50,000 did not differ from each other.
`INFERENCE` This directly contradicts Newton 2004 on whether 1:400,000 is as good as 1:200,000. **Resolution: route.** Larrabee is subcutaneous, Newton is intradermal, and the whole of this document shows those tissues behave differently. For a wheal, Newton is the applicable study. Both should be recorded; do not present either as settling the other.
### Ropivacaine blunts epinephrine, in two species
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` Cederholm 1992: ropivacaine-plus-epinephrine sites read lower than saline but **significantly higher than epinephrine alone at the 20-minute recording.** Their stated conclusion: the combination **did not accentuate but instead diminished** the vasoconstrictive effect of epinephrine.
`FULLTEXT` Kopacz 1989, in pig skin (full tables in 4.3a): **epinephrine added to ropivacaine changed almost nothing.** Maximum percent change went from −52 ± 11% to −48 ± 8% at 0.25% and from −54 ± 14% to −52 ± 13% at 0.75%. Tested over the full 30 minutes, ropivacaine versus ropivacaine-plus-epinephrine came out at **P = 0.03 at 0.25%** — which does not clear the authors’ own Bonferroni threshold of 0.003 — and **P = 0.9 at 0.75%**, i.e. no effect whatever. Their own summary sentence: **“The addition of epinephrine to ropivacaine did not alter the maximum decrease in blood flow observed.”**
But epinephrine **deepened** the effect markedly for everything else in the study. Against the corresponding plain solution: bupivacaine 0.25% went +90 ± 32% → −73 ± 9% (**P = 0.001**), bupivacaine 0.75% went +82 ± 48% → −70 ± 11% (**P = 0.03**), and saline went +32 ± 17% → −60 ± 7% (**P = 0.001**). `INFERENCE` **So in the same animals, in the same session, epinephrine converted a dilator into a constrictor and converted inert saline into a constrictor — and did nothing to ropivacaine.** That is the sharpest version of the blunting effect anywhere in the collection.
The mechanism candidate: `ABSTRACT-ONLY` Löfström 1992 lists an **“anti-adrenaline-like effect”** among the general properties of local anaesthetics on the peripheral vasculature, and `FULLTEXT` Cederholm 1994 invokes it explicitly, citing Åström 1964 on adrenaline contraction of isolated rabbit aorta strips — a paper not held. Fruhstorfer 1993 reports a parallel: mepivacaine **antagonised ornipressin mainly in the postcapillary bed**, and notes that antagonism between various local anaesthetics and vasoconstrictors including felypressin has been observed in several studies.
`FULLTEXT` **A third and fourth confirmation, cited by Sohn 2012:** combined pretreatment with epinephrine plus ropivacaine had **no effect on the reduction of nerve blood flow compared with ropivacaine alone**, and epinephrine plus levobupivacaine had **no effect on prolonging sensory block compared with levobupivacaine alone.** Sohn’s own interpretation is that the drugs’ intrinsic vasoconstriction is already strong enough that epinephrine adds nothing. `INFERENCE` **So the blunting is now reported in five preparations — human skin (Cederholm 1992), pig skin (Kopacz 1989), nerve blood flow, sensory block duration, and rat aorta pharmacology — and it is a robust finding rather than an oddity.**
> **⚠️ But one study says the opposite, and it is the newest and best-instrumented of them.** `FULLTEXT` **Yamashiro 2016** (4.5), in the rat palatal/labial model, found that **0.5% ropivacaine significantly AUGMENTED epinephrine’s reduction of blood flow.** Epinephrine 10 µg/mL alone cut flow to about 50% of control at 35 minutes and had **recovered to near control by 50 minutes**; ropivacaine plus epinephrine kept flow down and **had still not recovered at 60 minutes**, with significant differences between the two groups at 50 and 60 minutes (n = 4, P \\< 0.05). Their stated interpretation: “**the mild intrinsic vasoconstrictive effect of ropivacaine itself may prolong the vasoconstrictive effect of epinephrine.**” Epinephrine also **retained 3 to 5 times as much ropivacaine in the tissue from 10 to 60 minutes**, and at 60 minutes 13% of the peak amount was still present against essentially none without epinephrine.
`INFERENCE` **A candidate reconciliation, offered as a hypothesis: the augmentation may only appear late.** Kopacz stopped recording at **30 minutes** and found nothing; Cederholm’s ropivacaine-plus-epinephrine comparison is reported at **20 minutes**; Yamashiro’s significant divergence occurs at **50 and 60 minutes**, once epinephrine alone has worn off. On that reading all three are compatible: epinephrine dominates early and adds nothing detectable to ropivacaine in the first half hour, and ropivacaine extends the tail after epinephrine fades. **It is a hypothesis about three studies in three species by three routes and it should be labelled as one.** Either way, the page should no longer say the blunting is uncontested.
`INFERENCE` For a neuropathic leg this is a point *in favour* of the combination rather than against it — less narrowing for the same duration — but it also means any extra hours epinephrine buys are being bought through something other than the narrowing it usually works by, and what that something is has not been established.
### And the clinical epinephrine concentration is at the top of the vessel dose-response
`FULLTEXT` Nakamura 1993 makes the comparison directly: **clinical epinephrine is around 2 to 4 × 10⁻⁵ M, which induces maximal contraction in both artery and vein.** Their conclusion is blunt — the vasoconstrictive effect of ropivacaine **cannot obviate the necessity of adding epinephrine** to prolong action. Ropivacaine at 10⁻² M gave only 16.3 ± 2.0% of epinephrine’s contraction in artery and 5.5 ± 1.1% in vein.
## 4.9 Artery versus vein, and ropivacaine is not special
### The artery/vein split, measured directly
`FULLTEXT` **Nakamura, Toda, Kakuyama, Nishiwada, Yamamoto, Hatano & Mori 1993.** Twelve mongrel dogs, 10–15 kg. Femoral artery rings 0.8–1.2 mm outer diameter, femoral vein 1.0–1.5 mm, segments 2–3 mm long, Krebs’ bicarbonate pH 7.4 at 37 °C, 95% O₂ / 5% CO₂. Resting tension set for maximal 40 mmol/L KCl contraction: 2–4 g in arteries, 0.5–1.0 g in veins. Epinephrine 10⁻⁵ M taken as 100%; absolute values 3670 ± 382 mg in arteries (n = 22) and 2197 ± 263 mg in veins (n = 24).
**Under basal tension:**
<table header-row=\"true\">
<tr>
<td></td>
<td>Artery</td>
<td>Vein</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Ropivacaine max, at 10⁻³ M</td>
<td>**51.5 ± 2.8%** (n=11)</td>
<td>**27.0 ± 3.7%** (n=12)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Ropivacaine at 10⁻² M</td>
<td>**16.3 ± 2.0%** (n=11)</td>
<td>**5.5 ± 1.1%** (n=9)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Bupivacaine max, at 3 × 10⁻⁴ M</td>
<td>lower than ropivacaine’s max (P\\<0.01)</td>
<td>lower</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Lidocaine, 10⁻⁵ to 10⁻² M</td>
<td>no significant change</td>
<td>no significant change</td>
</tr>
</table>
Ropivacaine’s maximum was significantly greater than bupivacaine’s (P \\< 0.01), and ropivacaine needed a **higher** concentration than bupivacaine to reach its maximum.
**Pre-contracted with phenylephrine 10⁻⁶ M** (which itself gave 40.9 ± 2.3% in artery, n=20, and 40.7 ± 2.0% in vein, n=27, of the epinephrine contraction): arteries were constricted **further** by ropivacaine at 10⁻⁴ to 3 × 10⁻³ M (P \\< 0.01) and by bupivacaine at 3 × 10⁻⁴ M (P \\< 0.01). **Pre-contracted veins were relaxed by all three anaesthetics**, with the extent of relaxation significantly reduced by ropivacaine at 10⁻³ M (P \\< 0.01), bupivacaine at 3 × 10⁻⁴ M (P \\< 0.01) and lidocaine at 3 × 10⁻³ M (P \\< 0.05).
`INFERENCE` So under tone, **ropivacaine constricts arteries and relaxes veins.** That is the opposite differential from the one Fruhstorfer proposes for mepivacaine (venous constriction with arterial sparing), and if it carried over to skin it would predict flux falling while colour stayed normal — which is Newton 2000’s result exactly. Dog femoral vessels are not dermal microvessels and this is a cross-species, cross-calibre inference. Hold it as a candidate.
**Not adrenergic.** Pretreatment with phentolamine 10⁻⁶ M or yohimbine 10⁻⁶ M **did not alter** ropivacaine’s contractions. The α-adrenergic system is not involved. The authors also note that ropivacaine’s analgesic action is therefore not mediated by the α₂ mechanism of clonidine or dexmedetomidine.
**A pH detail worth keeping.** At 10⁻² M ropivacaine precipitated in Krebs’ bicarbonate, so that concentration had to be tested in HEPES buffer at pH 6.8 — where adding 10⁻² M ropivacaine **lowered the pH to 6.1.** The authors argue this is actually the more appropriate condition for examining the local effect on infiltration, since the clinical solution (10⁻² to 3 × 10⁻² M) is itself acidic. `INFERENCE` A 0.75% ampoule straight from the vial is an acidic solution, and Cederholm measured his solutions at pH 5.5.
### Human arteries, and the comparison that deflates ropivacaine’s reputation
`FULLTEXT` **Gherardini, Samuelson, Jernbeck, Åberg & Sjöstrand 1995.** Karolinska. **43 internal mammary artery rings from 22 thoracic surgery patients and 14 radial artery rings from 7 patients** undergoing free radial forearm flap reconstruction after head and neck tumour surgery. Rings 2–3 mm, 1 g prestretch (found to give maximal tension to noradrenaline 5 × 10⁻⁶ M), 60–90 min equilibration, Grass force displacement transducer. Endothelium mechanically rubbed off 19 IMA and 9 RA rings; acetylcholine 10⁻⁷ M relaxation used as evidence of preserved endothelium in the others. Concentrations 1.5 × 10⁻⁵, 4.5 × 10⁻⁵, 1.5 × 10⁻⁴, 4.5 × 10⁻⁴, 1.5 × 10⁻³, 4.5 × 10⁻³, 1.5 × 10⁻² M.
**Ropivacaine 4.5 × 10⁻⁵ to 1.5 × 10⁻³ M gave dose-dependent contraction in both rubbed and unrubbed rings; above 1.5 × 10⁻³ M the contraction was released back toward resting levels.** Maximal contractions in rubbed rings: **27% of the noradrenaline response in IMA, 76% in radial artery.**
**Lidocaine did the same thing.** Dose-dependent contraction in rubbed IMA, **maximum 40% of the noradrenaline response**, same peak at approximately 1.5 × 10⁻³ M, released above it. **No statistically significant differences between lidocaine and ropivacaine** in contractile or relaxing effects — not in consecutive rings from the same artery, and not across all rubbed IMA preparations.
**Removing the endothelium did not significantly affect contractile activity.** Denuded vessels appeared slightly more sensitive to ropivacaine but a partial endothelium dependence could not be ruled out.
**Their stated conclusion: in this model of human mammary artery preparations, ropivacaine is not a stronger vasoconstrictor than lidocaine.** And they note the vasoconstriction obtained **was not sufficient to reduce bleeding from a surgical incision in the pig.**
They also caution that clinical conclusions are difficult to extrapolate because the local concentration is affected not only by the given concentration but by the application technique and the type of surrounding tissue — the same point as 4.5, from a third direction.
### But a second study disagrees, and the conflict is real
`FULLTEXT` Gherardini found **no statistically significant difference** between ropivacaine and lidocaine, in consecutive rings from the same artery, with both releasing contraction above roughly 1.5 × 10⁻³ M. `FULLTEXT` **Sohn 2012 found ropivacaine significantly more potent than lidocaine (P \\< 0.001 pairwise) with peaks separated tenfold — 3 × 10⁻⁴ against 3 × 10⁻³ M.**
`INFERENCE` Both are conduit arteries under isometric tension, so route does not resolve it. The available differences: **species** (human surgical vessels versus rat aorta); **number of concentrations** (Gherardini tested ropivacaine at seven points and lidocaine at fewer, so the lidocaine curve is less well defined); and **endpoint** (Gherardini compares maximum contraction *magnitude* as a percentage of noradrenaline, Sohn compares ED₅₀, i.e. the *concentration* at which half-maximal contraction occurs — two drugs can have equal maxima at different ED₅₀s, and that alone may reconcile them). **The magnitude comparison and the potency comparison are different questions and each study answered only one.** Record it that way.
### Three things this kills
`INFERENCE`
1. **“Ropivacaine is a distinctively strong vasoconstrictor” is not supported.** In human arteries it is indistinguishable from lidocaine. In dog femoral vessels at clinical concentration it reaches 16% of epinephrine’s effect in artery and 5.5% in vein. Its reputation rests almost entirely on comparisons against **bupivacaine**, which dilates in skin.
2. **The lidocaine/ropivacaine contrast is NOT explained by a shared window, and the previous version of this page was wrong about this.** The earlier account said the two drugs differ in skin because their clinical concentrations sit at different distances from a window they share. `FULLTEXT` **They do not share a window.** Sohn 2012 measured the peak for each drug separately: **ropivacaine peaks at 3 × 10⁻⁴ M, lidocaine at 3 × 10⁻³ M — a tenfold separation**, and the separation tracks lipid solubility. See 4.5 and the problem stated in 4.9a below.
3. **Liddle 1998 was predicted.** Gherardini said in 1995 that the constriction was insufficient to reduce surgical bleeding in pig; `ABSTRACT-ONLY` Liddle, Hall, Arrowsmith & Smith 1998 then infiltrated 0.125% ropivacaine 75 mg against 0.125% bupivacaine 75 mg with epinephrine 5 µg/mL for reduction mammoplasty and got **696 mL blood loss against 300 mL, with the trial halted prematurely.**
## 4.9a One variable behind three properties — and the problem it creates
This section is new and it is the most consequential synthesis in the document after 4.5. **Read it before telling the human anything about why ropivacaine was chosen.**
### The unification
`FULLTEXT` `ABSTRACT-ONLY` Three separate orderings, each established by a different group using a different method, turn out to be **the same ordering driven by the same physical property — the octanol/buffer partition coefficient, i.e. lipid solubility.**
<table header-row=\"true\">
<tr>
<td>Property</td>
<td>Ordering, strongest to weakest</td>
<td>Source</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>**Conduction-block potency**</td>
<td>levobupivacaine 8 \\> ropivacaine 6 \\> lidocaine 2 \\> mepivacaine 1.5</td>
<td>`FULLTEXT` Sohn 2012’s Table 1, from C-fibre data in isolated rabbit vagus and sciatic nerve</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>**Vasoconstriction potency (ED₅₀)**</td>
<td>levobupivacaine \\> **ropivacaine** \\> lidocaine \\> mepivacaine, **every pairwise difference P \\< 0.001**</td>
<td>`FULLTEXT` Sohn 2012</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>**Local cytotoxicity**</td>
<td>tetracaine \\> bupivacaine \\> **ropivacaine** \\> prilocaine \\> procaine \\> lidocaine \\> articaine \\> mepivacaine</td>
<td>`ABSTRACT-ONLY` Werdehausen 2012; concordant with Werdehausen 2009 and Onizuka 2011</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>**Octanol/buffer coefficient**</td>
<td>levobupivacaine 2.539 \\> **ropivacaine 2.060** \\> lidocaine 1.633 \\> mepivacaine 1.322</td>
<td>`FULLTEXT` Sohn 2012</td>
</tr>
</table>
`FULLTEXT` Sohn’s ridge regression, run because the candidate variables are collinear: **the octanol/buffer partition coefficient is the dominant determinant of vasoconstriction potency** (standardised regression coefficient −0.4701), ahead of anaesthetic potency (−0.2428), pKa (−0.2068) and molecular weight (+0.0169), with r² = 0.923 for the full model. Log ED₅₀ against log P alone: **r² = 0.9563, P \\< 0.001.** The concentration producing maximal contraction in intact aorta against log P: **r² = 0.9283, P \\< 0.001.**
`FULLTEXT` Supporting evidence from the same group’s other work, which they cite: **lipid emulsion attenuates levobupivacaine- and ropivacaine-induced vasoconstriction but does not significantly affect mepivacaine’s**, and the attenuation is larger for the more lipophilic drug — consistent with the drug partitioning into the emulsion rather than the cell membrane.
### Why this matters to the decision, and it is a harder fact than anything else on this page
`INFERENCE` **Ropivacaine’s long duration, its vasoconstriction potency and its local cytotoxicity are three expressions of one property.** They are not independent features that happened to arrive together in one molecule.
> **⚠️ Be precise about the scope of that claim, because an earlier version of this section overreached and contradicted 6.4.** This is a **between-drug** finding. It says you cannot *choose* a drug that has ropivacaine’s duration and lidocaine’s toxicity, because one variable orders both and it orders them the same way. **It does not say the three properties are immune to dose.** They are not: 6.4 establishes that damage scales with concentration in every model that has looked, which is precisely a within-drug separation achieved by dose, and it is argument 3 for 0.25% in 10.4. **What is fixed is the drug’s position on the ladder relative to other drugs. What is adjustable is how far along its own curves you push it.**
`INFERENCE` That still reframes the project. The question is not **“which drug gives me this duration without the vasoconstriction and the cytotoxicity”** — that question has no answer, because the ladder is one ladder. The question is **“given that I have chosen a rung, how far along its curves do I need to go?”** Section 10 is the answer, and this is why the answer is about concentration rather than about switching drugs.
It also explains why the reassuring comparison is always against **bupivacaine**. Bupivacaine is one step further up the same ladder. Ropivacaine is genuinely better than bupivacaine on every one of these three axes at once — and genuinely worse than lidocaine and mepivacaine on all three, for the same reason.
### The problem: this predicts the wrong answer for skin
`INFERENCE` **State this openly. It is a real hole and the document should not paper over it.**
If each drug has its own window and its own peak, then what matters for a wheal is how far above its *own* peak each drug is injected. Work it through:
> **⚠️ This table was recomputed after an arithmetic audit. The earlier version used Sohn’s figure for 0.5% lidocaine while labelling it 1%, and mixed molar conventions between the two rows. Both are corrected below, and the correction changes the conclusion.**
`DERIVED` Both rows now use **Sohn 2012’s own convention (base molecular weights)**, since the peaks come from Sohn and mixing conventions is what produced the original error.
<table header-row=\"true\">

</tr>
<tr>
<td>Ropivacaine 0.75%</td>
<td>3 × 10⁻⁴ M</td>
<td>2.73 × 10⁻² M</td>

**What the corrected arithmetic says:**
- **On total concentration, ropivacaine sits far further above its own peak** — 91× against 14×. On that calculation the model predicts ropivacaine should be deeper into the released, non-constricting part of its curve, and therefore **less** constricting in skin.
- **On free concentration, the two are indistinguishable** — 5.5× against 5.1×. On that calculation the model predicts **no difference between them at all.**
**Neither prediction matches what is observed.** `FULLTEXT` In human skin, intradermal ropivacaine 0.75% drops below untouched skin while lidocaine 1% is the strongest dilator in Cederholm’s entire table — 2.7 V against a 0.4 V untreated line.
`INFERENCE` **The free-concentration version is the more interesting failure, and it is the one to present.** Free drug is what acts on the receptor (5.8.1), so it is the more defensible calculation — and it says these two drugs sit at effectively the same position relative to their own windows. **If position in the window were what mattered, they would behave the same in skin. They behave oppositely. So position in the window is not what separates them, and something drug-specific must be.** That is a cleaner argument for the brake-strength account below than the one this page made before the arithmetic was checked.
`INFERENCE` **So the drug-specific-window model, which is well supported for ropivacaine alone, fails to explain the ropivacaine-versus-lidocaine contrast in skin.** Candidates for what is missing, none established:
- **An active dilating mechanism specific to lidocaine** rather than mere release of contraction. Johns et al. found lidocaine dilates rat arterioles at infiltration concentrations; that may be a positive action, not an absence of one.
- **Dwell time — and this now has measured numbers, in the right direction.** `FULLTEXT` **Kimi 2012 tracked ropivacaine and lidocaine at the same injection site in the same model** (4.5), and the retention differs sharply. In the palatal mucosa, **lidocaine 2% peaked at 846.1 ± 125.6 ng/mg and ropivacaine 0.5% at 279.0 ± 23.3 ng/mg, both at 30 seconds** — but the clearance curves separate:

\tThe paper notes the asymmetry explicitly: for lidocaine **the 10-minute level was significantly lower than the 5-minute level (P \\< 0.01), whereas for ropivacaine there was no significant change between 2 and 5 or between 5 and 10 minutes.** `INFERENCE` **At 30 minutes ropivacaine retains seven times the fraction lidocaine does.** In absolute molar terms at 30 minutes, ropivacaine sits at roughly 0.059 mM — about twice its own \\~0.03 mM constricting threshold — while lidocaine sits at roughly 0.03 mM, which is about a hundredfold below its own 3 × 10⁻³ M peak. **Ropivacaine is still inside its window when lidocaine has effectively left its own.**
\t`FULLTEXT` And the mechanism for the retention is measured too, by equilibrium dialysis in the same paper: **ropivacaine’s uptake by palatal mucosa was about twice lidocaine’s, and its uptake by the maxillary nerve about twelve times lidocaine’s**, with higher affinity for mucosa, nerve, brain, liver and serum — but not for bone. `INFERENCE` **So ropivacaine binds the tissue harder and leaves it more slowly, which is the same 94%-protein-binding story from 5.8.1 seen from the tissue side.** Rat oral mucosa, not human dermis — but it is the first direct head-to-head measurement of the variable this section was speculating about, and it points the way the hypothesis predicted.
\t`FULLTEXT` **The other two compartments, with their absolute maxima**, since they bear on penetration rather than retention:

<td>Lidocaine 2%</td>
</tr>
<tr>

<td>279.0 ± 23.3 ng/mg at 0.5 min</td>
<td>846.1 ± 125.6 ng/mg at 0.5 min</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Maxillary bone</td>
<td>85.2 ± 19.4 ng/mg at 0.5 min, then 49.0% and 44.6% at 2 and 5 min</td>
<td>**188.7 ± 83.9 ng/mg at 2 MINUTES — 2.2× its own 0.5-minute value**</td>
</tr>
<tr>

<td>115.2 ± 16.5 ng/mg at 0.5 min, then **10.4%** at 2 min</td>

\t</table>
\t`INFERENCE` Two things in that table. **Lidocaine peaks in bone at 2 minutes, above its own 30-second value — it redistributes INTO the adjacent compartment, which ropivacaine does not do.** And in the nerve, **ropivacaine collapsed to 10.4% of maximum by 2 minutes while lidocaine was still at 79.7%**, with lidocaine negligible only after 20 minutes. The authors’ hypothesis for the whole study was that ropivacaine’s weaker dental-pulp anaesthesia reflects **poorer penetration into nerve tissue.** `INFERENCE` **So ropivacaine dwells longer in the mucosa and clears faster from the nerve than lidocaine does. Those two facts together are awkward for any simple account** — including this document’s — and they are worth holding onto, because his target is free nerve endings in the dermis rather than a named nerve trunk.
- **Brake strength — the other leading candidate. See 9a.5.** Ok 2013 showed the drug switches on an endothelial nitric oxide brake on its own contraction (4.10a), and Sohn 2012 observed that in endothelium-**intact** aorta the contraction magnitude was higher for mepivacaine than lidocaine, attributing it to differing endothelial NO release. `INFERENCE` **If how strongly a drug engages its own brake governs net constriction in intact tissue, that explains the skin orderings that position-in-window cannot** — including the decisive case of lidocaine and mepivacaine, which share a window and a multiple above it yet behave oppositely in human skin (9a.5).
- **Clearance rate.** Lidocaine at 64% protein binding leaves the tissue far faster than ropivacaine at 94%. If ropivacaine dwells in its window for hours while lidocaine passes through it in minutes, time-in-window rather than position-in-window is the operative variable. Testable — it predicts lidocaine’s constricting phase exists but is too brief to catch at 20-minute sampling. **Weaker than brake strength, because it cannot explain the lidocaine/mepivacaine pair.**
- **Different vascular beds.** Sohn and Tokinaga both measured aorta, and Sohn says so explicitly as a limitation: blood flow to nerves and skin “is controlled by changes in the diameter of small resistance arteriole,” not conduit vessels. The ordering in dermal microvessels has never been measured for any of these drugs.
- **The dilating arm may not track lipid solubility at all.** Sohn establishes lipid solubility as the determinant of the *constricting* ED₅₀. Nothing establishes what governs the reversal above the peak, and Tokinaga calls that reversal “difficult to explain.”
**How to present this to the human:** the window account is solid for why *ropivacaine* behaves as it does across its own concentration range, and it is what section 10 rests on. It is **not** solid as an account of why ropivacaine and lidocaine differ. If he asks why ropivacaine constricts and lidocaine reddens, the honest answer is that the best current model gets the direction wrong and the likeliest missing piece is how long each drug stays in its window rather than where it starts.
## 4.10 Mechanism at the cell — the calcium story
`FULLTEXT` **Tokinaga, Ogawa, Yu, Kuriyama, Minonishi & Hatano 2007**, *Acta Anaesthesiol Scand* 51(9):1155–1160. Male Wistar rats 250–350 g. Endothelium-denuded descending thoracic aorta; rings 3–4 mm for tension, strips \\~5 mm × 3.5 mm for calcium. Indometacin 10⁻⁵ M and L-NAME 10⁻⁴ M added to suppress residual endothelial prostaglandin I₂ and nitric oxide. Fura-2 acetoxymethyl ester 10⁻⁵ M, 6–9 h loading, with 0.1% cremophor EL. Resting tension 3 g (rings) or 3.5 g (strips). Everything expressed as a percentage of the 30 mM KCl response.
**Biphasic in both tension and calcium, in parallel.** Contraction from 3 × 10⁻⁵ to 3 × 10⁻⁴ M, peaking around 60–68% of the KCl response at 3 × 10⁻⁴ M; relaxation from 10⁻³ to 3 × 10⁻³ M. Intracellular calcium peaked at about 40% of the KCl-induced change, at the same concentration.
**Both calcium sources are involved:**
<table header-row=\"true\">
<tr>
<td>Intervention</td>
<td>Peak contraction at 3 × 10⁻⁴ M ropivacaine</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Control</td>
<td>\\~60–68% of KCl response</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Nicardipine 10⁻⁹ M (L-type Ca channel blocker)</td>
<td>37.1 ± 10.3%</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>2-APB 5 × 10⁻⁶ M (IP₃ receptor blocker)</td>
<td>50.2 ± 11.9%</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Both together</td>
<td>**23.7 ± 6.4%** — additive</td>
</tr>
</table>
Contraction fell as extracellular calcium fell, and in calcium-free solution was **markedly suppressed but not abolished.** The **calcium rise**, by contrast, was almost completely abolished by nicardipine 10⁻⁸ M or calcium-free solution (P \\< 0.01) and attenuated by 2-APB 5 × 10⁻⁵ M (P \\< 0.05).
`FULLTEXT` **That dissociation is the finding.** Tension persisted when the calcium rise had been abolished, so **a calcium-independent arm exists** — which the companion paper, `ABSTRACT-ONLY` Yu, Tokinaga, Kuriyama, Uematsu, Mizumoto & Hatano 2005 (*Anesthesiology* 103:548–555), attributes to PKC, Rho kinase and p44/42 MAPK-mediated calcium sensitisation.
**Why relaxation at high concentration is still unexplained.** Tokinaga says so directly: it is “difficult to explain.” Their suggestion is that higher concentrations may attenuate the activity of the protein kinases, the voltage-dependent calcium channels, and IP₃-induced calcium release — and they note both intracellular and membrane proteins may be targets given ropivacaine’s lipid solubility. `ABSTRACT-ONLY` The older alternative, cited by Newton 2005 from Åberg & Andersson 1972: constriction at low concentration from mobilisation of calcium in smooth muscle, relaxation at high concentration from **stabilisation of the membrane surrounding the calcium stores, inhibiting release.** Neither account is established.
### 4.10a The reversal has since been substantially explained — ropivacaine activates its own brakes
`FULLTEXT` **Ok, Sohn et al. 2013**, “Ropivacaine-induced contraction is attenuated by both endothelial nitric oxide and voltage-dependent potassium channels in isolated rat aortae,” DOI `10.1155/2013/565271`. Same laboratory as Sohn 2012, same preparation, but this time **endothelium-intact** rings with a full inhibitor panel. **This was not in the previous version of this page and it fills the gap Tokinaga left open.**
**The finding: ropivacaine switches on two relaxing mechanisms at the same time as it contracts, and both oppose its own contraction.**
<table header-row=\"true\">
<tr>
<td>Intervention</td>
<td>Effect on ropivacaine contraction</td>
<td>What it shows</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Endothelial denudation</td>
<td>**Enhanced**</td>
<td>An intact endothelium limits the contraction</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>L-NAME (non-selective NOS inhibitor)</td>
<td>**Enhanced**, P \\< 0.001 at 10⁻⁴–10⁻³ M</td>
<td>Nitric oxide is the brake</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>ODQ, methylene blue (guanylyl cyclase)</td>
<td>**Enhanced**, P \\< 0.001</td>
<td>Acting through the NO–GC–cGMP pathway</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Neuronal NOS inhibitor; inducible NOS inhibitor</td>
<td>**No effect**</td>
<td>Specifically **endothelial** NOS</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>**4-AP (voltage-dependent K⁺ channel)**</td>
<td>**Greatly enhanced**, P \\< 0.001</td>
<td>**The main non-endothelial brake**</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>TEA (calcium-activated K⁺ channel)</td>
<td>Slightly enhanced, P \\< 0.001 at 3 × 10⁻⁴ M</td>
<td>A partial second brake</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Barium chloride; glibenclamide</td>
<td>**No effect**</td>
<td>Inward-rectifying and ATP-sensitive K⁺ channels not involved</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Indomethacin (cyclooxygenase)</td>
<td>**Attenuated**, P \\< 0.05</td>
<td>COX products *contribute to* the contraction</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Fluconazole (P450 epoxygenase); wortmannin (PI3K)</td>
<td>No effect</td>
<td>Not those pathways</td>
</tr>
</table>
And directly: **ropivacaine induced eNOS phosphorylation in human umbilical vein endothelial cells at 30 and 60 minutes** (P \\< 0.05). 4-AP and TEA added to **denuded** rings already contracted by ropivacaine produced *further* contraction (P \\< 0.001), so the potassium-channel brake sits in the smooth muscle itself and does not need the endothelium.
`INFERENCE` **The coherent story, which the paper assembles and which resolves Tokinaga’s puzzle:** ropivacaine contracts by driving calcium influx through voltage-operated calcium channels. But that same calcium influx activates eNOS, which is calcium–calmodulin dependent, releasing nitric oxide that relaxes the muscle. And ropivacaine separately opens voltage-dependent potassium channels, hyperpolarising the membrane, which closes the very calcium channels the contraction depends on. **So the drug builds two negative feedback loops into its own contraction. As concentration rises, the brakes outrun the accelerator and the curve turns over.** That is a mechanism for the biphasic shape rather than a restatement of it.
### Two consequences, and the second one matters for the human
`INFERENCE`
1. **The class behaves this way, not just ropivacaine.** The same NO-mediated attenuation and eNOS phosphorylation are reported for levobupivacaine and mepivacaine, and Sohn 2012 found all four drugs biphasic. This is a property of the n-alkyl-substituted pipecolyl xylidine family.
2. **Every preparation that defines the constricting window used denuded vessels, so the window describes UNBRAKED constriction.** Tokinaga 2007 denuded and added L-NAME *and* indomethacin. Sohn 2012 denuded and added L-NAME. Gherardini 1995 rubbed the endothelium off 19 of 43 mammary rings. **In living skin with an intact endothelium, the constriction at any given concentration should be smaller than the in-vitro figures suggest** — Ok 2013 measured exactly that difference and it was significant.
`INFERENCE` **So the in-vitro window probably overstates the depth of narrowing achievable in intact tissue**, which is a point in favour of the practical reading in section 10 and against treating the window as a hard prediction. Note one genuine conflict: **Gherardini 1995 found endothelium removal did *not* significantly affect contraction in human arteries** (though denuded vessels “appeared slightly more sensitive”), where Ok 2013 found the difference significant in rat aorta. Species, vessel, or statistical power — not resolvable from what is held.
`INFERENCE` And a methodological aside worth keeping: Ok 2013 found **indomethacin attenuates** ropivacaine’s contraction, meaning cyclooxygenase products contribute *to* the contraction. Tokinaga 2007 included indomethacin in their bath to suppress residual prostacyclin. **If COX products are pro-contractile here, Tokinaga’s protocol would have reduced the contraction they measured, so their peak magnitude may understate it.** Their peak *concentration* — the 3 × 10⁻⁴ M that Sohn 2012 independently replicated — is unaffected by this.
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` One upstream step, cited by Ok 2013 and not read at source: in endothelium-denuded aortae the contraction is mediated **mainly by the lipoxygenase pathway and partly by cyclooxygenase**, downstream of cytosolic phospholipase A₂ activated by calcium influx (Ok et al. 2009, *Can J Anaesth*, DOI `10.1007/s12630-009-9059-0`).
### The correction to the project’s question 8
`INFERENCE` The question as posed was whether Tokinaga’s calcium finding explains constriction at low concentrations **and dilation at high ones**. It explains the first. It does **not** establish the second in skin, for two reasons:
1. What Tokinaga measures at high concentration is **relaxation of pre-tensioned aortic rings**, which is not the same event as widening a vessel in living skin.
2. **The top of the range in skin, 1% ropivacaine, matched saline rather than exceeding it.** There is still no measured vasodilation from ropivacaine in skin at any concentration.
## 4.11 What remains unmeasured about vessels
1. **Local tissue concentration over time at a wheal in HUMAN SKIN.** `FULLTEXT` Tokinaga 2007 stated the measurement had never been made, and this page repeated it. **That is now out of date: Kimi 2012 and Yamashiro 2016 measured it in rat palatal mucosa with radiolabelled drug (4.5), and the result both confirms the mechanism and shortens its timescale from hours to minutes.** What remains genuinely unmeasured is the time course in **human dermis**, in a depot of wheal size, with the concentration gradient inside the depot resolved rather than averaged away by tissue homogenisation.
2. **Flux at a ropivacaine wheal beyond 90 minutes.** Cederholm’s recordings stop there. `ABSTRACT-ONLY` The nearest thing is Häfner 2008, which followed 0.75% ropivacaine flux for **24 hours** — but at a fingerpad after an Oberst block, where the injection is at the digit base and the measurement at the pad, so the needle trauma is not at the measurement site. It found +155.2% with **sustained vasodilatation still demonstrable at 24 h.** This is the wrong preparation for a wheal, and it is the same Tübingen group as Schnabl. It does, however, mean the claim that nobody has recorded ropivacaine skin flux past 90 minutes is **false as stated** and should be narrowed to “nobody has recorded it at a wheal.”
3. **Flux and block duration in the same wheal — still true for skin, but the chain has now been partly observed elsewhere.** Nobody has measured both at one wheal, and both Kopacz 1989 and Newton 2005 say why: pinprick testing disturbs the cutaneous flow being measured. Kopacz states no attempt was made to measure duration for that reason; Newton says pinprick tests were not practical because they would have disturbed the blood flow.
\t**Three partial hits now exist and they change how firmly this gap should be stated:**
\t- `FULLTEXT` **Yamashiro 2016** measured tissue concentration and blood flow in the same rat palatal model, and the flow reduction appeared as the concentration descended into the window (4.5). Concentration and flow, not flow and block.
\t- `ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Bernards & Kopacz 1999** measured **microdialysis lidocaine concentration every 5 minutes, plus laser Doppler flow and sensory block every 10 minutes, for 5 hours** in the superficial peroneal nerve of 8 volunteers, comparing 1% lidocaine with and without epinephrine 2.5 µg/mL. **Epinephrine lowered flow, slowed clearance and prolonged sensory block**, with a two-compartment concentration model. **That is the full chain — flow, drug concentration and block — in one human preparation.** It is perineural rather than dermal, and the agent is lidocaine.
\t- `ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Liu 1995** measured pinprick duration at ten 0.2 mL subcutaneous forearm wheals and flow at matched abdominal sites, for 6 hours (5.8.3). Matched sites, not the same sites.
\t`INFERENCE` **So the honest statement is now: the chain has been observed in human perineural tissue and in rat oral mucosa, but never at a skin wheal, and never with a neuropathic-pain endpoint.**
4. **0.5% and 0.375% against untreated skin over time.** This lives in Cederholm 1992’s figures, which are `PAYWALLED` and confirmed unobtainable. The thesis ladder places 0.5% between 0.75% and 0.063% and no closer than that.
5. **Anything in neuropathic human skin.** Every vessel measurement in this document is in healthy volunteers, healthy animals, or surgical vessels from patients without neuropathy.
---
# PART II — HOW LONG IT LASTS
## 5.1 The intradermal wheal table
`FULLTEXT` **Cederholm, Åkerman & Evers 1994**, *Acta Anaesthesiol Scand* 38(4):322–327. **0.1 mL intradermal wheals, 30-G needle, ropivacaine at 0.25 / 0.5 / 0.75 / 1%.** An earlier version of this page said the per-concentration durations “were not available to this document.” They are below.
**Method:** 10 non-smoking healthy young male volunteers aged 20–30. Randomised, double-blind, simultaneous cross-over — each subject got every solution. Eight intradermal wheals raised on each forearm, volar surface, using prefilled 1.0 mL disposable syringes with 30-G needles, standardised 10-second injection time. Injections spaced over the whole lower arms in a standardised pattern, symmetrically positioned on contralateral arms, **at least 50 mm apart.** Each solution injected as **two wheals** — one for pinprick, one left undisturbed for colour observation. Series I plain; series II the same concentrations with adrenaline 5 µg/mL (1:200,000), at least 3 weeks later. Bupivacaine only went to 0.75% because it would not dissolve at 1%. **Solution pH: saline 6.0; ropivacaine and bupivacaine plain 5.5; adrenaline-containing solutions 3.9.** Pinprick with the blunt back end of disposable dental needles, 10 pricks over a \\~10 mm area while the subject looked away, at 1, 10, 30, 60, 120, 180, 240, 300 and 360 minutes. **Complete analgesia defined as 9 or 10 of 10 pricks not felt.** All injections by one person unaware of which drug; pinprick and colour by a second person also unaware.
**Table 1 — median (range) minutes of COMPLETE dermal analgesia, n = 10:**

All solutions of both drugs produced complete analgesia at the first recording, 1 minute after injection. Ropivacaine significantly outlasted bupivacaine at 0.25, 0.5 and 0.75% (P \\< 0.05). Adrenaline significantly increased duration for both drugs at every concentration (P \\< 0.01), with a minimum of 60 minutes for all solutions used. Adding adrenaline **flattened the concentration-response**: durations were similar at 0.25, 0.5 and 0.75%, with a more marked prolongation only for 1% ropivacaine-adrenaline, significantly longer than 0.25% ropivacaine-adrenaline (P \\< 0.05).
**Note the 1% anomaly and its explanation.** Plain ropivacaine 1% gave 60 minutes — **half** of 0.75%’s 120 and the same as 0.5%. The authors attribute it to a local vasodilatory effect of the high concentration, citing the flowmetry study where 1% ropivacaine produces vasodilatation. **Within this one study, the vessel effect and the block length track each other across the concentration range.** That is the strongest evidence in the whole collection for the narrowing-buys-duration link — and note it is an association across four concentrations in ten subjects, with the vascular measurement coming from a different study.
**A fourfold spread between 0.25% and 0.75%.** 30 minutes against 120. Not marginal.

**Significance markers: † = P \\< 0.05, ‡ = P \\< 0.01, Fisher’s exact test, each drug against the corresponding concentration of the other.** (The original paper uses single and double asterisks; daggers are substituted here because asterisks in a table cell are parsed as formatting and render wrongly.)
**The saline control showed local blanching in only 2 volunteers on 3 occasions in series I, and no skin colour change at all in series II.**
### Three things in these tables that matter for the human
1. **Blanching outlasted the analgesia.** At 0.5%, half the volunteers were still pale at 240 minutes against a median 60 minutes of complete block. At 0.75%, 8 of 10 were pale at 180 minutes against a median of 120.
2. **Some sites showed both at once — a central reddened zone surrounded by a peripheral blanched area.** The authors’ explanation: a central area with a high concentration of drug, surrounded by an area where diffusion has produced lower concentrations with vasoconstrictor effects. `INFERENCE` **That is section 4.5’s mechanism visible to the naked eye on a single wheal, and it is what a 0.75% wheal can be expected to look like.**
3. **The colour findings for ropivacaine and bupivacaine run opposite**, with the same observers in the same sessions on the same arms. A method that registered both directions found ropivacaine pale and bupivacaine red. That is an internal control and it is why the colour scoring cannot simply be dismissed as unreliable — it is reading *something* real, just not flux.
### And two incidental findings worth carrying
`FULLTEXT` **Haematomas:** 15 of 160 application sites in series I, 9 of 160 in series II, from inadvertent puncture of a peripheral vessel. No correlation with which solution.
`FULLTEXT` **Delayed pigmentation:** at 24 hours, two series I sites showed slight redness, both ropivacaine (1% and 0.5% plain), and one site injected with 0.75% bupivacaine was slightly tender to touch. At a control **a fortnight after the 24-hour check, pigmented skin areas 2–3 mm in diameter were observed at the injection sites in ALL volunteers.** Two weeks after that they were normal again. `INFERENCE` **So post-inflammatory pigmentation at wheal sites is the expected course, universal, and resolves in about four weeks.** If the human sees small pigmented dots weeks later, this is why.
## 5.3 Moffitt 2001 — two corrections to the page’s account
`FULLTEXT` **Moffitt, de Berker, Kennedy & Shutt 2001**, *Dermatol Surg* 27(5):437–440. 18 healthy volunteers aged 22–38, double-blind placebo-controlled, local ethics approval. Exclusions: pregnancy, known allergy to local anaesthetics.
### Correction 1 — the volume
**0.5 mL per site, not 0.1 mL.** A 1 mL insulin syringe with a **27-gauge** needle, injected intradermally **over 5 seconds** at three sites along the volar aspect of each forearm by a single dermatologist. Sites randomised to blind the volunteer to the concentration, which also reduced the chance of high concentrations affecting adjacent weaker sites. **Five times the volume of a 0.1 mL wheal.** Every duration figure from this study describes a depot five times larger.
Concentrations: 1, 2, 5 and 7.5 mg/mL with normal saline as control. A sixth site received lidocaine 2% with epinephrine 1:80,000 in a standard buffered formulation, for the pain comparison only, then ignored for the rest of the time course. Assessments by a trained research nurse. Pinprick by **dropping the injection syringe vertically onto the test site from a height of 0.5 cm** — tested every second for the first 30 seconds, then every 5 seconds for onset; **every 20 minutes** for duration.
### Correction 2 — the durations are censored, not extrapolated
The page previously described 692 and 773 minutes as “estimated, meaning extrapolated from the trend.” **The paper writes them with greater-than signs.**
<table header-row=\"true\">
<tr>
<td>Concentration</td>
<td>Mean time to regain FULL sensation</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>2 mg/mL (0.2%)</td>
<td>418 min (SD 217)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>5 mg/mL (0.5%)</td>
<td>**\\> 692 min** (SD 293)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>7.5 mg/mL (0.75%)</td>
<td>**\\> 773 min** (SD 253)</td>
</tr>
</table>
The stated reason: the means for the latter two **“could not be calculated accurately, as at 960 minutes (the time limit of the study) one of the 5 mg/mL and four of the 7.5 mg/mL infiltration sites had not regained full sensation.”** That is right-censoring, not trend extrapolation. **It makes them lower bounds, which is stronger than the page allowed** — but lower bounds on a 0.5 mL depot.
**Onset, mean time to full anaesthesia** (absence of sensation to pinprick): 161 s (SD 178) at 2 mg/mL; **74 s (SD 74) at 5 mg/mL; 51 s (SD 60) at 7.5 mg/mL.** Full anaesthesia was **not reached in 2 of the 20 sites at 1 mg/mL**, and those were excluded from analysis.
**Saline was not inert.** “Normal saline produced a degree of partial anesthesia,” and **full anaesthesia was reached in one control infiltration after 100 seconds and lasted 20 minutes.** `INFERENCE` A 0.5 mL intradermal bleb of saline can abolish pinprick by itself — presumably mechanically — which is a caution about every pinprick-based duration figure from large-volume wheals.
**Pain of infiltration.** Only 5 mg/mL differed significantly from control (P = .002); 7.5 mg/mL versus saline was P = .06. Lidocaine 2% with epinephrine was significantly more painful than control (P = .0002) and than 7.5 mg/mL ropivacaine (P = .0005), but **not significantly different from 5 mg/mL ropivacaine (P = .05).** The authors flag the oddity and offer an explanation: their **5 mg/mL solution was made by diluting 10 mg/mL with normal saline rather than with a buffered solution, which may have altered the pH** — though they note 1 mg/mL was made the same way and was not more painful. `INFERENCE` This is a real caution against the assumption that a diluted solution stings like the concentrate.
**Vessels.** “A vasoconstrictor effect was noted in the ropivacaine infiltration sites. This was **especially apparent with higher concentrations.**” Their Figure 2 is a photograph of a volunteer’s forearms with the caption noting evidence of vasoconstriction at some sites. `INFERENCE` **This runs opposite in direction to the flowmetry dose-response**, in human skin, at overlapping concentrations, and it is an unresolved conflict. Their own discussion states the biphasic story — low doses constrict, higher concentrations dilate — citing Gherardini 1995 and Cederholm 1994, which does not square with their own observation being stronger at higher concentration. Record both; do not resolve.
**Cost.** They note the price differential between ropivacaine and lidocaine is approximately **3:1**, similar in the US and UK.
## 5.4 Ginosar 2016 — the only plain 0.5% figure watched to completion
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Ginosar, Haroutounian, Kagan, Naveh, Aharon & Davidson 2016**, *Anesth Analg* 122(5):1673–1680.
**Pharmacodynamic phase:** 15 volunteers, each receiving **three separate subcutaneous injections of 2.5 mL** — proliposomal 4% ropivacaine, plain 0.5% ropivacaine, and the ropivacaine-free proliposomal vehicle — into the **lower back**, randomised and blinded, **with a separate area used as an uninjected open control.** Sensory assessment at repeated intervals over **72 hours** using pinprick and experimental heat pain tolerance by quantitative sensory testing.
**Plain 0.5% ropivacaine: pinprick anaesthesia 15.9 ± 3.5 hours (mean ± SE).** Heat pain block approximately 12 hours. (Proliposomal: 28.8 ± 6.0 h pinprick, \\~36 h heat pain; mean difference 16.8 h, 95% CI 10.0–23.7, P = 0.001.)
**Pharmacokinetic phase:** 9 volunteers, 6 proliposomal and 3 plain, plasma sampled over 72 hours. **Plain ropivacaine: peak plasma 100 ± 41 ng/mL, terminal half-life 5.9 ± 2.3 hours, AUC 593 ± 168 h·ng/mL.** The 99% upper prediction limit for peak plasma was 279 ng/mL for plain.
**Three limits.** Subcutaneous, not intradermal. **2.5 mL is 25 times a 0.1 mL wheal**, so it is a far larger depot. And plain ropivacaine was the comparator arm of a trial designed to test a liposomal formulation, not its subject. What it establishes is the direction of the error: when plain 0.5% ropivacaine has actually been watched to the end in a person, it ran longer than any intradermal figure.
## 5.5 The rest of the duration literature, and what each figure is measuring
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Keramidas & Rodopoulou 2007**, *Plast Reconstr Surg* 119(7):2148–2152. 70 patients randomised to 2.5 mL of 0.75% ropivacaine or 2.5 mL of 2% lidocaine for digital nerve blocks. Lidocaine mean 2.4 hours (range 1.4–4); **ropivacaine 21.5 hours** (range 19–23), with less analgesic requirement over the first 24 hours (P \\< 0.05). Onset ran the other way: **1.3 minutes for lidocaine against 4.5 for ropivacaine.** No side effects. No significant difference in digital-brachial systolic pressure index between groups.
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Vinycomb & Sahhar 2014**, *J Hand Surg Am* 39(4):744–751. Systematic review, six studies, 335 blocks; ropivacaine longest at 21.5 hours, lidocaine shortest at 1.8. **The 335 blocks are the review’s total across all six anaesthetics and the ropivacaine figure comes entirely from the single 70-patient study above.** The review-level total implies weight the ropivacaine number does not have.
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Schnabl, Unglaub, Leitz et al. 2013**, *Clin Hemorheol Microcirc* 55(2):241–253. 20 volunteers, 80 fingers, no operation. Two-injection dorsal digital technique. Group 1 ropivacaine 0.75%; group 2 ropivacaine 0.75% + epinephrine 1:1,000,000; group 3 tumescent 0.15% (ropivacaine, lidocaine, saline, epinephrine 1:1,000,000); group 4 control, lidocaine 2% with a 20-minute tourniquet. Perfusion: G1 maximum **+160.2%**; G2 no significant early change then **+66.6%**; G3 significant reduction **−54.1%** then **+45.7%**; G4 **−66.2%** then **+248.1%** after tourniquet release. **Longest pain reduction in group 2 at 18.0 hours; the other groups 4.6 to 8.1 hours.**
**The 60-minute claim previously carried on this page is wrong.** The abstract gives no recording schedule at all, and the study reports pain reduction to 18 hours, so subjects were followed at least that long. `ABSTRACT-ONLY` An independent audit found that **a later full-text review by the same research group describes perfusion observations through 24 hours, including explicit 6-hour and 24-hour values** — consistent with the companion study (Häfner 2008, Häfner being a co-author of Schnabl) which recorded flux for 24 hours. **The original paper could not be obtained from the publisher, so the exact timepoint list is secondary-source verified rather than primary-methods verified.** State it as “beyond 60 minutes, apparently 24 hours.
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Safa, Flynn, McHardy et al. 2021**, *Anesth Analg* 132(4):1129–1137. Doubling ropivacaine concentration from 0.5% to 1% for low-volume interscalene block did not lengthen the block: **13.8 against 15.8 hours, not significant.** 60 patients, 20 per arm, so a real difference of this size could be missed. Duration measured as time to a pain score above 3, not by pinprick.
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Christiansen, Madsen, Rothe et al. 2019**, *Eur J Anaesthesiol* 36(9):673–679. Ropivacaine dose held at **10 mg** and only concentration varied across an eightfold range, 0.5 to 4 mg/mL, for common peroneal nerve block in 60 healthy volunteers. Sensory block duration **10.4 to 13.4 hours across five groups, no significant difference.** Motor block duration **did** fall with dilution, from 8.5 to 4.0 hours.
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Ropivacaine US prescribing information, via DailyMed.** Local infiltration and field block: 2 mg/mL in 1–100 mL, or 5 mg/mL in 1–40 mL, in both cases **2 to 200 mg**, onset 1–15 minutes, **duration 2 to 6 hours.** `INFERENCE` That duration is time to first request for analgesia after surgical infiltration, not time to return of pinprick sensation at the skin, and it reflects the doses submitted for approval.
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Breuninger, Nogova, Hobbach et al. 2000**, *Hautarzt* 51. Subcutaneous infusion anaesthesia. Three parts: plasma levels in 10 volunteers after subcutaneous infusion of ropivacaine with epinephrine 1:1,000,000 at 300 mg; an effect comparison in 30 healthy volunteers with 30 mL each of three epinephrine-containing solutions (lidocaine alone, lidocaine mixed with ropivacaine, ropivacaine alone); and clinical application of various ropivacaine concentrations and ropivacaine/prilocaine mixtures in **4,670 operations on 3,015 patients**, ages 0.5 to 95 years, median 54.5, **with no patient excluded.** Plasma levels were low with protracted release from the subcutis. **Ropivacaine’s duration was significantly longer than lidocaine’s (p \\< 0.001).** No side effects or complications, low rebleeding rate, pain freedom generally lasting many hours. **Only finger and penis blocks were done without epinephrine.**
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` A separate Breuninger paper in *Dermatologic Surgery* 1999 is cited in the project as reporting 30 volunteers plus **5,220 procedures in 3,270 patients**. The numbers differ from the 2000 Hautarzt paper; most likely different accrual cutoffs. Both are real, neither has been read at source, and the two accrual figures should not be conflated.
### How to read this spread
`INFERENCE` The range from 30 minutes to 21.5 hours is not a contradiction. It is five different endpoints in four different tissues at three different volumes:

</tr>
<tr>
<td>Label</td>
<td>Surgical infiltration</td>
<td>1–40 mL</td>
<td>First request for analgesia</td>
<td>2–6 h</td>
</tr>
</table>
**Cederholm times when the block stops being complete; Moffitt times when sensation is fully back.** Those are the two ends of the same decay curve. A wheal can plausibly stop being *completely* numb at 2 hours and not be *fully* normal for 13. **Both numbers can be right and the page must stop treating them as rivals.**
**The 8-to-13-hour frame the previous page recommended planning around comes from Moffitt, at 0.5 mL.** For a 0.1 mL wheal it is not supported by anything, and Cederholm 1994 — the only per-concentration intradermal data at 0.1 mL — gives medians of 60 to 120 minutes for complete block.
## 5.6 His own endpoint is a third thing, and it is the easiest of the three
The human measures **absence of neuropathic pain in the leg.** Cederholm measures **complete abolition of pinprick at the wheal.** Moffitt measures **full return of normal sensation.** These are not the same variable and they will not end at the same time.
They come apart in the direction that helps him. **Silencing ectopic firing in damaged fibres does not require the concentration that abolishes normal pinprick sensation.**
### And this is now measured, with a separation of roughly forty-fold
> **This was previously flagged as “mechanism, not measurement.” It is a measurement, and it is the strongest single argument for a lower concentration anywhere in this document.**
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Tanelian & MacIver 1991**, *Anesthesiology*, DOI `10.1097/00000542-199105000-00020`. In **injured** rabbit corneal fibres, lidocaine **suppressed tonic Aδ and C-fibre discharge at 1–20 µg/mL, with an ED₅₀ of 5.7 µg/mL** — about **0.00057% w/v** — whereas **blocking evoked conduction required more than 250 µg/mL**, about **0.025% w/v.**
`INFERENCE` **That is a separation of roughly 44-fold between the concentration that silences spontaneous firing in damaged fibres and the concentration that blocks a normal signal.** Note where the numbers land: 0.00057% is far below anything injectable, and 0.025% is itself well below 0.25%.
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` Tanelian’s own framing: the ED₅₀ of 5.7 µg/mL “corresponds to clinically effective plasma concentrations for analgesia,” and their conclusion is that lidocaine analgesia “appears to result from **suppression of tonic neural discharge in injured peripheral A-delta and C fiber nociceptors**” rather than from conduction block. In vitro rabbit corneal nerve preparation.
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Dong 2008**: in compressed rat dorsal root ganglion neurons, **10 µM lidocaine suppressed the persistent sodium current and the bursting, whereas 5 mM was required to block single spikes and the transient current** — a **500-fold** separation.
### The number that is actually in his units
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Koplovitch P, Devor M 2018**, “Dilute lidocaine suppresses ectopic neuropathic discharge in dorsal root ganglia without blocking axonal propagation: a new approach to selective pain control,” *Pain*, DOI `10.1097/J.PAIN.0000000000001205`. **This is the most directly usable paper in the whole section because it reports percentages rather than µg/mL.**
In vivo electrophysiological recording in rats, with lidocaine applied epidurally to the dorsal root ganglion surface: **0.02% or 0.2% lidocaine substantially suppressed electrogenesis in the DRG with only a modest blocking effect on impulse propagation through the foramen.** Topically applied opiates and GABA blocked neither ongoing discharge nor through-conduction. The authors propose sustained delivery of dilute lidocaine as a strategy for conditions in which ganglion ectopia drives pain, naming post-herpetic neuralgia, trigeminal neuralgia, phantom limb pain, complex regional pain syndrome and radicular low back pain.
`INFERENCE` **0.02% is twelve times more dilute than 0.25% and thirty-seven times more dilute than 0.75%.** DRG surface rather than dermis, rat rather than human, lidocaine rather than ropivacaine — but it is the first number in this document expressed in the units he thinks in, and it sits an order of magnitude below the weakest solution he has considered.
### Two more confirmations, and then the caveats
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Xiao & Bennett 2008**, *Pain*: a 7-day lidocaine infusion yielding a plasma level of **210 ng/mL — nanograms, not micrograms** — **completely inhibited** inflammation-evoked spontaneous discharge in muscle C-fibres and **suppressed cutaneous C-fibre discharge by 50%**, with **no effect on A-fibre spontaneous discharge.** It reduced mechano-hyperalgesia but not allodynia or heat-hyperalgesia.
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Revenko 2000** offers the mechanism: **stimulus-dependent inhibition** of discharge from cutaneous C-fibre mechanothermosensitive units “can explain the paradoxical analgesic effect of local anesthetics at low concentrations, insufficient to block axonal conduction of nerve impulses.” `ABSTRACT-ONLY` And **Woolf & Wiesenfeld-Hallin 1985** showed systemic lidocaine up to 10 mg/kg produced **no** conduction block in Aβ, Aδ or C afferents while still selectively suppressing C-fibre-evoked reflexes — so the analgesia is not a weak conduction block.
**Now the caveats, and two of them cut against the argument:**
1. `ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Kirillova 2011**, *Pain*, is the one paper that measured the dose-response properly in injured cutaneous and muscle fibres — rat sural and gastrocnemius nerves, 4 to 11 days after crush, lidocaine locally from **1 to 1000 µg/mL**. Local lidocaine depressed ectopic activity in both A- and C-fibres dose-dependently, and responses to heat or mechanical stimulation were not suppressed even at the highest concentration. **But: “Depression was weaker in C- than in A-fibres.”** `INFERENCE` **Small fibre neuropathy is a C-fibre condition. If ectopic discharge in C-fibres is harder to suppress than in A-fibres, the separation Tanelian measured may be smaller for exactly the fibre class being treated.** This is the single most important qualification on argument 1 in section 10.4 and it was not in this document before.
2. **All of it is lidocaine.** The separation has never been measured for ropivacaine in any preparation. `INFERENCE` Ropivacaine’s higher lipid solubility and tenfold-lower vasoconstricting window (4.9a) give no reason to assume the ratio transfers.
3. **None of it is a skin wheal.** Rabbit cornea, rat DRG surface, rat nerve trunk, systemic infusion. Nobody has recorded a cutaneous C-fibre during a local anaesthetic wheal in a human. `ABSTRACT-ONLY` Named in this literature and not obtained: **Kankel 2012**, “Differential effects of low dose lidocaine on C-fiber classes in humans,” and **Miclescu 2015**, DOI `10.1016/j.sjpain.2015.04.026` — **the human microneurography entry points, and the closest thing to the missing experiment.**
`INFERENCE` **Net position: the phenomenon is real, large, replicated across five preparations, and measured down to 0.02% and to nanogram plasma levels. It is weaker in C-fibres than in A-fibres, untested for ropivacaine, and never observed at a skin wheal.** State all four.
`INFERENCE` **What this licenses saying to him:** the reason Cederholm’s 30 minutes at 0.25% is a poor guide to his result is not hand-waving about endpoints. **Suppressing ongoing discharge in damaged fibres is a demonstrably easier pharmacological task than abolishing pinprick, by one to two orders of magnitude wherever both have been measured.** His endpoint is the easy one.
`INFERENCE` Still the limiting gap, but state it in its corrected form: **nobody has measured neuropathic pain relief from an intradermal ropivacaine wheal at any concentration — though local anaesthetic infiltration for neuropathic pain is not itself novel and has some evidence behind it.** See gap 1 in section 13 for the studies that do exist and why none of them matches this protocol. **The accurate claim is that the intervention class has been tried; this specific protocol is unstudied.**
`INFERENCE` **And this is the real argument for trying 0.25% — a better one than the one he gave.** If his endpoint is neuropathic relief rather than complete anaesthesia, Cederholm’s 30 minutes at 0.25% is a lower bound on something he does not need, not a prediction of what he would get.
## 5.7 Neuropathic tissue changes the duration numbers — and every figure above is from healthy tissue
This section is new to the page and it is the most decision-relevant material in Part II.
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **ten Hoope, Hollmann, de Bruin et al. 2017**, *Anesthesiology*, DOI 10.1097/ALN.0000000000002035. 25 Zucker Diabetic Fatty rats aged 20 weeks with a neuropathy component confirmed neurophysiologically, plus controls. Three findings:
1. **The minimum local anaesthetic dose of lidocaine for sciatic motor block was significantly LOWER in diabetic animals: 0.9% against 1.4% in controls.**
2. At 60 minutes after block, **intraneural ¹⁴C-lidocaine was HIGHER in the diabetic animals** — the drug lingers. `ABSTRACT-ONLY` **They tracked radiolabelled lidocaine concentrations up to 90 minutes**, which makes this a third tissue-concentration measurement alongside Kimi and Yamashiro (4.5) — in nerve rather than skin, and for lidocaine.
3. Patch clamp on excised dorsal root ganglia showed a **lower inhibitory concentration of lidocaine for blocking sodium currents in neuropathic neurons.**
Their conclusion: **both pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic mechanisms contribute to prolonged block duration in neuropathy** — increased sensitivity *and* prolonged residence.
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Umek 2022**, *Pharmaceutics* 14(9):1824. 24 female C57BL/6J-OlaHsd mice, streptozotocin type 1 diabetes versus control, neuropathy confirmed by thermal latency. Sciatic block with bupivacaine HCl 7 mg/kg 0.25% or liposomal bupivacaine 35 mg/kg 1.31%. Sensory block with plain bupivacaine: **106 min control, 117.1 min diabetic.** With liposomal: **118 min control, above the 270-minute study limit in diabetic.** Diabetic neuropathy significantly increased duration with the extended-release formulation.
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Kalichman & Calcutt 1992**, *Anesthesiology*, DOI 10.1097/00000542-199211000-00017. Streptozotocin rats at 4 weeks. 500 µL of 1% lidocaine or procaine next to the sciatic nerve at midthigh; motor conduction block quantified from evoked hindpaw muscle response at 1-minute intervals up to 15 minutes. **In control animals procaine was much less effective than lidocaine. The rate and magnitude of lidocaine block did not differ between control and diabetic. But procaine’s block was sufficiently ENHANCED in diabetic rats to become comparable to lidocaine in control nerves.**
`INFERENCE` **That last finding is about procaine specifically.** It predicts that procaine punches above its published weight in neuropathic tissue.
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` Kroin 2010 (see 6.6) independently reports that **block duration was longer in the diabetic animals for every drug tested.**
### What this does to the duration estimates
`INFERENCE` Four independent animal studies, across two species and three different diabetes models (Zucker Diabetic Fatty rat, streptozotocin rat, streptozotocin mouse), agree that **local anaesthetic blocks last longer and work at lower concentrations in neuropathic nerve.** Every human duration figure in this document comes from healthy volunteers or surgical patients with intact nerves.
**Direction of the error: the published figures probably understate duration in his legs and overstate the concentration he needs.** That strengthens the case for trying 0.25%.
**But it cuts the other way on safety, and this is the pivot of the whole document.** Section 6 establishes that the one variable every toxicity model agrees on is **concentration × exposure time.** If neuropathic tissue holds the drug longer, exposure time is longer at any given concentration — which is precisely the mechanism Kroin proposed for why ropivacaine separated diabetic from healthy nerve in the one experiment that looked. **Longer block is the benefit and the risk, and they are the same fact.**
## 5.8 Why it lasts — and the vessel claim needs downgrading
Three properties, and they compound rather than add.
### 5.8.1 Protein binding
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Protein binding** is the fraction of drug molecules stuck to proteins in tissue and blood rather than dissolved free in the fluid. **Ropivacaine is about 94% bound; lidocaine roughly 64–70%.** (Markham & Faulds 1996; Nakamura 1993 gives “protein binding \\> 90% for both” ropivacaine and bupivacaine.)
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` Hübler 2015 measured it directly in patients: after 150 mg ropivacaine for psoas compartment block, **a maximum of 5% of the measured ropivacaine was unbound**, with a Pearson correlation between total and free concentrations of 0.96.
The mechanism: a local anaesthetic blocks a nerve by occupying the **sodium channel**, the pore that lets sodium ions rush in, that rush being the electrical signal. Molecules attach and come loose continuously, so what decides whether the nerve is blocked at any instant is the **free concentration** — the amount dissolved loose in the fluid, attached to nothing. Above a threshold free concentration the signal stops; below it, sensation returns with drug still present in the skin but not enough of it free. Blood removes only the free fraction: a red cell passing through can pick up a dissolved molecule, not one attached to a tissue protein. So heavy binding does two things at once — less drug is available to be carried off at any instant, and the bound fraction acts as a store releasing molecules back as free drug is removed. Going from 65% bound to 94% takes the free fraction from about a third to about a sixteenth.
### 5.8.2 Fat solubility
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` Ropivacaine is fat-soluble enough to dissolve into cell membranes and the fat around them, where the circulation cannot reach it, and is released back slowly. Same effect as protein binding, different store.
Worth naming separately because it is where ropivacaine differs from **bupivacaine** rather than from lidocaine: **ropivacaine is LESS fat-soluble than bupivacaine**, which is part of why it is less cardiotoxic — and yet it still outlasts bupivacaine in skin. `FULLTEXT` Cederholm 1994 raises exactly this puzzle and offers a candidate: **there is less lipid tissue intradermally than at other injection sites**, so bupivacaine’s greater lipid solubility buys it less in the dermis than elsewhere. They note that studies of brachial plexus and epidural blockade show **no** duration difference between plain ropivacaine and plain bupivacaine — the intradermal advantage is route-specific.
`FULLTEXT` **Nakamura 1993 offers the competing explanation and it does not involve vessels at all:** if ropivacaine has a longer-lasting action than bupivacaine over a wide range of doses, this may reflect **“not only its vascular effects but also its physicochemical properties such as its steric factor, since ropivacaine is a pure S isomer whereas bupivacaine is a racemic mixture.”**
### 5.8.3 Vessels — and the downgrade
The previous version of this page treated vessel narrowing as a principal reason ropivacaine outlasts lidocaine. That claim now has to be weakened, for four reasons.
1. `FULLTEXT` **Fruhstorfer & Wagener 1993 close by endorsing Covino’s view that the vasoconstrictive and anaesthetic potency of a local anaesthetic are UNRELATED.**
2. `FULLTEXT` **Gherardini 1995** found ropivacaine statistically indistinguishable from lidocaine as a vasoconstrictor in human arteries, while ropivacaine has a “clearly longer action on intradermal infiltration.” The vessel difference cannot carry the duration difference if the vessel difference is not there.
3. `FULLTEXT` **Nakamura 1993** proposes stereochemistry instead, and shows ropivacaine’s effect at clinical concentration is small in absolute terms.
4. `INFERENCE` **Nobody has ever measured flux and block duration in the same wheal** (4.11, item 3), because pinprick disturbs the flow being measured. The causal link has never been observed in one preparation in skin.
**What survives.** `FULLTEXT` Cederholm 1994’s within-study association across four concentrations — where 1% both dilates and gives a shorter block than 0.75% — remains the best evidence for the link, and it is real. `ABSTRACT-ONLY` Aps & Reynolds 1978’s isomer comparison, where the constricting isomer was also the longer-acting one, is a second. `ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Liu 1995 has now been obtained (abstract) and it is weaker support than this page assumed.** Ten 0.2 mL subcutaneous forearm wheals for pinprick with matched abdominal sites for flow; 1% lidocaine or 0.25% bupivacaine with epinephrine from none to 1:3,200,000; laser Doppler flow for 6 hours. **Epinephrine prolonged analgesia dose-dependently — but flow had returned to baseline by 30 minutes, while the duration effects persisted. And duration correlated with vasoconstriction at only ONE timepoint, 15 minutes, at r = 0.53 for lidocaine and r = 0.57 for bupivacaine** — so under a third of the variance, at a single early moment. `INFERENCE` **This is the paper this page has been citing as the direct vasoconstriction/duration correlation, and read properly it shows the flow signal normalising long before the duration benefit ends.** It supports a clearance mechanism operating early; it does not support duration being proportional to sustained visible narrowing. `ABSTRACT-ONLY` And Li 2017 found that in supraclavicular block the ED₅₀/ED₉₅ for changing blood flow (0.35%/1.94%) were **higher** than for sensory block (0.18%/0.33%) — the vascular effect needs more drug than the sensory effect, so they are at least partly independent.
`INFERENCE` **Honest position: the narrowing probably contributes something, it is not the main reason, and the main reason is most likely the 94% protein binding plus whatever the S-enantiomer’s steric properties contribute. State it that way.**
---
# PART III — NEUROTOXICITY
> **⚠️ This part has been substantially reversed from the previous version of this page.** The previous version stated that ropivacaine’s low-toxicity ranking was “the most solidly established finding on this page” with “nothing in the literature moving against the ranking.” That is false. At least four independent studies put ropivacaine ABOVE lidocaine in toxicity, the organising variable is lipophilicity, and ropivacaine is more lipophilic than lidocaine. Do not repeat the old claim.
## 6.1 The ranking is contested, and here is the whole contest
