**Score: four studies place ropivacaine ABOVE lidocaine in toxicity** (Pérez-Castro, both Werdehausens, Onizuka), **four place it BELOW** (Malet, Radwan, Kasaba, Yamashita), **a fifth leans harsher without a full ranking being held** (Yang 2011, where ropivacaine and bupivacaine killed more Schwann cells than the other agents tested), **two find no separation** (Lakhin, Sen), **and the one narrative review says there is no consensus.**
Malet is not the settled answer. It is one data point in a split field, and it happens to be the one the previous version of this page built on. **Note also the pattern in the split: the four studies placing ropivacaine below lidocaine are three different non-human preparations (chick embryo neurons, snail neurons, rabbit intrathecal) plus Malet; the four placing it above are all human cell lines.**
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Malet’s numbers are now verified in the primary abstract** (they were previously carried as unverifiable): LD₅₀ in mM after 20 minutes’ exposure — **bupivacaine 0.95 ± 0.08, lidocaine 3.35 ± 0.33, prilocaine 4.32 ± 0.39, mepivacaine 4.84 ± 1.28, articaine 8.98 ± 2.07, ropivacaine 13.43 ± 0.61.** Protocol: undifferentiated human **SH-SY5Y** neuroblastoma cells, **WST-1** tetrazolium metabolic-activity assay, LD₅₀ extrapolated from concentration–response curves. Statistics: ropivacaine differed from bupivacaine, lidocaine, mepivacaine and prilocaine (all P ≤ 0.009); **ropivacaine did not differ significantly from articaine**; bupivacaine was lower than every other agent (all P ≤ 0.003).
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Pérez-Castro’s ranking is also now verified, and the disagreement is sharper than this page previously said.** Least to most cytotoxic by LD₅₀: **procaine ≤ mepivacaine \\< lidocaine \\< chloroprocaine \\< ropivacaine \\< bupivacaine.** So ropivacaine is second most toxic of the six. Protocol: **the same SH-SY5Y human neuroblastoma line**, **10-minute** exposure, **MTT** colorimetry with LIVE/DEAD fluorescence confirmation, plus caspase-3/-7 and calcium experiments. The numerical LD₅₀ table is not in the abstract and could not be obtained; secondary webpages give mutually inconsistent numbers and none were promoted.
`INFERENCE` **The two studies used the same cell line and reached opposite conclusions about ropivacaine.** That rules out cell-type as the explanation, which is what this page previously leaned on. What remains: exposure time (20 vs 10 minutes), **viability assay (WST-1 metabolic activity vs MTT)**, concentration grid, curve fitting, culture conditions and possibly formulation. **Exposure duration alone does not resolve it. Leave it open and say why.**
## 6.2 The organising variable is lipophilicity, and that is bad news for ropivacaine
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` Three studies converge on the same explanation, independently:
- **Werdehausen 2009:** toxicity correlated with **octanol/buffer partition coefficients** and also with experimental conduction-blocking potency, **but was unrelated to the structure (ester or amide type).**
- **Werdehausen 2012:** toxicity correlated with octanol/buffer coefficients, independent of ester or amide linkage.
- **Onizuka 2011:** “drugs with high lipophilicity were most cytotoxic,” LD₅₀ correlated with **logP**; and separately, apoptosis (as distinct from necrosis) correlated with **pKa**, with the low-pKa drugs inducing relatively more apoptosis.
`INFERENCE` **Ropivacaine is more lipid-soluble than lidocaine** — the project’s own explanation for why it lasts longer (5.8.2) — **so a lipophilicity-driven toxicity ranking necessarily places it above lidocaine.** The two properties are the same property. **The mechanism that makes ropivacaine long-acting is the mechanism that makes it, on this account, more locally toxic than lidocaine.** That is a coherent and uncomfortable position, and it is the single most important reframing in this part.
`FULLTEXT` **And it extends further than toxicity. Sohn 2012 established, by ridge regression on four drugs, that the octanol/buffer partition coefficient is also the dominant determinant of vasoconstriction potency** (standardised coefficient −0.4701; log ED₅₀ against log P, r² = 0.9563, P \\< 0.001). **So the same single variable orders conduction-block potency, vasoconstriction potency, and local cytotoxicity, and it orders all three the same way.** The full table and its consequences are in **4.9a** — read that section alongside this one, because it is the same finding seen from the other side, and together they are the strongest reason in this document to prefer the lowest concentration that works.
Note the ordering it produces is almost perfectly the potency ordering: tetracaine and dibucaine at the top, bupivacaine next, ropivacaine below bupivacaine but above lidocaine, and mepivacaine/articaine/procaine at the bottom. Ropivacaine’s real advantage is over **bupivacaine**, which is where it has always been.
## 6.3 Being a pure S-enantiomer does not help locally
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Werdehausen 2012** compared S(−) bupivacaine against the racemate, and R(+) against S(−) ropivacaine, in Jurkat cells. **“There was no effect of stereoisomerism on apoptosis and necrosis.”** Their conclusion: “Although S(−) enantiomers may be advantageous with regard to systemic toxicity, they have no advantage in respect of local cytotoxicity in vitro.”
`INFERENCE` This removes an argument the project has been carrying implicitly. Single-enantiomer chemistry buys reduced **cardiac and CNS** toxicity — which is real and is why ropivacaine exists — but it does not buy reduced **local** toxicity at the injection site, which is the relevant kind for repeated wheals.
## 6.4 What every model does agree on
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Damage rises with concentration and with duration of exposure.** Yang 2011 is titled for it. Lakhin 2019 found every concentration of both lidocaine and ropivacaine produced inflammation and tissue damage against saline, with damage rising as concentration rose. Kalichman, Powell & Myers 1989 found **all four** drugs tested produced a concentration-dependent increase in **every** measure of injury. Aps & Badr 2020 conclude that all dental local anaesthetics are potentially neurotoxic “in a concentration and/or exposure time fashion.”
`INFERENCE` **This is the only part of the toxicity literature that can carry weight, and it is the part that argues for lower concentration.** Halving the concentration is a factor of two on the variable everyone agrees about. Ropivacaine’s claimed advantage over lidocaine is a factor of four on one measure in one cell line — and four other studies say the advantage runs the other way. **The concentration argument is now the stronger of the two by a wide margin.**
Against it: section 5.7 establishes that neuropathic tissue holds the drug longer, so exposure time is longer at any concentration. And Radwan 2002’s reversibility finding cuts in ropivacaine’s favour: at 20 hours after washout, **bupivacaine and ropivacaine showed insignificant growth cone collapse compared with control, whereas lidocaine and mepivacaine remained significantly higher.** Larger nerve growth factor concentrations did not improve reversibility for any drug.
## 6.5 The finding that matters most for small fibre neuropathy specifically
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Kalichman, Powell & Myers 1989**, *J Pharmacol Exp Ther*. Quantitative histology of rat sciatic nerve 48 hours after extraneural injection of three concentrations each of 2-chloroprocaine, procaine, etidocaine or lidocaine, with computer-assisted morphometry of 1 µm transverse sections plus quantitative electron microscopy. Edema, lipid inclusions and fibre injury were seen predominantly in the **subperineurial** region and less centrally. And:
> **“the Schwann cells of unmyelinated fibers were more likely to undergo lysis after exposure to local anesthetics, whereas those of myelinated fibers were more likely to accumulate cytoplasmic lipid droplets.”**
`INFERENCE` **Unmyelinated fibres are exactly the fibres being treated in small fibre neuropathy.** Their Schwann cells lyse; the myelinated ones merely accumulate lipid. This is the one histological finding in the literature that is selective for the fibre class at issue, and **it is not in the previous version of this page at all.** Caveats: rat sciatic, extraneural, 48 hours, and the drugs tested were two esters and two amides but **not ropivacaine.** Whether ropivacaine shares the selectivity is untested. But the two esters tested include **procaine**.
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Kalichman, Powell & Myers 1988**, *Acta Neuropathol* 75. Companion paper, 2-chloroprocaine, tetracaine, procaine, etidocaine or mepivacaine injected into soft tissue and fascia around rat sciatic nerve. The perineurial barrier was **not mechanically damaged** by the procedure, but at 48 hours **perineurial permeability was increased**, with neutrophils and eosinophils in the endoneurium indicating **disruption of blood-nerve barrier systems.** Endoneurial edema in subperineurial, interstitial and perivascular regions. Axonal degeneration and demyelination, the latter associated with **large lipid droplets in Schwann cells.** Degranulation of mast cells, proliferation of fibroblasts, and macrophage activity in affected areas. They describe it as the first model of endoneurial edema by a neurotoxin that penetrates the perineurium.
`INFERENCE` Mast cell degranulation connects this to the flare mechanism in 4.7 — the same cell population that drives the vascular response also features in the injury response.
## 6.6 Damaged nerve: three studies, and they agree
This is the most decision-relevant block in the document, and two of the three studies are new to the page.
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Kroin, Buvanendran, Williams, Wagenaar, Moric, Tuman & Kerns 2010**, *Reg Anesth Pain Med* 35(4):343–350. Sciatic nerve block in streptozotocin-diabetic rats and matched healthy rats; abnormal myelinated axon profiles counted at 2 days; perineural injection at 0.5%. **Diabetic rats given ropivacaine alone had significantly more abnormal axon profiles than non-diabetic rats given the same drug. Plain lidocaine did not produce that separation; lidocaine only did so when clonidine was added to lengthen its block. Block duration was longer in the diabetic animals for every drug tested.** Bounds: **no nerve in the study exceeded 3% fibre degeneration**, so the absolute effect was small; and a streptozotocin model is not small fibre neuropathy. Standing: 93 citing publications, 110 classified statements, 10 supporting, 0 contrasting, no editorial notices.
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Yu, Geng, Li et al. 2019**, *BJA*, DOI 10.1016/j.bja.2018.08.022. **This is an independent replication of Kroin nine years later with a stronger result, and it was not on the page.** Diabetic state established in adult male Sprague-Dawley rats with intraperitoneal streptozotocin. Injections of **ropivacaine 0.5%**, dexmedetomidine 20 µg/kg, the combination, or normal saline — **all in 0.2 mL** — around the sciatic nerve in control and diabetic rats, n = 8 per group. Sciatic nerves harvested at **post-injection day 7** for light and electron microscopy and pro-inflammatory cytokine measurement.
> **“Ropivacaine and dexmedetomidine alone or in combination did not produce nerve fibre damage in control non-diabetic rats. In diabetic rats, ropivacaine induced significant nerve fibre damage, which was enhanced by dexmedetomidine.”**
Manifested as: **slowed motor nerve conduction velocity, decreased axon density, and decreased ratio of inner to outer diameter of the myelin sheath (G ratio).** Electron microscopy showed **demyelination, axon disappearance, and empty vacuoles.** With an associated increase in nerve **interleukin-1β and tumour necrosis factor-α.** Their conclusion: ropivacaine 0.5% causes significant sciatic nerve injury in diabetic rats, greatly potentiated by high-dose dexmedetomidine, and further studies on ropivacaine use for peripheral nerve blockade in diabetic patients are warranted.
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Kalichman & Calcutt 1992** (already cited in 5.7 for the procaine finding) adds the injury half: at 2 days after extraneural injection, **lidocaine induced edema in both control and diabetic nerves, but 4% lidocaine induced significantly more edema in diabetic nerves than in controls.** No fibre injury in saline-treated nerves.
### The synthesis
`INFERENCE` **Three independent studies from three separate laboratories on three continents, spanning 27 years, all find that a local anaesthetic that does not damage healthy nerve does damage diabetic-neuropathic nerve.** Kalichman & Calcutt at UC San Diego in 1992, Kroin and colleagues at Rush in 2010, Yu and colleagues in China in 2019. Kroin found it for ropivacaine but not plain lidocaine. Yu found it for ropivacaine with **zero** damage in non-diabetic controls. Kalichman & Calcutt found it for high-concentration lidocaine as edema.
The proposed mechanism is the same in all three and it is the one everything else in Part III agrees about: **exposure time.** The block runs longer in neuropathic tissue (5.7), so the tissue is exposed longer, and exposure duration is the variable every toxicity model agrees on (6.4).
**What this justifies, and it is the same list the previous page had but now with a second confirming study behind it:**
- keeping the concentration as low as the endpoint allows
- keeping the treated area small
- leaving the epinephrine out, since it lengthens the block and therefore the exposure
- not adding an α₂ agonist — Yu’s dexmedetomidine arm and Kroin’s clonidine arm both show adjuvants that prolong the block **potentiate the damage**
`INFERENCE` **The honest framing of the whole of Part III for the human: the drug that looks safest in a dish is the one that separated damaged nerve from healthy nerve in both of the two experiments that have tested it that way, the ranking that made it look safest is contested and probably backwards, and the reason is most likely that it works for longer.** **Two experiments — Kroin 2010 and Yu 2019 — is the entire evidence base for that first clause. Say “two,” not “every.”** A third study looked at damaged nerve with local anaesthetic but tested lidocaine and procaine rather than ropivacaine (Kalichman & Calcutt 1992).
Note for balance: Yu’s dexmedetomidine dose was “considerably higher than that used in clinical practice,” by their own statement. And both models are streptozotocin diabetic rats, which is a large-fibre and metabolic model, not small fibre neuropathy.
## 6.7 The human measurement
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Wehrfritz, Namer, Ihmsen, Mueller, Filitz, Koppert & Leffler 2011**, *Eur J Pain* 15(9):907–912. **5% lidocaine patches applied to the skin of healthy volunteers for 42 days.** Result: a moderate but statistically significant **fall in epidermal nerve fibre density** in skin blister roofs from the treated skin, with **no such fall under placebo patches.** Sensory thresholds returned to baseline within two days of stopping; **the fibre count did not recover on that timescale.**
Limits: topical lidocaine, not injected ropivacaine; six weeks, not months or years; a patch covering intact skin, not a wheal; and healthy volunteers with no fibre loss to begin with. **It is nonetheless the only measurement in humans of what prolonged local-anaesthetic exposure does to the fibres at issue, and it went the wrong way.** Standing: 41 citing publications, 32 classified statements, 5 supporting, 0 contrasting.
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` The only objective measure of nerve loss in skin is **intraepidermal nerve fibre density**, counted in a small punch biopsy. This page has said its within-person variation is large enough to swamp a real change; **that is now quantified.**
**Selim 2010** followed **36 healthy volunteers for 24 weeks** with no intervention and found a **mean fall of 4.18 epidermal nerve fibres per mm**, alongside a small change in pinprick response, with **large unsystematic within-person variation in a subset.** For scale, the **sex difference in the same dataset was 12.2 fibres/mm.** `ABSTRACT-ONLY` Side-to-side and time variability and observer effects are addressed further by Lauria 2015 and Smith 2005.
`INFERENCE` **So an untreated healthy person’s count drifts downward by about 4 fibres/mm over six months.** Any treated-site measurement has to clear that drift before it means anything. What a usable study would need: **bilateral or within-limb site controls, a standardised biopsy location, standardised section counting, and a change threshold set by within-person measurement noise rather than by statistical significance against zero.** A raw before-and-after difference at one site cannot be attributed to the exposure. **This is the practical reason the IENFD question is hard to answer even with access to biopsies.**
## 6.8 The counterweight — a possible local anti-inflammatory effect
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` New to the page, from **Cederholm 1995** and its constituent paper **Cederholm, Briheim, Rutberg & Dahlgren 1994**, “Effects of five amino-amide local anaesthetic agents on human polymorphonuclear leukocytes measured by chemiluminescence,” *Acta Anaesthesiol Scand* 38:704–710.
Oxygen metabolite production by human polymorphonuclear leukocytes, measured by luminol-enhanced chemiluminescence, intracellular and extracellular reactions separately, with blood from 29 healthy donors. **By and large a decrease in chemiluminescence was seen with the higher concentrations of the various local anaesthetics. Ropivacaine at 1000 µg/mL depressed both intracellular and extracellular responses, similar to lidocaine at 1000 µg/mL.** Lidocaine showed a minor decrease even at lower concentrations. Prilocaine at 1000 µg/mL produced a marked increase in intracellular response with a reduction in extracellular response. Cederholm flags a possible local anti-inflammatory effect by topical administration as of great interest but requiring further investigation.
`INFERENCE` 1000 µg/mL is 0.1%, i.e. 3 × 10⁻³ M — **within the range a wheal passes through as it clears** (section 4.5). For repeated injection into skin that has already lost fibres, a local anti-inflammatory action is a consideration the page has never carried in either direction. It is one abstract, on leukocytes in a tube, and should be presented as a loose end rather than a benefit.
## 6.8a Four adjacent literatures on repeated or prolonged exposure
`INFERENCE` None of these is about ropivacaine wheals in neuropathic skin. Each covers one of the four causal exposures that 10.8 says need separating, and together they mark the outer edge of what is known.
**1. Dental repeated injection — the closest thing to a repeated same-site human record.** `ABSTRACT-ONLY` Dentistry injects local anaesthetic into small tissue volumes repeatedly over decades. Pogrel 2000, Hillerup 2011 and Gaffen & Haas 2009 establish that **persistent paraesthesia after dental blocks is real but rare**, with **articaine and prilocaine disproportionately implicated**, and an unresolved debate over whether the cause is needle trauma or the neurotoxicity of concentrated agent. `INFERENCE` Relevant because it is the only body of human evidence on cumulative local anaesthetic exposure at a site — but it concerns **nerve-trunk blocks with 4% solutions**, not dermal wheals, and it never measures fibre density.
**2. Continuous wound infiltration — the prolonged-exposure extreme.** `ABSTRACT-ONLY` Human studies expose surgical wounds to **0.2% ropivacaine continuously for 48 to 55 hours**, tracking systemic levels and pain (Bianconi 2004 and later mastectomy work). A rat wound study found **transient histological changes at day 3 but no mechanical deficit at day 14.** `INFERENCE` Reassuring in direction, but the tissue is incised and subcutaneous rather than intact neuropathic dermis, and no study measures innervation.
**3. Mesotherapy and cosmetic intradermal injection — the repeated-puncture harms.** `ABSTRACT-ONLY` Plachouri 2019 and Mammucari 2011 report **infection, nodules, granulomatous reactions, pigment change, scarring and fibrosis, and vascular events** after repeated intradermal injection of heterogeneous mixtures. `INFERENCE` Almost entirely case reports, and causally uninterpretable — puncture, technique, contamination and injectate cannot be separated. **Its value is in naming the outcome categories nobody has looked for in this use.**
**4. Tumescent pharmacokinetics — the dilute-and-large-volume extreme.** `ABSTRACT-ONLY` Klein 1990, Rubin 1999 and Kenkel 2004 show **delayed absorption under dilute epinephrine with high tissue pressure and very large subcutaneous volumes, with systemic peaks arriving 4 to 14 hours later.** `INFERENCE` Directly relevant to the safety record in 10.4 and 6.9; it says nothing about free-drug kinetics in a 0.1 mL intradermal wheal.
## 6.9 The tumescent record, which is the reason this is a practice and not an experiment
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` Ropivacaine has been used in **tumescent anaesthesia** — infiltrating large volumes of very dilute anaesthetic with dilute epinephrine — in thousands of dermatological procedures. Breuninger 2000 reports 4,670 operations in 3,015 patients with no patient excluded, low rebleeding, no complications; the 1999 *Dermatologic Surgery* paper is cited in the project for 5,220 procedures in 3,270 patients. Both used epinephrine 1:1,000,000 and report ropivacaine as longer-lasting than lidocaine or prilocaine.
`INFERENCE` This is a record in **intact skin at low concentrations over far larger areas than a wheal**, adjacent to this use rather than identical to it. It does not address repeated injection into the same fibre-depleted skin over months, and none of those patients had small fibre neuropathy. What it does establish is that ropivacaine in skin is an established dermatological practice with a large safety record at dilute concentrations.
---
# PART IV — DOSE AND SYSTEMIC EXPOSURE
## 7.4 Two interactions worth naming to a prescriber
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Ekström & Gunnarsson 1996**, *Drug Metab Dispos* 24(9):955–961. Ropivacaine is metabolised by cytochromes **P450 1A and 3A** in human liver microsomes — mainly CYP1A2 and CYP3A4.
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` A strong CYP1A2 inhibitor reduces plasma clearance substantially: with **fluvoxamine, by about 70%.** Anything in that class raises the blood level from the same injected dose. Liver or kidney impairment does the same.
`FULLTEXT` **The metabolites, and one situation where they matter.** Yamashiro 2016 states that in humans ropivacaine is metabolised by hepatic cytochrome P-450 to **2,6-pipecoloxylidide and 3-hydroxyropivacaine**, both with significantly less local anaesthetic potency than the parent drug — **but in advanced renal disease 2,6-pipecoloxylidide may accumulate and produce a toxic effect.** `FULLTEXT` In their rat model, serum radioactivity at 6 hours and beyond was “almost the recirculating metabolites,” and about **30% of the peak serum level persisted from 2 to 6 hours** after a 20 µL palatal injection.
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` Gromov 2021 identified the main pharmacokinetic covariates as **protein binding**, allometrically scaled body weight on clearance and volume, and unilateral versus bilateral surgery on volume. Emanuelsson 1997 examined whether ropivacaine disposition is non-linear in healthy subjects — relevant only at doses far above this protocol.
---
# PART V — THE COST THAT DECIDES HOW TO USE IT
## 8. Protective sensation
> **Do not raise this section with the human as a caution.** This is here for reference if he asks.
`INFERENCE` A long block of leg skin is that long without **protective sensation** — the ability to feel bathwater that is too hot, or pressure building at a contact point before it damages tissue. This is not a side effect. It is the drug working, for as long as it works.
It weighs more here than for someone else for a specific reason: in small fibre neuropathy the fibres carrying pain and temperature are the ones dying, so protective sensation is **already reduced before anything is injected.** A block on top of that removes what remains, over whatever area was treated.
What the evidence supports, as reference rather than instruction:
- **Timing.** A block starting late at night and running its course overnight is spent mostly asleep and off the feet.
- **Water temperature.** Test with a hand or a thermometer rather than a foot or a shin on a treatment day.
- **Area.** A smaller treated patch means a smaller region of absent sensation while the duration is still being established.
- **Inspection at the end of the block.** For pressure marks, redness that does not fade, or anything broken. Anything that happened during the numb period will not have announced itself at the time, and this is the only opportunity to catch it.
### A contested premise worth knowing about
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` This document has repeated the standard claim that ropivacaine blocks pain fibres preferentially over motor fibres. **The in vitro literature on conduction block says the opposite, and this is a long-standing controversy rather than a settled point.**
- **Gissen, Covino & Gregus 1980**, *Anesthesiology*: for lidocaine, tetracaine, etidocaine and bupivacaine, **large fast-conducting A fibres were blocked at the LOWEST concentration, B fibres at a higher one, and the smallest slowest C fibres required the HIGHEST concentration for conduction blockade.** Procaine, chloroprocaine, cocaine, tetrodotoxin and saxitoxin behaved similarly on B and C fibres.
- **Fink & Cairns 1984**, *Anesthesiology*, measured it on individual axons in rabbit vagus: mean blocking concentration **0.43 ± 0.15 mM for myelinated axons against 0.63 ± 0.14 mM for unmyelinated**, P \\< 0.01. Myelinated axons blocked at lower concentration, though they showed more conduction slowing before block.
- Against that, **Torebjörk & Hallin 1973**, in awake humans with percutaneous microelectrodes in skin nerves, found that after injecting **a low concentration of lidocaine the C waves were abolished BEFORE the A deflections.**
`INFERENCE` **So human microneurography and in vitro single-axon work disagree about which fibre class goes first.** The likely reconciliation is that clinical “differential block” reflects diffusion geometry, fibre depth within the nerve and the length of nerve exposed rather than intrinsic fibre sensitivity — which is why it looks different in an isolated nerve bath than in a person. **For a wheal, where the drug is deposited directly among free nerve endings rather than having to penetrate a nerve trunk, the in vitro ordering may be the more relevant one.**
`INFERENCE` **What this means for him, and it is a caution rather than a warning:** if C fibres are intrinsically harder to block than larger fibres, then a wheal strong enough to silence C-fibre nociceptors is comfortably strong enough to affect everything else in the same skin. It also compounds the Kirillova caveat in 5.6: C fibres may be both harder to block and harder to silence ectopically.
The conventional account is that ropivacaine blocks pain fibres more than motor fibres — `ABSTRACT-ONLY` McClellan & Faulds 2000 describe it as blocking Aδ and C fibres to a greater degree than Aβ fibres controlling motor function — and Christiansen 2019 found **motor block duration fell with dilution** (8.5 to 4.0 hours) while sensory block duration did not. `INFERENCE` So dilution costs motor block first, which for this use is a benefit rather than a cost.
---
# PART VI — MIXING, AND THE PROCAINE PROBLEM
This part is substantially expanded. The ester/amide interaction turns out to be better characterised — and more persistent — than the page previously recorded.
## 9.1 The intradermal experiment, in humans
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Kim, Goto & Arakawa 1979**, *Anesth Analg*, DOI 10.1213/00000539-197909000-00003. Double-blind trial in **20 human volunteers**, intradermal:
<table header-row=\"true\">
<tr>
<td>Solution</td>
<td>Duration of intradermal anaesthesia</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Bupivacaine 0.5 mg alone</td>
<td>**139 ± 78 min**</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Bupivacaine 0.5 mg + chloroprocaine 3 mg</td>
<td>**36 ± 16 min**</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Chloroprocaine 3 mg alone</td>
<td>**24 ± 11 min**</td>
</tr>
</table>
Mean ± 1 SD. **The mixture’s duration was significantly less than bupivacaine alone, and similar to chloroprocaine alone.** The long-acting amide’s contribution was effectively erased. Their proposed explanation: bupivacaine **competes with chloroprocaine for nonspecific receptors at the biophase and in the surrounding nerve tissue.**
This is human, intradermal, double-blind, and it confirms the 139 → 36 figure the project was carrying. **It is the single most transferable mixing result available** — the only one in the right tissue by the right route.
## 9.2 And the interference persists after the ester has fully worn off
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Corke, Carlson & Dettbarn 1984**, *Anesthesiology*, DOI 10.1097/00000542-198401000-00006. Isolated rat sciatic nerves, compound action potential amplitude after suprathreshold stimulation.
<table header-row=\"true\">
<tr>
<td>Condition</td>
<td>Half-time of CAP recovery after bupivacaine 5 × 10⁻⁴ M</td>
<td>Recovery rate</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Control (bupivacaine alone)</td>
<td>**50 ± 4 min**</td>
<td>1.7 ± 0.6 %/min</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>After 2-chloroprocaine 5 × 10⁻⁴ M, **allowed to fully recover**, then bupivacaine</td>
<td>**25 ± 5 min**</td>
<td>2.8 ± 0.3 %/min</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>After 4-amino-2-chlorobenzoic acid (the chloroprocaine metabolite, **which produces no nerve block itself**), then bupivacaine</td>
<td>**22 ± 5 min**</td>
<td>2.0 ± 0.2 %/min</td>
</tr>
</table>
`INFERENCE` **This is the finding that changes the practical advice and it was not on the page.** Two things follow:
1. **The interference does not require the two drugs to be in the syringe together.** Pretreatment with chloroprocaine, allowed to **fully recover**, still halved the subsequent bupivacaine block.
2. **The agent responsible is the ester’s metabolite, not the ester.** 4-amino-2-chlorobenzoic acid produced no block at all and still cut bupivacaine’s duration to 22 minutes.
`INFERENCE` **Extension to procaine, flagged as inference and not measured.** Procaine is hydrolysed by plasma and tissue esterases to **para-aminobenzoic acid (PABA)**, which is the direct structural analogue of chloroprocaine’s 4-amino-2-chlorobenzoic acid (the latter being PABA with a chlorine substitution). If the mechanism is the metabolite rather than the parent drug, **procaine sessions could shorten a subsequent ropivacaine block even when separated in time, and even at sites that have fully recovered.** This has never been tested for procaine and ropivacaine, in any tissue.
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **One piece of evidence that cuts the other way, and it should be stated alongside.** Foldes & McNall 1952, cited by Willatts & Reynolds 1985: the intradermal duration of procaine and chloroprocaine in man was **not prolonged by adding a plasma cholinesterase inhibitor**, with or without adrenaline. Their conclusion was that **in-situ hydrolysis of ester local anaesthetics is not a factor determining intradermal duration.** `INFERENCE` If local hydrolysis is slow enough not to limit the ester’s own duration, the local metabolite concentration may be lower than Corke’s mechanism requires. **This does not remove the concern — Corke produced the effect by applying the metabolite directly, so the question is how much accumulates, not whether it acts — but it is a reason not to overstate it.**
**What this justifies operationally:** the previous page’s advice was “use one drug or the other, in separate sessions.” **That advice may be insufficient.** The testable version: if a ropivacaine session follows a procaine session at the same sites within some window, and the ropivacaine block comes out short, that is the signal. `INFERENCE` Worth recording procaine dates and sites alongside ropivacaine sessions so the comparison can be made retrospectively. **Does not require changing anything now.**
## 9.3 pH decides the outcome of a mixture
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Galindo & Witcher 1980**, *Anesth Analg*, DOI 10.1213/00000539-198009000-00009. Rat sciatic nerve. A mixture of equal parts commercially available chloroprocaine 2% and bupivacaine 0.5% produced a nerve blockade **with the characteristics of a chloroprocaine block.** Changing the pH of that mixture from **3.60 to 5.56 changed the characteristics to a blockade resembling bupivacaine’s.** Their conclusion: nerve blockades obtained by mixing commercially available solutions are **unpredictable** and may depend on the types of drugs *and* the pH of the mixture.
`INFERENCE` Relevant beyond mixing. Cederholm’s solutions were pH 5.5 plain and **3.9 with adrenaline**; Nakamura found 10⁻² M ropivacaine lowered a HEPES buffer to **pH 6.1**; Moffitt raised pH as the explanation for their anomalous pain result when diluting with unbuffered saline. **pH is an uncontrolled variable across this entire literature and across any dilution the human performs.** A 0.75% ampoule is acidic; diluting it with unbuffered normal saline changes the pH in a direction nobody has characterised for this route.
## 9.4 The two 2025 mixing trials
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Yousef et al. 2025**, *Eur J Anaesthesiol* 42(12):1046–1055, PMID 40859881, NCT06381622. 78 patients, hand surgery, three ultrasound-guided infraclavicular brachial plexus blocks.
<table header-row=\"true\">
<tr>
<td>Arm</td>
<td>Ropivacaine</td>
<td>Lidocaine</td>
<td>Duration of analgesia</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Plain</td>
<td>30 mL of 5 mg/mL = **150 mg**</td>
<td>—</td>
<td>**847 min**</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Weaker mixture</td>
<td>20 mL of 5 mg/mL = 100 mg</td>
<td>200 mg with epi 1:200,000</td>
<td>**536 min**</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Stronger mixture</td>
<td>20 mL of 7.5 mg/mL = **150 mg**</td>
<td>200 mg with epi 1:200,000</td>
<td>**671 min**</td>
</tr>
</table>
**The decisive detail: the stronger mixture contained the identical 150 mg of ropivacaine as the plain arm and still lost 177 minutes.** Nothing was taken away; something was added. So the lost time is the mixing, not a smaller ropivacaine dose. Sensory onset was **not** improved either — 17 to 18 minutes across all three groups. And the lidocaine in both mixtures carried epinephrine 1:200,000, so the mixtures had a vasoconstrictor working for them and still came out shorter. Limits: brachial plexus block not skin; 78 patients across three arms; the difference between the two mixtures lost significance after adjustment for multiple testing. Standing: published September 2025, one citing publication, no classified citation statements yet.
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Gricourt, Occéan, Favrelle et al. 2025**, *J Hand Surg Am* 50(7):790–796. Tests mixing by **infiltration into skin and soft tissue** rather than around a nerve, which makes it the more transferable of the two. 91 patients, hand surgery under wide-awake local anaesthesia (operative field infiltrated, no tourniquet, no nerve block), randomised to 20 mL of 1% lidocaine with epinephrine, or 18 mL of the same plus 2 mL of 0.75% ropivacaine. **Median duration of analgesia 8 hours in both groups.** Recovery scores, rescue analgesia, pain scores through the first week, and chronic pain at three months all unchanged. Limits: this adds a little ropivacaine to a lidocaine field block rather than diluting ropivacaine with lidocaine, so it tests the **reverse** direction of mixing; a median with a 4-to-12-hour interquartile range is a blunt endpoint; hand surgery, not leg skin.
`INFERENCE` Yousef found harm, Gricourt found no benefit, and they test opposite directions of the same mixture. Together with Kim 1979 they leave no reason to accept an unmeasured risk in exchange for a block that comes out the same or shorter.
## 9.5 The proposed mechanism, and the toxicity reason
`INFERENCE` The mechanism offered for the shortening is that the two drugs **compete for the same binding site inside the sodium channel, in the same channel state**, so each interferes with the other’s attachment rather than adding to it. That is a hypothesis, not a measurement, and Kim 1979’s “nonspecific receptors at the biophase and in surrounding nerve tissue” is a different and vaguer version of it. The clinical result does not depend on either.
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` The second reason comes from a different direction. **Zhao et al. 2014**, *Life Sciences*: in rats given the two drugs together into the spinal fluid, **lidocaine’s toxicity to nerve tissue was significantly increased by combining it with ropivacaine**, while combining lidocaine with bupivacaine at up to a one-to-one ratio did not increase it. A high-concentration intrathecal model at 5% lidocaine and 1.5% ropivacaine, which does not transfer directly to skin wheals. What it establishes is that **a mixture is not simply the sum of its parts.**
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Kang, Zhao & Wang 2016**, *Rev Bras Anestesiol*, tested the lidocaine/ropivacaine combination for cytotoxicity in human melanoma cell lines A375 and Hs294T. At 72 hours: lidocaine 2% reduced viable cells to 10 ± 2% and 14 ± 2%; ropivacaine 0.75% to 15 ± 3% and 25 ± 3%; **the combination to 10 ± 2% and 18 ± 2%**; melphalan control to 8 ± 1% and 12 ± 2%. Apoptosis mediated through caspase-3 and caspase-8. `INFERENCE` The combination was not worse than lidocaine alone here, so this does not support Zhao. Melanoma cells, not neurons, and 72 hours of exposure. Weak evidence either way; recorded for completeness.
**Operational conclusion, unchanged from the previous page but now better founded: use one drug or the other, in separate sessions — and see 9.2 for why “separate sessions” may not be sufficient for the ester.**
---
# PART VI-A — HOW ROPIVACAINE COMPARES WITH THE ALTERNATIVES
> **Read this before Part VII.** Section 4.9a established that duration, vasoconstriction and local cytotoxicity are three expressions of one property — lipid solubility. If that is right, then **choosing a drug is choosing a rung on a single ladder**, and the concentration question in Part VII is a second-order question asked after the rung has been picked. This part sets out the ladder. It also covers one drug nobody in this project has considered.
## 9a.1 The ladder
`FULLTEXT` `ABSTRACT-ONLY` Every column below is ordered the same way, and the ordering is lipid solubility.
<table header-row=\"true\">
<tr>
<td>Drug</td>
<td>Octanol/buffer</td>
<td>Block potency</td>
<td>Vasoconstriction ED₅₀ rank</td>
<td>Cytotoxicity rank</td>
<td>Protein binding</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Tetracaine / amethocaine</td>
<td>highest of the set</td>
<td>—</td>
<td>—</td>

`INFERENCE` **Ropivacaine sits second from the top.** It is genuinely and consistently better than bupivacaine, which is the comparison that built its reputation. It is consistently worse than lidocaine, procaine and mepivacaine on toxicity, and consistently stronger than them on duration — **because those are the same fact.**
## 9a.2 The only six-drug human intradermal head-to-head, and it includes procaine
`FULLTEXT` **Willatts & Reynolds 1985**, “Comparison of the vasoactivity of amide and ester local anaesthetics,” *British Journal of Anaesthesia* 57(10):1006–1011, DOI `10.1093/bja/57.10.1006`. **This paper was not in the previous version of this page and it is the single most useful comparative document in the collection**, because it is human, intradermal, 0.1 mL, double-blind, and it measures vasoactivity and duration on the same blebs in the same subjects.
**Method.** 10 fit adults, 8 male, aged 21–40. Two parts, same 10 subjects. Six drugs at three concentrations each plus a saline control, each as a pair of **0.1 mL intradermal injections** on the flexor forearm at 40 mm spacing, position randomised and unknown to subject and investigator. Colour scored **pink / nil / pale** at 5–10 minutes, once the initial distension had gone. Analgesia by pricking the centre of each bleb five times with a lancet, every 5 minutes for the first hour then every 10 minutes, endpoint **time to 50% recovery** of sharpness.
**Concentrations:** procaine 0.1 / 0.3 / 1.0%; amethocaine 0.025 / 0.0833 / 0.25%; cinchocaine 0.01 / 0.033 / 0.1%; lignocaine 0.1 / 0.33 / 1.0%; racemic mepivacaine 0.1 / 0.33 / 1.0%; racemic prilocaine 0.1 / 0.33 / 1.0%.
### The vascular result

<td>Class</td>
<td>Vascular effect</td>
</tr>
<tr>

<td>ester</td>
<td>**Vasodilator, significantly greater than control at the two higher concentrations (P \\< 0.05). NO vasoconstriction at ANY concentration.** Redness well demarcated, no oedema.</td>
</tr>
<tr>

<td>ester</td>
<td>Vasodilator, P \\< 0.05. A true spreading **flare** with irregular margins, plus **weal formation at 12, 16 and 16 sites** across the three concentrations. Its vasoconstriction was not concentration-dependent and not different from control.</td>

**The class split, stated as a single number: of 120 ester blebs, 108 showed vasodilatation. Of 240 amide blebs, 49 did. P \\< 0.001.**
And: **vasoconstrictor activity DECLINED with increasing concentration for lignocaine, mepivacaine and cinchocaine** — the biphasic pattern again, in humans, by colour, in 1985. The trend was not statistically significant.
### The duration result, and the distinction that matters
`FULLTEXT` **Slopes of duration against log concentration, in minutes per log unit ± SEM:**

<tr>

<td>Slope</td>
</tr>

<td>11.41 ± 2.31</td>
</tr>
<tr>

</tr>
<tr>

</tr>
<tr>

</tr>

</tr>

Difference between slopes highly significant, P \\< 0.001 by F test, with significant pairwise differences between procaine and mepivacaine, cinchocaine and prilocaine, and between amethocaine and mepivacaine (P \\< 0.05). **“In general, the steepness of the slope reflected the observed vasoactivity of the agent.”**
`FULLTEXT` **But — and this is the distinction the whole project has been missing — they also state: “A comparison of figures 1 and 2 demonstrates that there was no relationship between vasoactivity and INITIAL analgesic activity.”**
`INFERENCE` **So vasoactivity does not set how well a drug blocks. It sets how steeply duration rises as concentration rises.** Those are two different claims and the literature has been conflating them. Covino’s position that vasoconstrictive and anaesthetic potency are unrelated (5.8.3) is about **potency** and it survives. The narrowing-buys-duration claim is about the **concentration–duration slope** and it survives too. **They are not in conflict; they were never about the same thing.**
`INFERENCE` **This is the best available support for the specific thing Part VII turns on** — why ropivacaine gives 30 minutes at 0.25% and 120 at 0.75% (5.1). A steep concentration–duration slope is exactly what a vasoconstricting drug is predicted to have, and mepivacaine, the most consistently constricting of the six, had the steepest slope of the six.
### Two caveats, one of which the authors raise themselves
`FULLTEXT` **1. Every solution contained 0.1% sodium metabisulphite**, added to stabilise the esters, and it was not inert. The authors report that **the saline-plus-metabisulphite control produced vasodilatation in 65% of blebs, against 0–10% previously observed with plain 0.9% saline** — and that the metabisulphite “appeared to increase the pain of the injection.” They attribute their unusually flat concentration-dependence to it.
`INFERENCE` **Consequence: their amide-versus-ester contrast survives intact, because both arms carried metabisulphite. Their amide-versus-saline comparison does not**, because the control was artificially dilated. When they say the amides produced “less vasodilatation than saline,” the saline in question was dilating three to six times more often than plain saline does. **Do not use this paper to claim lidocaine constricts skin relative to plain saline.**
`INFERENCE` **2. And that 0–10% figure is itself a significant finding for section 4.6.** Plain 0.9% saline injected intradermally is visibly red in under one bleb in ten — while Cederholm 1991 measured saline flux at **4.5 times the untreated level**. A 4.5-fold rise in flux that is invisible to the eye nine times out of ten. **That is the pallor/flux dissociation stated as starkly as it can be**, and it comes from a third independent source.
`FULLTEXT` **3. Complete recovery from every injection had occurred by 80 minutes.** These are short-acting drugs at these concentrations, so the duration figures are not comparable in absolute terms to anything in Part II.
## 9a.3 Procaine, specifically
`FULLTEXT` `ABSTRACT-ONLY` Assembling what is now held:
1. **It dilates skin and never constricts it.** Willatts & Reynolds: vasodilator at the two higher concentrations, **no vasoconstriction at any concentration tested.** `ABSTRACT-ONLY` Padfield 1967, cited in the same discussion, found a **60% incidence of vasodilatation with 2% procaine** intradermally in volunteers.
2. **Its concentration–duration slope is the flattest of the six** (11.41 min per log unit). `INFERENCE` Raising the concentration of procaine buys less extra time than raising the concentration of any other drug in that study — the mirror image of ropivacaine’s steep 0.25%→0.75% response.
3. **It is among the least locally cytotoxic drugs tested anywhere.** Least toxic equal with articaine in Werdehausen 2009; second least in Onizuka; least equal with mepivacaine in Kasaba; fifth of eight in Werdehausen 2012.
4. **But it damages nerve in a concentration-dependent way like everything else**, and it is one of the four drugs in the study that found **Schwann cells of unmyelinated fibres lyse** while myelinated fibres’ merely accumulate lipid (6.5). Unmyelinated fibres are the ones being treated.
5. **It punches above its weight in neuropathic tissue, specifically.** `ABSTRACT-ONLY` Kalichman & Calcutt 1992: in control rats procaine was much less effective than lidocaine, but in diabetic rats **procaine’s block was enhanced enough to become comparable to lidocaine in controls** (5.7).
6. **Ester hydrolysis is not what limits its intradermal duration.** `ABSTRACT-ONLY` Foldes & McNall 1952, cited by Willatts & Reynolds: the intradermal duration of procaine and chloroprocaine in man was **not prolonged by adding a plasma cholinesterase inhibitor**, with or without adrenaline. `INFERENCE` **This slightly weakens the worry in 9.2** — if local hydrolysis is not rate-limiting for the ester’s own duration, the local metabolite concentration may be lower than the Corke 1984 mechanism needs. It does not remove the worry, because Corke’s effect was produced by the metabolite applied directly, but it is a reason not to overstate it.
`INFERENCE` **What this adds up to for him:** procaine and ropivacaine are near-opposite drugs on every axis in this document — vasoactivity, duration, concentration-response slope, toxicity.

`INFERENCE` **The conflict to be aware of:** Willatts & Reynolds scored lignocaine as **paler than control at all three concentrations**, while Cederholm measured lidocaine 1% flux **above** saline (P = 0.005) and Fruhstorfer found plain lignocaine 0.5% raised flux with **no colour change at all.** Three human intradermal studies, three apparently different answers.
`INFERENCE` **Two things reconcile most of it.** First, Willatts’ control was metabisulphite-dilated (9a.2), so their amide arm is scored against an inflated baseline. Second, and more interestingly, **Willatts found lignocaine’s constrictor activity declined as concentration rose** — so pallor at 0.1% and flux above saline at 1% are the same biphasic curve read at two ends by two instruments. **The residual disagreement is Fruhstorfer seeing no colour change at 0.5% where Willatts saw pallor**, and that remains open.
## 9a.5 Mepivacaine — the drug nobody in this project has considered
`INFERENCE` **Flagging this because it is the one genuinely unexplored option the comparative evidence throws up.** It is not a recommendation and there is no prescriber conversation behind it.
**What the evidence says:**
- `ABSTRACT-ONLY` **It is the least locally cytotoxic amide across the most studies.** Least toxic of eight in Werdehausen 2012; safest of four by an order of magnitude in Radwan 2002 (IC₅₀ 10⁻¹·⁶ M against ropivacaine’s 10⁻²·⁵ — roughly 8-fold less toxic); least toxic equal with procaine in Kasaba; middle in Malet and Werdehausen 2009. `ABSTRACT-ONLY` Radwan’s own stated conclusion is that **mepivacaine was safer than lidocaine, bupivacaine and ropivacaine.**
- `FULLTEXT` **It has the lowest lipid solubility of the set** (1.322) and the **least potent vasoconstriction by ED₅₀** (Sohn 2012).
- `FULLTEXT` **And yet in human skin it is the most consistent vasoconstrictor of the six drugs ever compared head to head** — significant at all three concentrations, P \\< 0.001, the only drug in Willatts & Reynolds to reach that threshold (9a.2). **“Most consistent” rather than “strongest”: the comparison is incidence of pallor across concentrations, not depth of flow reduction, and no study has compared the magnitude of mepivacaine’s and ropivacaine’s constriction in skin.** `FULLTEXT` Fruhstorfer 1993 independently found mepivacaine 0.25% produced **distinct pallor in 9 of 12 subjects** where plain lignocaine produced none, with pallor area about 0.8 cm².
\t> **⚠️ Be careful not to let Fruhstorfer say more than it does.** It supports “mepivacaine constricts the postcapillary bed where lignocaine does not” — which is the point being made. It does **not** support “mepivacaine is a strong vasoconstrictor,” and its authors say the opposite in their own words: **“both local anaesthetics have only a mild constrictive effect on precapillary vessels,”** mepivacaine produced a **“small pallor,”** and **“the pallor produced by plain mepivacaine was in all cases smaller than that elicited by the two vasoconstrictors”** (adrenaline and ornipressin). `INFERENCE` **So the calibration is: mepivacaine constricts reliably and weakly. “Strongest of six local anaesthetics” and “much weaker than adrenaline” are both true and neither should be dropped.** The “strongest of six” claim rests on Willatts & Reynolds alone. `ABSTRACT-ONLY` Guinard 1992 found **clinically useful mepivacaine does not raise capillary flow above saline, while lower concentrations blunt the rise** — the only drug in that study to manage it.
- `FULLTEXT` **It had the steepest concentration–duration slope of the six** (31.12 min per log unit).
### The anomaly, and its resolution
`INFERENCE` **This looks like a contradiction and it is the most interesting thing in this part.** The lipid-solubility ladder predicts mepivacaine should constrict *least*. Three human studies find it constricts skin *most*. And the position-in-window model cannot rescue it: **lidocaine and mepivacaine have the same in-vitro peak — both 3 × 10⁻³ M in Sohn 2012 — and at 1% they sit 14× and 12× above it respectively** (`DERIVED` from Sohn’s own stated conversions). **Same window, multiples within 20% of each other, opposite behaviour in human skin.**
`FULLTEXT` **Sohn 2012 supplies the resolution, in one sentence in their discussion:** in contrast to the ED₅₀ ordering in denuded aorta, **the magnitude of maximal contraction in endothelium-INTACT aorta was higher for mepivacaine than for lidocaine**, which they attribute to **differences in endothelial nitric oxide release between the two drugs.**
`INFERENCE` **Put that beside Ok 2013 (4.10a) and the picture closes.** Ropivacaine, and the class generally, contract vessels while simultaneously switching on an endothelial NO brake. **How strongly a drug engages its own brake, not where its concentration sits in its window, is what determines net constriction in intact tissue.** Mepivacaine appears to engage the brake weakly, so it out-constricts drugs that are more potent constrictors in denuded preparations. Lidocaine appears to engage it strongly, which is why a drug with mepivacaine’s window and mepivacaine’s multiple-above-window nevertheless reddens skin.
`INFERENCE` **This bears directly on the open problem in 4.9a, and the two candidates there are now co-equal rather than ranked.** When that section was written, clearance rate was a hypothesis and brake strength was a measurement, so brake strength looked stronger. **Both are now measured:** `FULLTEXT` Kimi 2012 measured the clearance difference directly (ropivacaine retaining seven times lidocaine’s fraction at 30 minutes, and 2× the mucosal and 12× the nerve tissue affinity — 4.9a), and Sohn 2012 observed the intact-aorta magnitude difference that brake strength rests on.
**What separates them now is scope, not evidential status:**
- **Brake strength** explains the **lidocaine/mepivacaine** pair, which clearance cannot — Kimi never tested mepivacaine, and the two drugs’ protein binding (64% and 77.5%) is too close to generate an order-of-magnitude dwell difference.
- **Clearance** explains the **ropivacaine/lidocaine** pair with a directly measured number, where brake strength only has an inference from a mepivacaine-versus-lidocaine observation.
`INFERENCE` **So the honest position is that the two mechanisms probably both operate and each explains a different pair. Neither has been measured for ropivacaine against lidocaine in the same vascular preparation, and all of the vascular work is rat aorta.** Do not present either as the answer.
### What mepivacaine would and would not buy
`INFERENCE` **Would:** the lowest local cytotoxicity of the amides, on the variable the whole toxicity literature agrees about; reliable skin vasoconstriction, which is what he originally wanted; a steep concentration–duration slope; and no metabisulphite or epinephrine.
**Would not:** duration. `FULLTEXT` Block potency 1.5 against ropivacaine’s 6, and in Willatts & Reynolds every mepivacaine bleb had fully recovered within 80 minutes. **His endpoint is hours of neuropathic relief, and mepivacaine is an intermediate-duration drug at best.** `ABSTRACT-ONLY` Löfström 1992 also notes mepivacaine’s isomers differ in vasoactivity where bupivacaine’s do not, and the commercial product is racemic — so half of what is injected may not be the active vasoactive isomer.
`INFERENCE` **The honest summary: mepivacaine is the least damaging amide and the most reliable skin vasoconstrictor, and it is probably too short-acting for what he needs. Worth knowing about; not worth switching to on this evidence.** If he ever wants the vessel effect without the duration — which he does not — it would be the drug.
## 9a.6 What the comparison cannot tell him
`INFERENCE`
1. **No study has compared any two of these drugs for neuropathic pain relief from an intradermal wheal.** Every comparison above uses pinprick, colour, flux, or cell death. Gap 1 in section 13 applies to the whole of this part.
2. **The cytotoxicity rankings are cell lines.** Eight drugs, five cell types, contested orderings (6.1). They establish that lipid solubility organises the field; they do not establish that a wheal of one drug is safer than a wheal of another in living skin.
3. **The duration comparisons are at unmatched concentrations and in unmatched tissues.** Willatts’ six drugs recovered within 80 minutes at up to 1%; Cederholm’s ropivacaine ran 120 minutes at 0.75%; Keramidas’ ran 21.5 hours in a digital block. **Only Cederholm 1994 compares two drugs at matched concentration, matched volume and matched route, and the two drugs are ropivacaine and bupivacaine** — with ropivacaine winning at 0.25, 0.5 and 0.75% (5.1).
4. **Nothing here is in neuropathic skin**, and section 5.7 establishes that neuropathic tissue changes both sensitivity and dwell time. The ladder may be ordered differently in his legs, and there is no way to know.
---
# PART VII — THE 0.25% DECISION
> **This part answers section 1’s governing question. If the human asks only one thing, it is probably this. Read it with section 4.5 and section 5.6 at hand.**
## 10.1 Two premises in the question are false, and they are why it felt unanswerable
### False premise 1 — “0.75 también causa vasodilatación”
He believes 0.75% sits on the dilating side, which is what makes moving down feel like a risk he might not need to take. Every measurement in skin says otherwise.

</tr>

<td>`FULLTEXT`</td>

<td>“A decrease was seen for ropivacaine 0.75%”</td>

The only preparation where 0.75% raises perfusion is the **finger after a digital block**: Schnabl 2013 at +160.2% and Häfner 2008 at +155.2% sustained to 24 h. Drug deposited around named nerves at the digit base, measured at the pad, injection trauma not at the measurement site. That is not a wheal.
`INFERENCE` **And nothing in the skin-wheal literature shows ropivacaine producing more flow than saline at any concentration.** The top of the range, 1%, *matches* saline — a null result on top of the puncture, not a measurement of widening.
### False premise 2 — “0.75 no ayuda mucho más que 0.25”
`FULLTEXT` **Cederholm 1994, the only per-concentration intradermal wheal data: 0.25% gives a median 30 minutes of complete analgesia, 0.75% gives 120.** A fourfold difference, not a marginal one. `FULLTEXT` Moffitt 2001, at 0.5 mL: 2 mg/mL → 418 min, 5 mg/mL → \\>692, 7.5 mg/mL → \\>773.
**Where the “concentration barely matters” belief comes from, and why none of it applies:**
<table header-row=\"true\">
<tr>
<td>Study</td>
<td>What it varied</td>
<td>Route</td>
<td>Applies to a 0.1 mL wheal?</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Safa 2021</td>
<td>0.5% vs 1%</td>
<td>Interscalene nerve block</td>
<td>No. Nerve block; does not reach 0.25%</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Christiansen 2019</td>
<td>0.5 to 4 mg/mL at fixed 10 mg dose</td>
<td>Peroneal nerve block</td>
<td>No. Nerve block; concentration range sits *below* 5 mg/mL</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Moffitt 2001</td>
<td>5 vs 7.5 mg/mL — the “12%” figure</td>
<td>Intradermal, 0.5 mL</td>
<td>Partly, but only over the top of the range</td>
</tr>
</table>
**Not one of those goes down to 0.25%, and not one is a 0.1 mL wheal.** `INFERENCE` The flat concentration-response is a **nerve block** phenomenon, where the drug has to travel to reach fibres and total dose dominates. In a 0.1 mL intradermal depot sitting directly among free nerve endings, concentration is most of what there is.
## 10.2 The answer to the question as asked
`INFERENCE` **Nothing he can buy avoids it.** Two of the three concentrations with untreated-referenced evidence — 0.25% and 0.75% — both go below the untreated line (4.3), and the third, 0.063%, reaches roughly adrenaline’s level by a two-step chain (4.4). On the saline-referenced ladder the only concentration that fails to narrow at all is **1%, the highest tested**, which merely matches saline. **So the direction that satisfies his stated criterion is up, not down — and 1% is not available on the Mexican market anyway** (11.1), which closes off the only option his criterion would have selected.
**Be careful with the strength of this claim.** What is measured is that 0.25% and 0.75% both drop below an untreated site in human forearm, and that everything from 0.5% downward drops below saline. **The untreated comparison for 0.5%, 0.375% and 0.125% is interpolation, not measurement** (4.1, item 1). It does not change the answer, but it changes how firmly the answer should be stated.
**But the criterion as stated is the wrong criterion, and this is the useful part.** From 4.5: every wheal is injected far above the constricting window and falls into it within seconds (Kimi 2012). So the question “does this concentration constrict?” has no time-independent answer. What differs between the concentrations in the measurements is **when** the site sits below untouched skin — though the mechanism once given for that difference has been retracted (item 2 below, 4.5, 10.7).
`FULLTEXT` `INFERENCE` **From the only human intradermal dataset with absolute values, as multiples of untouched skin:**

<tr>
<td></td>
<td>20 min</td>
<td>40 min</td>
<td>60 min</td>
<td>90 min</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>**Ropivacaine 0.25%**</td>
<td>1.25×</td>
<td>**0.75×**</td>
<td>**0.75×**</td>
<td>**0.75×**</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>**Ropivacaine 0.75%**</td>
<td>2.50×</td>
<td>1.25×</td>
<td>1.00×</td>
<td>**0.75×**</td>
</tr>

**Three things to take from those six numbers:**
1. **Both reach the same trough.** 0.3 V, about a quarter below untouched skin, at 90 minutes. `FULLTEXT` Kopacz tested the two head to head in pig skin and got **−52 ± 11% and −54 ± 14%, and states the comparison explicitly: reduced to a similar degree at both concentrations, P = 0.4** — with Figure 1’s caption confirming no significant difference between the two concentrations. Two studies, two species, two routes, **no detectable difference in trough depth.** One qualification that must travel with this: on Kopacz’s *other* analysis — the 30-minute trajectory rather than the maximum — only 0.75% reached significance against the uninjected control, with 0.25% at P = 0.6 (4.3a). **The maximum troughs are indistinguishable; the sustained averages are not equally well established.**
2. **What differs in the measurement is timing — but the mechanism that was supposed to explain it has been retracted.** In Cederholm’s medians, 0.25% is at or below untouched skin from 40 minutes while 0.75% is not until 60–90; across the 90 minutes anybody has measured, **0.75% spends most of the window above untouched skin and 0.25% spends most of it at or below.** That is what the data show and it stands.
\t**Two separate caveats, and both matter:**
\t- These are medians transcribed from figure captions in a 12-subject group, and the difference between 0.3 V and 0.4 V is 25% — real, but close to the 11.8–20% reproducibility floor for the method (4.1a.3b). **Do not present the timing difference as precisely established.**
\t- **The explanation this page used to give for it is gone.** The account was that a stronger solution takes longer to descend into the constricting window. `FULLTEXT` Kimi 2012 measured the descent at **eighteen-fold within 30 seconds** (4.5, 10.7), so concentration cannot be setting the arrival time. **The observed timing difference in Cederholm is now an unexplained finding rather than a predicted one** — which is a reason to lean on it less, not more.
3. **So if total reduced-flow time is what he cares about, the weaker solution is WORSE inside the recorded window, not better.** Past 90 minutes nobody has measured either one.
`INFERENCE` **Therefore the choice between 0.25% and 0.75% is not a vessel decision.** The vessel objection he was worried about does not separate them on depth, and separates them on timing in the direction opposite to his intuition. **It has to be argued on duration and supply instead.**
## 10.3 The economic premise also fails, for a different reason
His arithmetic — 0.25 × 3 = 0.75, so the vial lasts triple — assumes the binding constraint is milligrams. It is not.
### Block-minutes per milligram, within one study
`INFERENCE` From Cederholm 1994, all at 0.1 mL so the milligrams scale with concentration:

**On that dataset 0.75% is the most drug-efficient concentration tested.** Diluting does not stretch the supply in block-hours; it shrinks it slightly, and 1% is catastrophically inefficient.
`INFERENCE` From Moffitt 2001, all at 0.5 mL, the opposite:

**These two cannot be reconciled from what is held.** Endpoints differ (end of complete block versus return of full sensation), volumes differ fivefold, and Moffitt’s strong-solution figures are censored lower bounds so their true per-milligram efficiency is higher than shown. Per-milligram efficiency is only comparable within a study and the two studies point opposite ways. **Record as unresolved.**
### The real constraint is the ampoule
`INFERENCE` The smallest unit on the Mexican market is a **10 mL ampoule at 7.5 mg/mL — 75 mg, single-dose, preservative-free.** A twenty-wheal session at 0.1 mL per site uses 2 mL, which is 15 mg at 0.75%.
**So one ampoule already holds five sessions’ worth of drug at full strength. The reason it does not last five sessions is that it cannot be kept once opened.**
**Diluting changes the milligrams per session and changes nothing about the number of ampoules consumed**, unless the remainder can be subdivided and stored sterile. **That is a compounding question for his prescriber or pharmacist, not a pharmacology question, and it is the single most useful thing to tell him about the economics.** If the remainder cannot be kept, the supply lasts exactly as long at 0.25% as at 0.75%, and he would be paying for the dilution in duration and in earlier narrowing **for no gain at all.**
`INFERENCE` **The conditional is the whole answer: if a single ampoule can be safely subdivided and stored across sessions, diluting triples the supply. If it cannot, diluting buys nothing.** Establish that first. Everything else about 0.25% is downstream of it.
## 10.4 What genuinely does argue for 0.25%
Six things, and they are better arguments than the two he gave.
1. **His endpoint is not pinprick, and the gap between the two endpoints is now quantified.** `INFERENCE` (5.6) He measures neuropathic relief, not abolition of normal sensation. `ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Tanelian & MacIver measured a \\~44-fold separation in injured fibres — ED₅₀ 5.7 µg/mL to silence tonic Aδ/C discharge against more than 250 µg/mL to block evoked conduction — and Dong found a 500-fold separation in compressed DRG neurons.** Both with lidocaine, in animal preparations. `INFERENCE` **So Cederholm’s 30 minutes at 0.25% is a lower bound on a harder task than the one he is performing. This is the strongest argument on the list and it is no longer merely mechanistic.**
2. **Neuropathic tissue needs less drug and holds it longer.** `ABSTRACT-ONLY` (5.7) ten Hoope found the minimum local anaesthetic dose in diabetic rats was **0.9% against 1.4%** in controls, with higher intraneural drug at 60 minutes and a lower inhibitory concentration for sodium currents in neuropathic DRG neurons. Kroin found longer block in diabetic animals for every drug. Four independent animal studies agree on the direction.
3. **Toxicity scales with concentration in every model that has looked.** `ABSTRACT-ONLY` (6.4) This is the one thing the whole contested toxicity literature agrees on, and halving the concentration is a factor of two on it. It is now the *stronger* of the two toxicity arguments, because the ropivacaine-is-gentler ranking has turned out to be contested and probably backwards (6.1, 6.2).
4. **Motor block is what dilution costs first.** `ABSTRACT-ONLY` Christiansen 2019 found motor block duration fell from 8.5 to 4.0 hours with dilution while sensory block did not change. For this use, losing motor block is a benefit.
5. **The large clinical safety record for ropivacaine in skin is at concentrations FAR MORE DILUTE than 0.25% — and the figures are now sharper.** `ABSTRACT-ONLY` There are **three** Breuninger-group publications, not two, and their counts do **not** increase monotonically, so they are separate publications with **likely overlapping** clinical experience rather than clean successive cutoffs: 1999 *Dermatologic Surgery* **5,220 procedures in 3,270 patients**; 2000 *Hautarzt* **4,670 operations in 3,015 patients**; 2000 *Langenbeck’s Archives of Surgery* **5,020 operations in 3,270 patients**. The first two both report a maximum ropivacaine dose of **300 mg**.
\t`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **And the concentrations are much lower than “0.15%”.** A dermatologic-surgery formulation table attributed to this programme gives *total* anaesthetic concentrations of **0.30%, 0.15% and 0.08%** — but reconstructing the recipes gives **ropivacaine at approximately 0.10%, 0.05% and 0.03%**, with prilocaine supplying the balance and epinephrine around 1:1,000,000. This is secondary-source and not reverified in each primary paper.
\t`INFERENCE` **So the thousands of documented uses of ropivacaine in human skin sit at roughly 0.03% to 0.10% — two-and-a-half to eight times more dilute than 0.25%, and seven to twenty-five times more dilute than 0.75%. The 0.75% concentration has no comparable clinical record in skin at all;** it is the strength used for nerve blocks and epidurals. Moving to 0.25% moves *toward* the band with the safety record, and even 0.25% is above it. Caveat: tumescent solutions are large volumes of dilute drug into intact skin with epinephrine and prilocaine, which is a different exposure from a small undiluted wheal without either — adjacent evidence, not matched.
6. **Two-fold dilution made no difference to duration in the one large-animal comparison at very dilute concentrations.** `ABSTRACT-ONLY` In dogs, **0.05% against 0.1% ropivacaine at 15 mL/kg produced similar return of sensitivity at about 7 hours**, with the 0.1% plasma peak at 240 minutes (Abimussi 2014, Credie 2013). `INFERENCE` Tumescent-style volumes in a different species, and no wheal-site vascular endpoint — but it is a doubling of concentration with no duration gain, at concentrations far below 0.75%, and it runs against the steep concentration–duration slope seen in 0.1 mL human wheals (5.1). **Consistent with the general pattern that concentration matters most in small depots and least in large ones** (10.1).
## 10.5 What argues against, honestly
1. **The duration hit is real and fourfold on the closest-matched data.** 30 minutes against 120, Cederholm 1994, same volume, same needle.
2. **The narrowing arrives sooner.** 40 minutes against 60–90 on the only human intradermal absolute data. Trough depth is the same but the site spends more of the measured window below baseline.
3. **0.063% matched adrenaline 1:200,000 on flux** (4.4). The bottom of the ladder is not a safe place, and 0.25% is on the way there.
4. **pH is uncontrolled.** `INFERENCE` (9.3) Diluting a 0.75% ampoule with unbuffered normal saline changes the solution pH in a direction nobody has characterised for intradermal injection, and Moffitt’s own anomalous pain result was attributed to exactly this.
5. **Dilution requires opening and manipulating a preservative-free single-dose ampoule.** That is a sterility question, and it is the same question as the supply question in 10.3.
## 10.6 The balance, stated plainly
`INFERENCE` **0.25% is worth trying, and the reason is his endpoint, not his vessels.**
- The vessel objection does not separate 0.25% from 0.75% on depth, and where it does separate them — timing — it mildly favours 0.75%. **So vessels are not a reason to avoid 0.25%, and not a reason to prefer it either.** The question that occasioned this whole investigation turns out not to bear on the decision. That is a real finding, not a failure.
- Duration is the genuine cost and it may be four-fold on pinprick — but pinprick is not what he is treating, and neuropathic tissue is more sensitive and holds drug longer than any of the source studies’ healthy tissue.
- Toxicity argues for the lower concentration more strongly than the page previously allowed, because ropivacaine’s supposed gentleness is contested while the concentration-dependence is not.
- **The supply argument, which is what motivated the question, depends entirely on whether an opened ampoule can be stored. Settle that first.**
`INFERENCE` **The cheapest informative experiment is not a switch. It is a split.** Both legs, same session, **0.75% in one and 0.25% in the other**, same wheal count, size, syringe, needle and pattern — which is the design section 12 already specifies. It gives the duration comparison and the colour comparison at once, in his own tissue, with day-to-day variation removed, and it costs one session. Do that before committing the supply strategy to either concentration.
## 10.7 Three claims in this part that must be downgraded
> **An independent audit identified these as the places where this document is most confident and least supported. It was right. State them as hypotheses.**
<table header-row=\"true\">
<tr>
<td>Claim</td>
<td>Why it has to be downgraded</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>**“Every concentration below 1% narrows vessels below untouched skin”**</td>
<td>Untreated-referenced evidence exists for **three** concentrations only — 0.25% and 0.75% from Cederholm 1991’s absolute medians, and 0.063% through a two-step chain. Everything from 0.5% downward is measured **against saline**, and saline sits \\~3.9× above untouched skin. The per-timepoint figures and the ropivacaine-versus-untreated statistics live in Cederholm 1992, which is `PAYWALLED` and confirmed unobtainable after a second independent attempt. **The claim is a reasonable interpolation. It is not established.**</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>**“0.25% and 0.75% reach the same trough”**</td>
<td>Rests on Cederholm’s medians transcribed from **figure captions** in a 12-subject group, where the difference between 0.3 V and 0.4 V is 25% against a method reproducibility floor of 11.8–20% (4.1a.3b); plus Kopacz’s P = 0.4, which is a **maximum-change** comparison in pig subcutaneous tissue. Kopacz’s *other* analysis, the 30-minute trajectory against an uninjected control, gave **0.25% at P = 0.6 — not significant** (4.3a). **Supported, but not firmly.**</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>**Any claim about the time or depth of narrowing at 0.25% versus 0.75% outside the 20-to-90-minute human window**</td>
<td>**Not supportable in either direction.** Cederholm measured four timepoints from 20 to 90 minutes and nothing outside them exists for a wheal (13 item 5). The rat data in 4.5 put the tissue below the window by 60 minutes. **Anything said about the first 20 minutes or after 90 is extrapolation.**</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>**“Concentration changes timing, not depth”**</td>
<td>**This is the one to retract.** It was built on the assumption that a wheal descends into the constricting window over hours, so a stronger solution would take longer to get there. `FULLTEXT` Kimi 2012 measured an **eighteen-fold fall within 30 seconds** (4.5). If dilution is that fast, **concentration barely changes the timing at all** — every clinical concentration is in or near the window almost immediately. What concentration plausibly changes is **how long the site stays inside the window and how much total drug is there**, not when it arrives.</td>
</tr>
</table>
## 10.8 The question, reframed
`INFERENCE` **This section exists because the governing question in section 1 turns out to be the wrong question, and saying so is more useful than answering it.**
The question assumes nominal solution concentration is what the tissue experiences. It is not. `FULLTEXT` A 0.5% injection is 15.2 mM in the syringe and **0.85 mM in the tissue 30 seconds later** — and that 0.85 mM is itself a homogenate average over a specimen seven to ten times the injected volume, so the real gradient runs from something near the syringe concentration at the needle tip down to nothing a few millimetres away.
What actually determines the exposure, none of which appears in a percentage:
- **mass per wheal** — 0.75 mg at 0.75% against 0.25 mg at 0.25%, for 0.1 mL
- **volume per wheal**, which sets the depot geometry and the surface-to-volume ratio governing clearance
- **dermal versus subcutaneous placement**, which changes the tissue, the vasculature and every published comparator
- **number of wheals and total treated area**
- **spacing**, which determines whether adjacent depots overlap
- **pH and formulation** (9.3)
- **local perfusion**, which is itself altered by the drug
- **site history** — prior fibrosis, pigmentation, altered absorption from previous sessions
- **repeat interval and site rotation**
`INFERENCE` **A better question, and the one the evidence is actually shaped to answer:**
> **At a fixed target effect on his neuropathic pain, what combination of mass per wheal, volume, spacing, treated area, site history and repeat interval produces the least cumulative disturbance to the skin?**
**And the outcome variable needs reframing too.** “Does it constrict?” is a yes/no question about a continuous quantity. What matters biologically is closer to the **depth-duration integral of local hypoperfusion** — how far below normal, for how long, over how much tissue — together with whether repeated exposure changes repair, innervation, pigmentation, immune response or absorption. A brief dip and a sustained low-flow state are not the same insult, and **neither pallor nor a single laser Doppler reading measures the integral.**
`INFERENCE` **What this means practically for him, and it is genuinely useful rather than a counsel of despair:** the variables — volume per site, number of sites, total area, interval between sessions, whether sites are rotated — are **unexamined, and several of them are probably more consequential than the concentration.** **The single most informative change to the protocol is not a different percentage; it is starting to record and vary those.**
`INFERENCE` **And the long-term safety question is broader than nerve fibre density.** Repeated wheals could alter collagen, microvasculature, pigmentation, immune cell populations, tissue compliance or absorption before any change in fibre count became detectable — and 6.7 now shows fibre count is a noisy measure anyway. Four causal exposures need separating and no study separates them: **drug identity and concentration; total local mass and dwell time; mechanical puncture and repeated-site trauma; and formulation, sterility and excipients.** `ABSTRACT-ONLY` The mesotherapy literature reports infection, nodules, granulomatous reactions, pigment change, scarring, fibrosis and vascular events after repeated intradermal injection of heterogeneous mixtures — mostly case reports, and it cannot separate puncture from injectate from technique from contamination. **That is the shape of the risk this page cannot quantify, and pigmentation is one component of it** (5.2: universal 2–3 mm pigmented spots at two weeks in every volunteer, resolving by four).
---
# PART VIII — PRACTICAL
## 11.1 What is on the Mexican market
`UNSOURCED` **Everything in this subsection is from registry aggregators and online pharmacy listings checked in September 2026 and has not been verified at a primary regulatory source.** Registry aggregators lag the market in both directions — a listed product may be discontinued and a new generic may not yet appear. **Confirm the strength on the box before diluting anything.**
**Only two of the four worldwide strengths are registered in Mexico: 2 mg/mL and 7.5 mg/mL.** There is no 5 mg/mL product and no 10 mg/mL product. So 0.5% is reachable only by dilution, and 0.75% is what will actually be bought.
