**Prices checked September 2026** at Farmacia La Paz, Vitau, Farmalisto, Sanorim and MercadoLibre: Ropiconest 7.5 mg/mL, 5 × 20 mL — 1,347 pesos at Farmacia La Paz, 1,372 at Vitau, against list prices of 1,789 and 1,879; 2,099 from a MercadoLibre seller. Apirovin 7.5 mg/mL, 5 × 20 mL — 789 pesos at Sanorim, marked down from 999, **out of stock.** Naropin 7.5 mg/mL, 5 × 20 mL — out of stock at Farmalisto, no price shown. Online pharmacy prices are not what a hospital or a physician’s supplier pays; stock status changes weekly; several listings require the prescription to be uploaded before shipping.
**Regulatory status.** Ropivacaine is **fracción IV** in Mexico — the classification is carried in the registro number itself, as in 153M98 SSA **IV**, under article 226 of the Ley General de Salud. A prescription is required, **the prescription may be refilled as many times as the prescriber writes on it, and the pharmacy does not retain it.** Not a controlled substance; no special prescription form. Not sold over the counter, and these are hospital presentations, so a pharmacy that does not normally carry anaesthetics may have to order it in.
**The pack size is the practical problem, not the price.** The smallest box is five ampoules of 20 mL, which at 7.5 mg/mL is **750 mg** of ropivacaine — about fifty times what a twenty-wheal session uses. **The 10 mL Ropiconest ampoule is the smallest unit sold and is the one to ask for:** at 7.5 mg/mL it holds 75 mg.
## 11.2 Dilution arithmetic, if dilution is ever wanted
`INFERENCE` From the 7.5 mg/mL product:
<table header-row=\"true\">
<tr>
<td>Target</td>
<td>Ratio</td>
<td>Worked example</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>0.5% (5 mg/mL)</td>
<td>2 parts drug : 1 part saline</td>
<td>10 mL into 5 mL saline = 15 mL at 5 mg/mL. Check: 10 mL × 7.5 = 75 mg, spread through 15 mL = 5 mg/mL.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>0.375% (3.75 mg/mL)</td>
<td>1 : 1</td>
<td>10 mL into 10 mL = 20 mL at 3.75 mg/mL</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>**0.25% (2.5 mg/mL)**</td>
<td>**1 : 2**</td>
<td>10 mL into 20 mL saline = 30 mL at 2.5 mg/mL. Check: 75 mg through 30 mL = 2.5 mg/mL.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>0.125%</td>
<td>1 : 5</td>
<td>10 mL into 50 mL = 60 mL at 1.25 mg/mL</td>
</tr>
</table>
`INFERENCE` Two cautions that are easy to miss. (a) **pH** — see 9.3; unbuffered saline is not pH-neutral with respect to the drug’s behaviour and Moffitt attributed an anomalous result to exactly this. (b) **Sterility** — the ampoules are single-dose and preservative-free; a diluted batch is short-lived for sterility reasons rather than chemical ones and should be made up close to when it is used. Whether a remainder can be stored at all is the question in 10.3 and it is for the prescriber or pharmacist.
`FULLTEXT` **A third caution, precipitation.** Section 2.3 of the US Naropin label, read in the label’s own words on 2026-09-19 (the rest of the label was not read here), says ropivacaine’s solubility is limited above pH 6, so it may precipitate if mixed with alkaline solutions, and that a solution showing particles or discolouration should not be given. `INFERENCE` So anything added to the syringe — saline or anything else — is worth holding to the light and checking for cloudiness or particles before injecting.
`FULLTEXT` **The pH of the Heel ampoules, checked on 2026-09-26.** The German product information for Traumeel S (March 2023) lists water for injection and sodium chloride as its only excipients, states no pH, and gives its incompatibilities as not applicable. `INFERENCE` So no Heel label found gives a figure, and the two pages in this record that call the Heel ampoules pH-neutral give no source for it. A neutral solution would sit above the pH 6 at which the Naropin label says ropivacaine’s solubility becomes limited, which makes a Heel-and-ropivacaine mixture an untested one to check against the light, not one expected to fail.
`FULLTEXT` The US Traumeel Injection Solution label, read 2026-09-26, lists water and sodium chloride as its inactive ingredients, states no pH, says nothing about blood vessels, and says it “may be mixed with lidocaine or similar agents at the discretion of the physician.” `INFERENCE` Whether a Heel ampoule changes ropivacaine’s narrowing of skin vessels has never been measured; the searches behind that are recorded in <mention-page url=\"https://app.notion.com/p/3e010b7903aa81e2887ac0bd2e6b39ce\"/>.
## 11.3 Storage
`INFERENCE` Ropivacaine solutions are stored at ordinary room temperature and contain no epinephrine. That removes two things a lidocaine-with-epinephrine mixture needs: the acid required to keep epinephrine stable on the shelf, and **sodium metabisulfite**, the antioxidant added to stop epinephrine oxidising. It also removes the light-, heat- and pH-driven breakdown that limits a mixed lidocaine-with-epinephrine solution to roughly a week refrigerated. **The constraint on a ropivacaine batch is sterility, not chemistry** — which is simpler, and is one of ropivacaine’s real practical advantages.
## 11.4 Syringe, needle, technique
`INFERENCE` **Measure the volume on a syringe that can read it.** A U-100 insulin syringe, where one insulin unit is 0.01 mL, or a 1 mL tuberculin syringe graduated in 0.01 mL. A 3 mL syringe has 0.1 mL divisions, so 0.05 mL falls between two marks and cannot be set on it.
`FULLTEXT` **Needle: 30 G.** This is not arbitrary. Cederholm 1991 Group II compared 20, 23 and 30 G: all raised flow, **the rise was larger with the bigger needles**, and they recommended 30 G both for that and because it is technically easier to place 0.1 mL precisely intradermally — 20 and 23 G being “technically more difficult to use for this purpose.” Cederholm 1994 used 30 G; Newton 2000 used 30 G; Newton 2004 and 2005 and Moffitt used 27 G; Fruhstorfer used 26 G.
`FULLTEXT` **Injection speed.** Cederholm 1991 used **0.1 mL per 10 seconds**; Cederholm 1994 used a standardised 10-second injection time; Moffitt used 0.5 mL over 5 seconds. `INFERENCE` A 10-second injection for 0.1 mL is slow by clinical habit and is what the matched studies did.
`INFERENCE` **Placement.** Bevel up, needle almost flat against the skin, tip just under the surface. A correctly placed injection meets real resistance and raises a pale, firm wheal with the pores standing out like the skin of an orange — the sign the drug went into the **dermis** rather than the fat below. Note `FULLTEXT` that Fruhstorfer aimed deliberately “as shallow as possible, but deep enough to avoid the development of a pale papule,” which is the same target described from the other side.
`FULLTEXT` **Spacing.** Cederholm 1991 used a **minimum 3 cm** separation between sites and confirmed that neither needle insertion nor 0.1 mL of fluid influenced flow at remote or nearby test sites. Newton 2000 used 34 mm circles; Newton 2004 and 2005 used 3 cm and state that pilot experiments confirmed 3 cm was sufficient to prevent responses overlapping. **Cederholm 1994 used at least 50 mm between different solutions**, because they were comparing drugs and needed no analgesic cross-talk. `INFERENCE` **So: 3 cm is enough to keep the vascular responses separate; 5 cm is what to use if two different solutions are being compared in the same session** — which matters directly for the split-leg test in section 12 and the 0.25%/0.75% comparison in 10.6. Note also that the flare from a puncture spreads **up to several centimetres** (Fruhstorfer), so 3 cm is the floor, not a comfortable margin.
`INFERENCE` **Room temperature matters.** Every skin flowmetry study in this document controlled it: Cederholm 1991 at 21–22 °C with the subject supine under a blanket to minimise heat loss, varying no more than 0.2 °C; Newton 2000 at 23 ± 1 °C; Newton 2004 and 2005 at 22 °C; Fruhstorfer at room temperature with no figure given. Resting skin flow at 22 °C is low *because* thermoregulatory vasoconstriction is active (4.3). A warm room, a hot shower beforehand, or a cold room would all change the baseline he is comparing against — **which is a reason to keep the conditions of his own sessions as constant as he can if he wants them to be comparable to each other.**
## 11.5 Injection pain
`FULLTEXT` **Moffitt 2001.** Lidocaine 2% with epinephrine 1:80,000 was significantly more painful than control (P = .0002) and than 7.5 mg/mL ropivacaine (P = .0005). **Ropivacaine’s own injection pain rose with concentration**, but only 5 mg/mL reached significance against saline (P = .002) — and 7.5 mg/mL versus saline was P = .06, i.e. not significant. The authors flag their own 5 mg/mL result as odd and attribute it to the solution having been made by diluting 10 mg/mL with **unbuffered** normal saline, possibly altering the pH.
`INFERENCE` **The expected direction is that ropivacaine is more comfortable than lidocaine-with-epinephrine**, because the sting of a lidocaine-with-epinephrine solution comes from its acidity and from the metabisulfite, and ropivacaine has neither. But **do not tell the human that a diluted solution will sting less than the concentrate.** Moffitt’s own data says the opposite happened when they diluted with unbuffered saline, and they say so.
---
# PART IX — TESTING PROTOCOL
`INFERENCE` The split-leg design is what makes any of this readable, because day-to-day variation in neuropathic pain is larger than the effect being measured. Comparing a session today against a session last week confounds the drug with sleep, weather, activity and the natural fluctuation of the condition. Injecting both on one day removes all of those at once and leaves the drug as the only difference between the two legs.
## 12.1 The design
1. **Rate each leg separately before injecting.** Two numbers, not one.
2. **Inject one leg with one solution and the other with the comparator**, using the same wheal count, wheal size, syringe, needle and pattern on both. `INFERENCE` **Keep sites at least 5 cm apart when two solutions are being compared** (11.4). Record which leg got which.
3. **Rate each leg separately every hour while awake.** Record the clock time of the last hourly rating at which the leg was still numb and the first at which it was not, rather than a single estimate afterwards.
4. **An hourly schedule cannot cover a block this long.** Inject in the evening, rate hourly until sleep, **record the time on waking and whether the leg is still numb**, then resume hourly. A block still running at breakfast has established the thing that matters most.
5. **Repeat two or three times before drawing a conclusion, swapping which leg gets which solution**, since the two legs may not be equally affected to begin with.
## 12.2 The comparison worth running first
`INFERENCE` Per 10.6, the highest-value split is **0.75% against 0.25%**. It answers the live question, in his tissue, in one session.
## 12.3 Readings to collect
**Five readings, and three of them are new to the protocol.**
1. **Duration of neuropathic relief.** The primary endpoint and his own. `INFERENCE` Not the same as pinprick duration; see 5.6.
2. **Duration of numbness.** Separate from (1). Record both, because the gap between them is itself informative and is the thing 5.6 predicts will be large.
3. **The tail.** In the hours after numbness returns, is the treated leg worse than its own baseline? `INFERENCE` Nothing in ropivacaine’s pharmacology predicts a worse tail — it activates no adrenaline receptor (`FULLTEXT` Nakamura showed phentolamine and yohimbine do not touch its vascular effect) and leaves nothing behind that would make a nerve ending easier to fire. **A worse tail would be an unexplained finding and a reason to stop and ask, not to adjust the technique.**
4. **Whether the relief outlasts the block.** `INFERENCE` This is the reading a one-hour block could not produce and it is the more interesting question. A short block tells you almost nothing about whether interrupting the pain signal for long enough changes anything after it wears off. Rate the treated leg for **two or three days** after a session, not only until sensation returns.
5. **Colour, with the timing recorded and the limits understood.** `INFERENCE` Score nil / pink / pale following Cederholm’s own scheme, **and record that both can occur at once as a central red zone with a blanched ring** (5.2). Note the clock time pallor first appears and when it fades. **But: per 4.6, colour does not measure flux and cannot answer the vasoconstriction question in either direction.** It is worth collecting because it is free and because it indexes the postcapillary bed, not because it settles anything.
## 12.3a Protocol design from the closest existing intervention
One wheal protocol with a local anaesthetic and a pain outcome has been published: 1 mL of 2% lidocaine diluted in 5 mL of saline, 0.2–0.3 mL wheals 1 cm apart, three sessions two weeks apart, with pain lower at 2 and 4 weeks and at 3 months in 20 people with notalgia paresthetica, uncontrolled, and none in small fibre neuropathy (Mülkoğlu 2020, PMID 32416719). `INFERENCE` **The nearest randomised design is intradermal botulinum toxin A for painful diabetic neuropathy**, which is delivered as a grid of small intradermal injections and has randomised trials behind it. The pharmacology does not transfer at all; **the grid design does.**
`AUDIT-READ` **Ghasemi 2014**: 40 patients with painful diabetic neuropathy randomised to intradermal botulinum toxin A or saline. **Twelve approximately equally spaced sites arranged in a 3 × 4 grid over the dorsum of one foot**, total dose 100 U at roughly 8–10 U per site, delivered with an **insulin syringe**. At 3 weeks, **6 of 20 in the toxin arm and 0 of 20 on saline reported no pain on the visual analogue scale (P = 0.01)**, with improvement in every Neuropathic Pain Scale domain except cold sensation. The paper does **not** report dilution volume per site, needle gauge, or measured dermal depth.
`ABSTRACT-ONLY` Two further entry points: Yuan 2009, an 18-person randomised crossover in diabetic neuropathy; and Xiao 2010, a post-herpetic neuralgia trial using **subcutaneous** botulinum toxin A at 5 U/mL against 0.5% lidocaine and saline.
`INFERENCE` **What transfers:** a twelve-site grid on one foot dorsum as a documented, tolerated, effective pattern; the insulin syringe, which matches 11.4; one limb treated with the other as control, which matches 12.1; and a three-week follow-up interval. **What does not:** the drug, the mechanism, the dose, the duration, and the fact that botulinum’s effect builds over weeks while a wheal’s is over in hours.
## 12.4 One addition that costs nothing
`INFERENCE`
- **Record the room temperature and whether he showered beforehand.** Every source study controlled this to within a degree (11.4). If his sessions are to be comparable to each other, the baseline has to be.
## 12.5 What the protocol cannot do
`INFERENCE` It cannot measure flux, so it cannot answer the vasoconstriction question. Nothing he can do at home can, because the instrument required is a laser Doppler flowmeter and the comparison required is against an untouched control site scanned in the same session. **The honest statement to him: his skin can tell him about duration and about colour, and it cannot tell him about blood flow.** That is a limit of the method, not a gap in his diligence.
---
# PART X — WHAT IS UNKNOWN
## 13. The open gaps
Ordered by how much each gap bears on his decisions. **Each has been through an independent gap audit; where that audit found evidence, the item says so and the gap is narrowed rather than deleted.**
**The two most important additions are new items 14 and 15**, which are gaps in this document’s own framing rather than in the literature.
1. **Nobody has measured neuropathic pain relief from an intradermal ropivacaine wheal at any concentration — but the gap is a PARTIAL one and this page previously stated it too absolutely.** `INFERENCE` Every duration figure in this document uses pinprick, return of full sensation, or time to analgesic request, in healthy volunteers or surgical patients with intact nerves.
\t**What independent search did find, none of it matched to the intervention:**
\t- `ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Amjad & Mashhood 2005**: a randomised post-herpetic neuralgia trial giving **three local infiltrations at two-week intervals**. Lidocaine alone produced complete relief in **16.6% at 6 weeks and 6.6% at 12 weeks**; triamcinolone plus lidocaine gave **63.3% and 83.3%.** Route depth, volume per site and concentration are not in the abstract. **A local-infiltration hit for neuropathic pain — but the steroid arm carries most of the effect and the procedural reporting is incomplete.**
\t- `ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Cui 2016** and similar acute-zoster trials use intradermal lidocaine as an active control or co-intervention, always alongside valaciclovir, methylene blue, corticosteroids, methylcobalamin or nerve blocks. **None isolates the anaesthetic effect.**
\t- `ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Ferguson 2021**: subcutaneous lidocaine **infusion** in palliative care — systemic delivery, not a local wheal. In an 18-infusion retrospective series, patients considered **13 of 18 infusions effective**, with varying dose and duration.
\t`INFERENCE` **So local anaesthetic infiltration for neuropathic pain has been tried and has some evidence behind it. What has been reported only once is an anaesthetic-only wheal protocol with per-wheal volume, spacing, a repeated-session schedule and a pain outcome: 1 mL of 2% lidocaine in 5 mL of saline, 0.2–0.3 mL wheals 1 cm apart, three sessions two weeks apart, pain lower at 2 and 4 weeks and at 3 months, in 20 people with notalgia paresthetica, uncontrolled (Mülkoğlu 2020, PMID 32416719) — and nothing at all in small fibre neuropathy.** Revised statement: **the intervention class is not novel; this specific protocol is unstudied.** That is a weaker and more accurate claim than the one this page carried, and it is mildly reassuring rather than the reverse. **His split-leg test remains the only route to a number for his legs.**
2. **Nobody has used ropivacaine wheals for small fibre neuropathy.** No trial exists. Skin that has lost fibres may take up, hold and clear a drug differently, and the fibres being blocked are damaged ones. What would settle it: a trial of intradermal ropivacaine in neuropathy patients measuring both block duration and pain relief.
3. **Local tissue concentration over time at a wheal in human skin has never been MEASURED — but it has been measured in rat oral mucosa (Kimi 2012, Yamashiro 2016; 4.5) and calculated, and both support the mechanism.** `FULLTEXT` Tokinaga 2007 states the measurement has never been made, which was true of the vascular literature when written and is no longer true in general (4.5). `FULLTEXT` Sohn 2012 computed the expected free tissue concentration from protein binding and got **5.46 × 10⁻⁴ to 1.63 × 10⁻³ M for clinical ropivacaine — inside the constricting window** (4.5). **So this gap is narrower than the previous version of this page claimed, but it is not closed:** Sohn’s figure is the unbound fraction at the moment of injection, accounting for protein binding and nothing else — no diffusion, no interstitial dilution, no clearance, no time course. **What is still missing is the time course in human dermis**, which is what would settle how long a wheal stays inside the window — minutes, on the rat measurement (4.5) — and therefore how long 0.25% and 0.75% each keep a site there.
4. **Flux and block duration have never been measured in the same wheal.** Both Kopacz and Newton say why: pinprick disturbs the flow being measured. **Narrowed by three partial hits — Yamashiro 2016 (concentration + flow, rat oral mucosa), Bernards & Kopacz 1999 (concentration + flow + block, human perineural, 5 h), and Liu 1995 (duration + flow at matched sites, human, 6 h). See 4.11 item 3.** What is still missing is the chain at a skin wheal, and with a neuropathic-pain endpoint rather than pinprick.
5. **Skin blood flow at a ropivacaine wheal has never been recorded past 90 minutes — confirmed by independent search.** Cederholm’s recordings stop there. `ABSTRACT-ONLY` Häfner 2008 went to 24 hours but at a fingerpad after a digital block. The nearest misses found on re-search: **Liu 1995** followed flow at matched subcutaneous sites for **6 hours**, but lidocaine and bupivacaine flow had returned to baseline by 30 minutes; **Bernards & Kopacz 1999** followed perineural flow for **5 hours**; `ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Thiem 2021** used hyperspectral imaging for **120 minutes** after subcutaneous 4% articaine.
6. **0.5% and 0.375% against untreated skin over time — the single most valuable missing document.** It lives in Cederholm 1992’s figures, `PAYWALLED` and now confirmed unobtainable after a **second independent attempt** covering the BMJ/RAPM archive, Scite full-text retrieval, the Internet Archive issue record, the Linköping DiVA dissertation record, SciSpace, ResearchGate and institutional repositories. The DiVA record states no full text exists. The thesis ladder places 0.5% between 0.75% and 0.063% and no closer. `INFERENCE` **Three of this document’s claims are waiting on this one paper** (10.7). It was quoted as orderable for about US\\$71.
7. **Whether the ester metabolite interference applies to procaine and ropivacaine.** `INFERENCE` (9.2) Corke 1984 established it for chloroprocaine’s metabolite and bupivacaine. The procaine/ropivacaine version has never been tested, in any tissue.
8. **Whether repeated ropivacaine over months does anything to skin that has already lost fibres.** Unstudied, exactly as for repeated epinephrine. `ABSTRACT-ONLY` Wehrfritz 2011 is the nearest human measurement and it went the wrong way, with topical lidocaine over 42 days. The only objective measure is intraepidermal nerve fibre density from a punch biopsy, whose within-person year-to-year variation is large enough to swamp a real change of the size at issue. What would settle it: IENFD at a treated and an untreated site in the same person, before starting and after a year.
9. **Whether ropivacaine shares the unmyelinated-Schwann-cell selectivity.** `ABSTRACT-ONLY` (6.5) Kalichman 1989 found unmyelinated fibres’ Schwann cells lyse while myelinated fibres’ merely accumulate lipid — but tested chloroprocaine, procaine, etidocaine and lidocaine, **not ropivacaine.** Unmyelinated fibres are the ones being treated.
10. **Which way the toxicity ranking actually goes.** `ABSTRACT-ONLY` (6.1) Four studies each way and a review saying there is no consensus. The lipophilicity account (6.2) predicts ropivacaine is worse than lidocaine locally, which is the direction four of the eight support.
11. **Why ropivacaine relaxes vessels above the constricting window — LARGELY CLOSED.** `FULLTEXT` Tokinaga 2007 called it difficult to explain. Ok 2013 has since shown the drug activates two brakes on its own contraction — endothelial NO via eNOS–GC–cGMP, and voltage-dependent potassium channels hyperpolarising the smooth muscle — either of which can outrun the calcium influx driving the contraction (4.10a). **What remains open is which brake dominates in dermal microvessels**, since all of this is rat aorta, and whether the balance differs in skin with impaired endothelial function.
12. **The discrepancy between Cederholm’s own two accounts** of whether 0.75% reaches the untreated level (4.4).
13. **Whether Guinard’s 285% and 260% are rises to or by.** `PAYWALLED`. Settled to “by” at high confidence by three converging lines (4.2), **but an independent audit examined the same abstract and declined to resolve it**, holding that the abstract’s wording alone cannot adjudicate the normalisation convention. Record it as a high-confidence inference under dispute. **And see the cautionary pair in section 14: Ghali reports flow AS a percentage of baseline while Guinard reports the INCREASE as a percentage.**
14. **Nothing has ever been measured in neuropathic human skin after an injected local anaesthetic.** `INFERENCE` Confirmed by independent search. The LDIflare literature establishes that the **axon-reflex flare is blunted to roughly a third of normal** in diabetic neuropathy (4.7) — but **no study injects a local anaesthetic into neuropathic skin and measures the local vascular response against untreated, needle-only and saline controls.** **This is the most directly relevant missing experiment for him, and every vascular number in Part I comes from healthy tissue.**
15. **The exposure variables are unexamined — a gap in the question, not the literature.** `INFERENCE` See 10.8. Volume per wheal, number of wheals, total treated area, spacing, session interval and site rotation are all under his direct control, all plausibly more consequential than the concentration difference he has been weighing. No study varies them. **A document about the right percentage was answering the easiest of the eight relevant questions.**
16. **The causal chain has never been observed end to end in the target tissue.** `INFERENCE` The chain runs: nominal concentration → local concentration over time → vascular response → clearance → duration of neuropathic relief → cumulative tissue effect. **Every link is now supported by something, and no two adjacent links have been measured in the same human dermal preparation.** It is a model assembled from a rat palate, a rabbit cornea, a pig flank, a human forearm and a human peroneal nerve. **Say so when presenting it.**
---
# PART XI — FIGURES IN CIRCULATION THAT THIS DOCUMENT REJECTS OR DOWNGRADES
## 14. The rejected-claims table
Kept as a section because these claims are in the human’s journal, in earlier versions of this page, or in the general literature, and an AI reading this page needs to recognise them and not repeat them.
<table header-row=\"true\">
<tr>
<td>Claim</td>
<td>Status</td>
<td>What is true instead</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Doppler showed increased blood flow past 0.5%” (his journal; he has since corrected it)</td>
<td>**Wrong**</td>
<td>At 1% flux **matched** saline. That is a null result on top of the puncture, not measured widening. Across Cederholm 1992’s tested set the effect runs from narrowing at the weak end to nothing at the strong end. Never to widening.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“0.75% ropivacaine causes vasodilation”</td>
<td>**Wrong**</td>
<td>Narrows in six preparations (10.1). The only dilation is in fingers after digital blocks.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Ropivacaine’s low neurotoxicity ranking is unchallenged”</td>
<td>**Wrong**</td>
<td>Four studies put it above lidocaine, four below, one review says no consensus (6.1).</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Ropivacaine is a strong vasoconstrictor”</td>
<td>**Downgraded**</td>
<td>Indistinguishable from lidocaine in human arteries; 16% of epinephrine’s effect in dog artery at clinical concentration; insufficient to reduce surgical bleeding (4.9).</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Vessel narrowing is why ropivacaine outlasts lidocaine”</td>
<td>**Downgraded**</td>
<td>Covino’s position, endorsed by Fruhstorfer, is that vasoconstrictive and anaesthetic potency are unrelated. Protein binding at 94% and possibly the S-enantiomer’s steric properties are the better candidates (5.8).</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Being a pure S-enantiomer reduces local toxicity”</td>
<td>**Wrong**</td>
<td>Werdehausen 2012 found no stereoisomerism effect on local cytotoxicity, while granting the systemic advantage (6.3).</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Moffitt’s durations are extrapolated from the trend”</td>
<td>**Wrong**</td>
<td>They are right-censored at the 960-minute study limit, with 1 of the 5 mg/mL and 4 of the 7.5 mg/mL sites still numb. Lower bounds, which is stronger (5.3).</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Moffitt used 0.1 mL wheals”</td>
<td>**Wrong**</td>
<td>**0.5 mL**, five times a wheal, 27 G (5.3).</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“0.75% was never tested by flowmetry in skin”</td>
<td>**Wrong**</td>
<td>Cederholm 1991 tested it intradermally in 12 people; Kopacz 1989 subcutaneously in pigs. Both found narrowing (4.3).</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Nobody has recorded ropivacaine skin flux past 90 minutes”</td>
<td>**Wrong as stated**</td>
<td>Häfner 2008 went to 24 hours, at a fingerpad after an Oberst block. Narrow the claim to “at a wheal” (4.11).</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Schnabl’s recording ran to 60 minutes”</td>
<td>**WRONG**</td>
<td>Confirmed wrong by independent audit. The abstract gives no recording schedule, and **a later full-text review by the same research group describes perfusion observations through 24 hours, including 6-hour and 24-hour values.** The original paper could not be obtained, so the exact timepoint list is **secondary-source verified, not primary-methods verified.** Delete the 60-minute claim; say “beyond 60 minutes, apparently 24 hours.”</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Epinephrine at 1:400,000 narrows skin vessels for thirty minutes to two hours”</td>
<td>`UNSOURCED`</td>
<td>Replace with: in skin, epinephrine reaches maximal constriction by 10 minutes alone or \\~20 minutes combined, suppresses the flare within 2 minutes, and shows **no dose-response between 1:800,000 and 1:200,000** (Newton 2004). Larrabee 1987 disagrees but is subcutaneous.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Cederholm 1992 had epinephrine drop flow almost to untreated level”</td>
<td>**Correct, keep**</td>
<td>And note what it means: epinephrine alone stayed slightly **above** untreated, because an injected site carries the needle’s own rise too. It cancelled the flare, not the injury, and not below baseline.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“A 207% rise in forearm skin blood flow after 1% lidocaine”</td>
<td>**TRACED AND CORRECTED**</td>
<td>`ABSTRACT-ONLY` The source is **Ghali 2008**. Five volunteers, **0.5 mL subdermal** forearm injections of phosphate-buffered saline against 1% lidocaine; first-five-minute maxima were **133% and 207% of normal blood flow** respectively. **So 207% means “to 207% of baseline”, i.e. an increase of 107% — not an increase BY 207%.** Flow after lidocaine remained elevated beyond 40 minutes. Route and volume differ from a wheal (subdermal, 0.5 mL). `FULLTEXT` The matched intradermal figure remains Cederholm 1991’s: lidocaine 1% took median flux to 2.7 V against a 0.4 V untreated control, the strongest dilator in the table.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>**“to” versus “by” — a cautionary pair**</td>
<td>**Both conventions are in use in this literature**</td>
<td>`ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Ghali 2008 reports flow AS A PERCENTAGE OF baseline** (207% = +107%). `ABSTRACT-ONLY` **Guinard 1992 reports the INCREASE as a percentage** (285% = a rise to 3.85× baseline). Two papers, two conventions, similar-looking numbers, factor-of-two-plus difference in meaning. **Never carry a bare percentage from this literature without checking which convention its paper used.** An independent audit of this page declined to resolve Guinard’s convention from the abstract alone, so the reading in 4.2 remains a high-confidence inference rather than a verified fact.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“The thesis says 0.063% was almost as low as untreated skin”</td>
<td>**Wrong**</td>
<td>Aggregator splice. The printed abstract stops at “similar to adrenaline-injection (5 µg/mL).” The untreated comparison is a two-step chain across two documents (4.4).</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Per-milligram, dilution stretches the supply”</td>
<td>**Unresolved**</td>
<td>Cederholm 1994 says 0.75% is the most drug-efficient concentration tested; Moffitt says the opposite. Different endpoints, fivefold volume difference, censored figures (10.3).</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Diluting with saline adds some vasodilation back”</td>
<td>**Wrong**</td>
<td>Saline is inert on flux; the needle does the dilating. Three independent confirmations (4.2).</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Ropivacaine causes vasoconstriction below 0.5% and vasodilation above 1%”</td>
<td>**Citation drift — reject the second half**</td>
<td>This appears verbatim in a 2022 review citing Cederholm 1992. **Cederholm 1992’s abstract says 1% ropivacaine “provoked an increase in skin blood flow SIMILAR TO SALINE”** — matching the needle’s own response, not exceeding it, and 1% was the highest concentration tested so nothing was measured “above 1%” at all. `INFERENCE` **This is the likely origin of the human’s own belief that 0.75% dilates** (10.1), and it is worth telling him where it came from: a real finding, restated once with a small distortion, then propagated.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Ropivacaine and lidocaine share one constricting window and differ only in where their clinical concentrations fall in it”</td>
<td>**Wrong — this page said it and it is now falsified**</td>
<td>Sohn 2012 measured the peaks separately: ropivacaine 3 × 10⁻⁴ M, lidocaine 3 × 10⁻³ M, a tenfold separation tracking lipid solubility. And the corrected model **predicts the wrong direction** for the skin contrast (4.9a). The honest position is that the ropivacaine-versus-lidocaine difference in skin is unexplained.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“A diluted solution will sting less”</td>
<td>**Wrong, or at least unsupported**</td>
<td>Moffitt’s 5 mg/mL, made by diluting with unbuffered saline, was the **only** concentration significantly more painful than control (11.5).</td>
</tr>
</table>
