# PART XII — SOURCES
Each entry gives what it contributes, what limits it, and **whether it has actually been read.** Where a **Standing** clause appears it records how the paper has been cited since — citing publications, classified supporting or contrasting statements, and whether any retraction, correction or expression of concern is attached. **A finding with no contrasting citations is not thereby correct; it may simply never have been retested.** The clause distinguishes the two.
**Access legend:** `FULL TEXT HELD` = the PDF is in the project. `ABSTRACT` = published abstract read. `PAYWALLED` = could not be obtained at all.
## 15.1 Vessels in skin — the core
**Cederholm I, Evers H, Löfström JB.** Effect of intradermal injection of saline or a local anaesthetic agent on skin blood flow — a methodological study in man. *Acta Anaesthesiologica Scandinavica* 1991;35(3):208–215. PMID 2038927. DOI `10.1111/j.1399-6576.1991.tb03275.x`. — **`FULL TEXT HELD`****. The single most important paper in the project.** Contributes: the only human intradermal dataset with ropivacaine and an untreated control on one absolute scale; the median flux table; NaCl vs Needle not significant; saline volume 0.05–0.5 mL making no difference; needle gauge effect; pallor with raised flow in Group I; flowmotion waves absent after ropivacaine; and the methodological rationale for saline referencing. Limits: medians read off figure captions, not a numerical table; **ropivacaine is never statistically tested against the untreated control**; 12 subjects in Group III; volar forearm, not leg; healthy young males.
**Cederholm I, Evers H, Löfström JB.** Skin blood flow after intradermal injection of ropivacaine in various concentrations with and without epinephrine evaluated by laser Doppler flowmetry. *Regional Anesthesia* 1992;17(6):322–328. PMID 1286053. DOI `10.1136/rapm-00115550-199217060-00003`. — **`PAYWALLED`****. Could not be obtained.** The journal now runs as *Regional Anesthesia and Pain Medicine* (BMJ), which holds the archive. Contributes, from the abstract only: 23 non-smoking healthy young male volunteers, four sites per forearm, randomised double-blind, 0.1 mL through 30 G, recordings at 20/40/60/90 min, **controls saline + 1% lidocaine + an untreated area.** Series 1 at 1, 0.5, 0.375, 0.125, 0.063%: 1% raised flow like saline; 0.5% and weaker reduced flow **compared with saline**, more pronounced at 0.125 and 0.063%. Series 2 with epinephrine 1:200,000 and ropivacaine 1, 0.5, 0.25%: epinephrine alone gave a flow almost as low as the untreated control sites; ropivacaine-plus-epinephrine read lower than saline but significantly higher than epinephrine alone at 20 min; the combination diminished rather than accentuated epinephrine’s effect. **Every ropivacaine comparison in the abstract is against saline. The untreated area is invoked once, for epinephrine alone. No figure, table or per-timepoint value from this paper has ever been read.** 0.75% was not tested. **Also unavailable and worth naming: the units and calibration details — so it is not known whether the 1992 figures are on the same 0–10 V scale as 1991, and therefore whether the two papers’ numbers can be compared at all.** Standing: 6 citing publications, **no classified citation statements of any kind** — no supporting, no contrasting, no editorial notices. The dose-response has been neither challenged nor repeated since 1992.
**Guinard JP, Carpenter RL, Morell RC.** Effect of local anesthetic concentration on capillary blood flow in human skin. *Regional Anesthesia* 1992;17(6):317–321. PMID 1286052. DOI `10.1136/rapm-00115550-199217060-00002`. — **`PAYWALLED`****.** Same issue as Cederholm 1992, five pages earlier. Contributes from the abstract: the needle-stick and saline magnitudes (260 ± 121% and 285 ± 237%); lidocaine 277–511%, mepivacaine 124–316%, bupivacaine 242–725%; and the finding that **clinically useful mepivacaine does not raise flow above saline while lower concentrations blunt the rise.** Limits: subcutaneous abdomen, 8 volunteers, 0.2 mL, no ropivacaine, 60-minute recording. **The “to versus by” reading is settled at high confidence but by inference (4.2), not by reading the methods.**
**Cederholm I, Åkerman B, Evers H.** Local analgesic and vascular effects of intradermal ropivacaine and bupivacaine in various concentrations with and without addition of adrenaline in man. *Acta Anaesthesiologica Scandinavica* 1994;38(4):322–327. PMID 8067217. DOI `10.1111/j.1399-6576.1994.tb03901.x`. — **`FULL TEXT HELD`****.** Contributes: the per-concentration duration table; the per-timepoint blanching and redness tables; the central-red-with-blanched-ring observation; the 1% duration anomaly with its vasodilation explanation; haematoma rate; the universal 2–3 mm pigmentation at two weeks resolving by four; pH of every solution; and the intradermal-lipid explanation for outlasting bupivacaine. Limits: 10 volunteers; colour by visual inspection, not instrument; volar forearm; healthy young males aged 20–30. Standing: 76 citing publications, 45 classified statements — 4 supporting, 1 contrasting, no editorial notices. **All four supporting statements are for the vascular finding**; the single contrasting statement concerns the absence of tissue-necrosis complications during wound infusion and bears on neither the duration nor the blanching result.
**Cederholm I.** Ropivacaine: an experimental and clinical study with special reference to analgesic, circulatory and antiinflammatory effects. *Acta Anaesthesiologica Scandinavica* 1995;39:711. Linköping University Medical Dissertations No. 407, 1994. **ISBN 91-7870-949-0** (this page previously carried 91-7871-949-0; the Linköping DiVA record gives 91-7870-949-0). ISSN 0345-0082. DOI `10.1111/j.1399-6576.1995.tb04154.x`. — **Access: the published one-page dissertation abstract is held in full; the dissertation itself is not.** Claims drawn from it are therefore tagged `ABSTRACT-ONLY` throughout the body, because the one page is itself a summary. Contributes: **the complete seven-concentration ladder**, pooling 1991 and 1992; the saline-referencing rationale in the author’s own words; the leukocyte chemiluminescence result; the epidural result; and the list of the five constituent papers. **Used to correct an aggregator splice** (4.4). Limits: it is a summary, gives no numbers, and its account of 0.75% is in tension with the 1991 figure captions.
**Kopacz DJ, Carpenter RL, Mackey DC.** Effect of ropivacaine on cutaneous capillary blood flow in pigs. *Anesthesiology* 1989;71(1):69–74. DOI `10.1097/00000542-198907000-00013`. PMID 2751142. — **`FULL TEXT HELD`****. Free at the publisher.** Contributes: absolute flow values with an uninjected control; the 0.25%-versus-0.75% head-to-head (−52 ± 11% and −54 ± 14%, **P = 0.4 between them**); bupivacaine going the other way; saline at only +32 ± 17%; epinephrine adding nothing to ropivacaine while deepening bupivacaine and saline; the normalisation formula; **and their own Bonferroni threshold of P \\< 0.003, which the 0.75%-versus-control P = 0.03 does not clear.** Coefficient of variation at the uninjected control over 30 min: 11.8%, range 0–30%. Limits: six piglets 13.3 kg; **subcutaneous, because pig skin will not distend enough for intradermal**; 1 mL per site, ten to twenty times a wheal; recording stops at 30 min; no duration measured, deliberately, because pinprick alters cutaneous flow.
**Newton DJ, Burke D, Khan F, McLeod GA, Belch JJF, McKenzie M, Bannister J.** Skin blood flow changes in response to intradermal injection of bupivacaine and levobupivacaine, assessed by laser Doppler imaging. *Regional Anesthesia and Pain Medicine* 2000;25(6):626–631. DOI `10.1053/rapm.2000.9853` (also cited as `10.1097/00115550-200011000-00014`). PMID 11097672. — **`FULL TEXT HELD`****.** Contributes: absolute perfusion units with **both** a saline site and an untreated site; the untreated site not changing significantly (P = .307); the late below-baseline drift after 40 min; the depot-decay hypothesis with its 0.125 mg threshold; the honest statement that saline subtraction is an imperfect way to remove needle trauma because drug and trauma effects may not be additive; **and the pallor/flux dissociation stated by the authors of their own data.** Limits: bupivacaine and levobupivacaine, **not ropivacaine**; 10 male volunteers 28–45; 0.1 mL; 90-minute ceiling; no pinprick, deliberately.
**Newton DJ, McLeod GA, Khan F, Belch JJF.** The effect of adjuvant epinephrine concentration on the vasoactivity of the local anesthetics bupivacaine and levobupivacaine in human skin. *Regional Anesthesia and Pain Medicine* 2004;29(4):307–311. DOI `10.1016/j.rapm.2004.04.011`. — **`FULL TEXT HELD`****. The definitive epinephrine-in-skin study.** Contributes: 28 solutions across four visits; **no significant difference between 1:800,000, 1:400,000 and 1:200,000** (P = .586); the AUC values; epinephrine at 2 min giving +25% against saline’s +180%; maximal constriction by 10 min alone and \\~20 min combined; and the explicit statement that 1.25 µg/mL is 1:800,000 and 2.5 µg/mL is 1:400,000. Limits: 10 healthy males 18–35; bupivacaine and levobupivacaine, not ropivacaine; 0.1 mL, 27 G; 60-minute ceiling; technique reproducibility 15–20%.
**Newton DJ, McLeod GA, Khan F, Belch JJF.** Vasoactive characteristics of bupivacaine and levobupivacaine with and without adjuvant epinephrine in peripheral human skin. *British Journal of Anaesthesia* 2005;94(5):662–667. DOI `10.1093/bja/aei095`. — **`FULL TEXT HELD`****.** Contributes: eight concentrations from 0.008% to 0.75%; **net vasoconstriction only at 0.008–0.031%, with 0.0625% ≈ saline**; every epinephrine-containing response below saline; the Åberg calcium-mobilisation/membrane-stabilisation mechanism; the iontophoresis finding that 0.5% sodium chloride has no vasodilatory effect in human skin; and the reason pinprick was not attempted. Limits: as 2004.
**Fruhstorfer H, Wagener G.** Effects of intradermal lignocaine and mepivacaine on human cutaneous circulation in areas with histamine-induced neurogenic inflammation. *British Journal of Anaesthesia* 1993;70(2):167–172. DOI `10.1093/bja/70.2.167`. — **`FULL TEXT HELD`****. The paper that explains the pallor/flux dissociation.** Contributes: flux and **pallor area measured together and weighed**; plain lignocaine raising flux with no colour change at all; plain mepivacaine producing pallor in 9 of 12 with flux still enhanced; **the precapillary/postcapillary mechanism**; the three-mechanism account including sympathetic tone release; the flare spreading up to several centimetres; the brachial-artery infusion evidence (no resistance change, marked venous tone increase); mepivacaine antagonising ornipressin in the postcapillary bed; and Covino’s position that vasoconstrictive and anaesthetic potency are unrelated. Limits: lignocaine and mepivacaine, **not ropivacaine**; histamine-primed skin, which is a non-physiological starting condition chosen deliberately; 26 G, 100 µL; 24 volunteers.
**Nakamura K, Toda H, Kakuyama M, Nishiwada M, Yamamoto M, Hatano Y, Mori K.** Direct vascular effect of ropivacaine in femoral artery and vein of the dog. *Acta Anaesthesiologica Scandinavica* 1993;37(3):269–273. — **`FULL TEXT HELD`****.** Contributes: the artery/vein split; ropivacaine’s maximum at 10⁻³ M and its collapse to 16.3%/5.5% at 10⁻² M; lidocaine doing nothing at basal tension; **veins relaxing under phenylephrine tone while arteries constricted further**; phentolamine and yohimbine not affecting it, so not α-adrenergic; clinical epinephrine at 2–4 × 10⁻⁵ M producing maximal contraction; **the volume/dilution reconciliation of Kopacz against Guinard 1991**; the steric explanation for duration; and the pH detail (ropivacaine precipitating in Krebs, HEPES at 10⁻² M falling to pH 6.1). Limits: dog femoral conductance vessels, not dermal microvessels; in vitro; denervated.
**Gherardini G, Samuelson U, Jernbeck J, Åberg B, Sjöstrand N.** Comparison of vascular effects of ropivacaine and lidocaine on isolated rings of human arteries. *Acta Anaesthesiologica Scandinavica* 1995;39:765–768. — **`FULL TEXT HELD`****. The only human-vessel comparison.** Contributes: biphasic contraction 4.5 × 10⁻⁵ to 1.5 × 10⁻³ M with release above; maxima of 27% (IMA) and 76% (RA) of noradrenaline; **lidocaine at 40% with the same peak and no statistically significant difference from ropivacaine**; endothelium removal not significant; and the statement that the constriction was insufficient to reduce surgical bleeding in pig. Limits: in vitro; internal mammary and radial arteries from surgical patients, not dermal microvessels; ropivacaine tested at seven concentrations, lidocaine at fewer.
**Tokinaga Y, Ogawa K, Yu J, Kuriyama T, Minonishi T, Hatano Y.** Mechanism of the ropivacaine-induced increase in intracellular Ca²⁺ concentration in rat aortic smooth muscle. *Acta Anaesthesiologica Scandinavica* 2007;51(9):1155–1160. DOI `10.1111/j.1399-6576.2007.01390.x`. PMID 17714580. Epub 20 Aug 2007. — **`FULL TEXT HELD`****.** Contributes: the biphasic window 3 × 10⁻⁵ to 3 × 10⁻⁴ M with relaxation from 10⁻³; the nicardipine/2-APB dissection and its additivity; **the tension/calcium dissociation implying a calcium-independent arm**; the explicit conversion of the clinical range to (0.6–2.4) × 10⁻² M; **the statement that clinical concentrations appear too high to contract vascular smooth muscle**; the interstitial-dilution reconciliation; and the statement that **local injected concentration has never been measured** — **a statement that was true of the vascular literature but is false in general; see Kimi 2012 and Yamashiro 2016 in 4.5, which this page previously missed.** Limits: rat aorta, a conductance vessel; endothelium-denuded; in vitro; relaxation at high concentration acknowledged as unexplained.
**Yu J, Tokinaga Y, Kuriyama T, Uematsu N, Mizumoto K, Hatano Y.** Involvement of Ca²⁺ sensitisation in ropivacaine-induced contraction of rat aortic smooth muscle. *Anesthesiology* 2005;103(3):548–555. DOI `10.1097/00000542-200509000-00018`. PMID 16129980. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** The companion paper. Same biphasic calcium and tension result, with the signalling worked out: PKC, Rho kinase and p44/42 MAPK-mediated calcium sensitisation, each inhibitor attenuating contraction dose-dependently, with a greater tension/calcium ratio than potassium chloride produces.
**Sohn J-T, Ok S-H, Kim H-J et al.** Vasoconstriction potency induced by aminoamide local anesthetics correlates with lipid solubility. *Anesthesiology Research and Practice* 2012;2012:170958. DOI `10.1155/2012/170958`. — **`FULL TEXT HELD`**** (open access). Found late, via the citation graph of Cederholm 1992, and it is the most important addition to this document since Yu 2019.** Contributes: **an independent replication of ropivacaine’s 3 × 10⁻⁴ M constricting peak**, matching Tokinaga 2007 from a different laboratory; per-drug peaks showing **ropivacaine and lidocaine do NOT share a window** (3 × 10⁻⁴ versus 3 × 10⁻³ M); the biphasic pattern confirmed in all four drugs in both denuded and intact aorta; **the ridge-regression demonstration that octanol/buffer partition coefficient is the dominant determinant of vasoconstriction potency** (SRC −0.4701, model r² = 0.923; log ED₅₀ vs log P r² = 0.9563, P \\< 0.001); the vasoconstriction potency order levobupivacaine \\> ropivacaine \\> lidocaine \\> mepivacaine with every pairwise difference P \\< 0.001; the octanol/buffer coefficients (2.539 / 2.060 / 1.633 / 1.322); relative conduction-block potencies (8 / 6 / 2 / 1.5, from C-fibre data in isolated rabbit vagus and sciatic nerve); **the calculated expected free tissue concentration for clinical ropivacaine, 5.46 × 10⁻⁴ to 1.63 × 10⁻³ M**; a third independent statement of the interstitial-dilution mechanism; two further confirmations of epinephrine’s failure to add anything to ropivacaine; and the lipid-emulsion evidence for the lipid-solubility mechanism. Limits, one of which the authors state themselves: **rat descending thoracic aorta is a conduit vessel, and they note that blood flow to nerves and skin is governed by small resistance arterioles**; endothelium-denuded rings pretreated with L-NAME; six rats per drug; in vitro; and the tissue-concentration figure is a protein-binding calculation, not a measurement. **This paper is why sections 4.9a and 6.2 now cross-reference each other.**
**Ok S-H, Sohn J-T et al.** Ropivacaine-induced contraction is attenuated by both endothelial nitric oxide and voltage-dependent potassium channels in isolated rat aortae. *BioMed Research International* 2013;2013:565271. DOI `10.1155/2013/565271`. — **`FULL TEXT HELD`**** (open access). Found via the citation graph of Cederholm 1992.** Contributes the mechanism for the biphasic reversal that Tokinaga could not explain: endothelial denudation, L-NAME, ODQ and methylene blue all **enhance** ropivacaine’s contraction while selective neuronal and inducible NOS inhibitors do nothing, placing the brake on **endothelial** NOS acting through NO–GC–cGMP; 4-AP greatly enhances and TEA slightly enhances the contraction, placing a second, endothelium-independent brake on **voltage-dependent** and partly calcium-activated potassium channels; barium chloride, glibenclamide, fluconazole and wortmannin have no effect; indomethacin **attenuates** the contraction, so COX products are pro-contractile; and ropivacaine induces eNOS phosphorylation in HUVECs at 30 and 60 min. **Also establishes that every preparation defining the constricting window used denuded vessels, so the window describes unbraked constriction** (4.10a). Limits: rat thoracic aorta, a conduit vessel; HUVECs rather than rat aortic endothelial cells for the western blotting, which the authors flag themselves; in vitro.
**Ok S-H, Sohn J-T et al.** Direct effect of ropivacaine involves lipoxygenase pathway activation in rat aortic smooth muscle. *Canadian Journal of Anesthesia* 2009. DOI `10.1007/s12630-009-9059-0`. — **`CITATION ONLY`**, via Ok 2013’s account. Places the contraction downstream of cytosolic phospholipase A₂ activated by calcium influx, mediated **mainly by the lipoxygenase pathway and partly by cyclooxygenase** in denuded aortae. Not read at source.
**Löfström JB.** The effect of local anesthetics on the peripheral vasculature. *Regional Anesthesia* 1992;17(1). DOI `10.1136/rapm-00115550-199217010-00001`. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** Cederholm’s co-author, first article of the same volume. The governing principles: resistance to flow reduced at high concentrations and vasoconstriction at low; route of application matters; **target organ matters greatly** — skin behaving unlike placenta, lung, splanchnic bed or spinal cord; **an anti-adrenaline-like effect**; and isomers differing, mepivacaine’s doing so and bupivacaine’s not.
**Willatts DG, Reynolds F.** Comparison of the vasoactivity of amide and ester local anaesthetics: an intradermal study. *British Journal of Anaesthesia* 1985;57(10):1006–1011. DOI `10.1093/bja/57.10.1006`. — **`FULL TEXT HELD`****. The most useful comparative document in the collection and the basis of Part VI-A.** Human, intradermal, 0.1 mL, double-blind, 10 subjects, six drugs at three concentrations each, with vasoactivity and duration measured on the same blebs. Contributes: **procaine dilates and never constricts at any concentration**; amethocaine dilates with a true flare and raises weals; **mepivacaine is the strongest and most consistent constrictor of the six, P \\< 0.001 at all three concentrations**; lignocaine significant at all three, P \\< 0.05; the class split of 108/120 ester blebs dilated against 49/240 amide, P \\< 0.001; **vasoconstrictor activity declining as concentration rises** for three of the amides; the concentration–duration slopes (procaine 11.41 to mepivacaine 31.12 min per log unit, difference P \\< 0.001); **the crucial distinction that vasoactivity tracked the concentration–duration SLOPE but had no relationship to initial analgesic activity**; the finding that **plain 0.9% saline is visibly red in only 0–10% of blebs**; and that **0.1% sodium metabisulphite dilated 65% of control blebs and increased injection pain.** Limits, the first self-reported: **metabisulphite in every solution compromises their amide-versus-saline comparison** though not the amide-versus-ester contrast; colour scored once at 5–10 min rather than serially; all injections fully recovered within 80 min, so the durations are not comparable to Part II; no ropivacaine and no bupivacaine.
**Foldes FF, McNall PG 1952**; and **Padfield A 1967**, *Anaesthesia* 22:556–561. — **`CITATION ONLY`**, both via Willatts & Reynolds 1985. Foldes: intradermal procaine and chloroprocaine duration in man **not prolonged by a plasma cholinesterase inhibitor**, with or without adrenaline, so in-situ ester hydrolysis does not determine intradermal duration (bears on 9.2). Padfield: prilocaine, 2% lignocaine and 2% procaine intradermally were equi-analgesic shortly after injection, with **procaine shortest and prilocaine longest in duration, and a 60% incidence of vasodilatation with procaine.** Neither read at source.
**Altura BM, Altura BT 1974**; **Jacobs M, Keating MA 1974**; **Luduena FP 1969**; **Fairley JW, Reynolds F 1981**, *BJA* 53:1211–1216; **Aps C, Reynolds F 1976**, *BJA* 48:1171–1174. — **`CITATION ONLY`**, all via Willatts & Reynolds 1985. Contribute, in order: procaine and amethocaine inhibiting rabbit aortic contraction to several agonists from 1 nmol/L to 1 mmol/L, with potencies unrelated to anaesthetic potency; procaine facilitating electrical and mechanical activity in sheep carotid up to 20 mmol/L then blocking it and causing contraction-then-relaxation at 80 mmol/L; the claim that the longer action of L(+) prilocaine, L(+) mepivacaine and L(−) bupivacaine over their isomers was **solely** attributable to greater vasoconstriction; the **contrary** finding that L(+) mepivacaine’s longer action was not associated with greater vasoconstriction and that at the highest concentration L(+) mepivacaine was the more potent vasodilator; and a greater incidence of vasoconstriction with 0.125% than 0.25% bupivacaine **with no significant duration difference**, read as increasing concentration being offset by reduced vasoconstriction. `INFERENCE` **That last one is the cleanest statement in the literature of the trade-off Part VII is about, and it is second-hand.** Worth obtaining.
**Aps C, Reynolds F.** An intradermal study of the local anaesthetic and vascular effects of the isomers of bupivacaine. *British Journal of Clinical Pharmacology* 1978;6(1):63–68. DOI `10.1111/j.1365-2125.1978.tb01683.x`. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** Origin of the nil/pink/pale scoring method Cederholm 1994 used. 17 volunteers, eight concentrations 0.06–7.69 mmol/L. Only L(−) constricted, **maximally at 0.48 mmol/L with 92% pallor incidence**, and L(−) outlasted D(+) from 0.48–3.84 mmol/L. The historical case where colour and duration tracked together.
**Schnabl SM, Herrmann N, Wilder D, Breuninger H, Häfner HM.** Skin perfusion and pain evaluation with different local anaesthetics in a double blind randomized study following digital nerve block anaesthesia. *Clinical Hemorheology and Microcirculation* 2013;55(2):241–253. DOI `10.3233/CH-2012-1629`. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** Ropivacaine 0.75% alone raised finger perfusion to a maximum of **160.2%**; with epinephrine 1:1,000,000, no significant early change then **+66.6%** and the longest pain reduction of any group at **18.0 hours** against 4.6–8.1. Limits: 20 volunteers, 80 fingers, no operation; **fingers after a two-injection dorsal digital block, not wheals**; drug sits around named nerves. Standing: 21 citing publications, 21 classified statements, **none supporting and none contrasting**, no editorial notices. `UNSOURCED` The “60-minute recording limit” attributed to this paper in earlier project notes is not in the abstract and is probably wrong.
**Häfner HM, Schmid U, Moehrle M.** Changes in acral blood flux under local application of ropivacaine and lidocaine with and without an adrenaline additive: a double-blind, randomized, placebo-controlled study. *Clinical Hemorheology and Microcirculation* 2008. No DOI found. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** Same Tübingen group as Schnabl. 20 vascularly healthy subjects, Oberst-method blocks, 6 mL each of ropivacaine 7.5 mg/mL, lidocaine 1%, lidocaine 1% + adrenaline 1:200,000, or saline placebo; laser Doppler flux at fingerpads D II and D IV on both hands **over 24 hours.** Ropivacaine **+155.2% with sustained vasodilatation still demonstrable at 24 h**; lidocaine initially −27% then dilating; lidocaine+adrenaline −55% reversible after 40 min and no longer measurable at 6 and 24 h; **saline placebo no change at all.** Their conclusion: an adrenaline additive does not decrease blood flow more than 55% for a period of 16 minutes, so it can be safely used at the acra in healthy persons. Limits: fingerpad after a digit-base block, so the trauma is not at the measurement site — which is why saline showed nothing and why this is **not** a wheal study.
**Ghali S, Knox KR, Verbesey J, Scarpidis U, Izadi K, Ganchi PA.** Effects of lidocaine and epinephrine on cutaneous blood flow. *Journal of Plastic, Reconstructive & Aesthetic Surgery* 2008;61(10):1226–1231. DOI `10.1016/j.bjps.2007.09.011`. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** Five volunteers, 30 forearm and 40 facial injections, laser Doppler imaging, 1% lidocaine ± epinephrine 1:100,000 or 1:200,000 against phosphate-buffered saline. Significant flow increase with lidocaine in the forearm against saline; epinephrine 1:100,000 giving an immediate decrease maximal at 10 min in forearm and 8 min in face. Candidate origin of the untraced 207% figure; **not verified as such.**
**Larrabee WF Jr, Lanier BJ, Miekle D.** Effect of epinephrine on local cutaneous blood flow. *Head & Neck Surgery* 1987;9(5):287–289. DOI `10.1002/hed.2890090507`. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** Subcutaneous saline-with-epinephrine and lidocaine-with-epinephrine, laser Doppler. 1:200,000 and higher gave good vasoconstriction in both magnitude and duration; **1:400,000 differed significantly from all others (P = 0.0001)**; 1:200,000, 1:100,000 and 1:50,000 did not differ from each other. Conflicts with Newton 2004; resolution is route (subcutaneous vs intradermal).
**Li T, Ye Q et al.** Dose-response studies of ropivacaine in blood flow of upper extremity after supraclavicular block. *BMC Anesthesiology* 2017;17. DOI `10.1186/s12871-017-0447-7`. NCT02139982. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** Six concentrations 0.125–0.75%, 30 mL, n = 13 per group, brachial artery Doppler at baseline and 30 min. **ED₅₀/ED₉₅ for blood flow change 0.35%/1.94%; for sensory block 0.18%/0.33%.** Blood flow rose consistently with concentration. `INFERENCE` The vascular effect needs more drug than the sensory effect, so the two are at least partly independent. Limits: supraclavicular nerve block, brachial artery, not skin.
**Kalman / Fruhstorfer, Nolte & Ziegenhagel 1990**, *Regional-Anaesthesie* 13:97–100, on cutaneous flow after subcutaneous lidocaine with and without adrenaline or ornipressin; and **Mesnil de Rochemont & Hensel 1960**, *Naunyn-Schmiedeberg’s Archiv* 239:464–474, thermal clearance after intradermal 1% lidocaine or mepivacaine showing a reduction in flow **following an initial increase of about 20 minutes’ duration** probably attributable to injection trauma. — **`ABSTRACT`****, via Fruhstorfer 1993’s reference list only.** The 1960 paper is the earliest description of the biphasic time course this document is built on.
**Holloway GA Jr.** Cutaneous blood flow responses to injection trauma measured by laser Doppler velocimetry. *Journal of Investigative Dermatology* 1980;74(1):1–4. — **`CITATION ONLY`****.** The original demonstration that injection trauma alone raises cutaneous flow. Cited by both Cederholm 1991 and Fruhstorfer 1993 for that point. Not read.
## 15.2 Duration
**Moffitt DL, de Berker DAR, Kennedy CTK, Shutt LE.** Assessment of ropivacaine as a local anesthetic for skin infiltration in skin surgery. *Dermatologic Surgery* 2001;27(5):437–440. PMID 11359489. DOI `10.1046/j.1524-4725.2001.00204.x`. — **`FULL TEXT HELD`****.** Contributes: onset 74 s and 51 s at 5 and 7.5 mg/mL; **durations of \\>692 and \\>773 min, right-censored at the 960-minute study limit** with 1 of the 5 and 4 of the 7.5 mg/mL sites still numb; 418 min at 2 mg/mL uncensored; **0.5 mL per site, 27 G**; saline producing partial anaesthesia and one saline site reaching full anaesthesia for 20 min; the injection-pain comparison and its pH caveat; vasoconstriction noted **especially at higher concentrations**; and the 3:1 cost ratio. Limits: 18 volunteers; five times a wheal’s volume; vasoconstriction observed, not measured. Standing: 28 citing publications, 11 classified citation statements, **all mentioning — no supporting and no contrasting statement**, no editorial notices. Unchallenged and unreplicated.
**Ginosar Y, Haroutounian S, Kagan L, Naveh M, Aharon A, Davidson EM.** Proliposomal ropivacaine oil: pharmacokinetic and pharmacodynamic data after subcutaneous administration in volunteers. *Anesthesia and Analgesia* 2016;122(5):1673–1680. DOI `10.1213/ANE.0000000000001217`. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** **The only human duration figure for plain 0.5% ropivacaine observed to completion: 15.9 ± 3.5 h pinprick, \\~12 h heat pain**, from 2.5 mL subcutaneous in 15 volunteers assessed over 72 hours with an uninjected open control. Plus the pharmacokinetics: peak plasma 100 ± 41 ng/mL, terminal half-life 5.9 ± 2.3 h, AUC 593 ± 168 h·ng/mL, 99% upper prediction limit 279 ng/mL. Limits: **subcutaneous, not intradermal; 2.5 mL is 25 times a 0.1 mL wheal**; plain ropivacaine was the comparator arm of a liposomal trial, not its subject. Standing: 19 citing publications, 9 classified statements — 0 supporting, 1 contrasting, no editorial notices; the contrasting statement concerns the liposomal formulation, not the plain arm.
**Keramidas EG, Rodopoulou SG.** Ropivacaine versus lidocaine in digital nerve blocks: a prospective study. *Plastic and Reconstructive Surgery* 2007;119(7):2148–2152. PMID 17519714. DOI `10.1097/01.prs.0000260725.33655.88`. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** The 21.5-hour figure (range 19–23) against 2.4 h (1.4–4) for 2% lidocaine, 70 patients; onset 1.3 min lidocaine against 4.5 min ropivacaine; no difference in digital-brachial systolic pressure index. Limit: digital nerve block, not skin. Standing: 48 citing publications, 36 classified statements — 1 supporting, 1 contrasting, no editorial notices; the contrasting statement concerns onset time in a dental study, and **no paper has contradicted the 21.5-hour duration.**
**Vinycomb TI, Sahhar LJ.** Comparison of local anesthetics for digital nerve blocks: a systematic review. *Journal of Hand Surgery (American)* 2014;39(4):744–751. PMID 24612831. DOI `10.1016/j.jhsa.2014.01.017`. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** Six studies, 335 blocks; ropivacaine longest at 21.5 h, lidocaine shortest at 1.8. **The 335 blocks are the review’s total across all six anaesthetics; the ropivacaine figure comes entirely from Keramidas and carries none of the weight the review-level total implies.** Standing: 52 citing publications, 29 classified statements, none supporting and none contrasting.
**Safa B, Flynn B, McHardy PG, Kiss A, Haslam L, Awad IT, Gollish J, Ganapathy S.** Comparison of the analgesic duration of 0.5% bupivacaine with 1:200,000 epinephrine versus 0.5% ropivacaine versus 1% ropivacaine for low-volume ultrasound-guided interscalene brachial plexus block. *Anesthesia and Analgesia* 2021;132(4):1129–1137. DOI `10.1213/ANE.0000000000005373`. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** Doubling 0.5% to 1% did not lengthen the block: 13.8 vs 15.8 h, not significant. Limits: 60 patients, 20 per arm; interscalene block; duration by pain score above 3. Standing: 21 citing publications, 22 classified statements — 4 supporting, 0 contrasting.
**Christiansen CB, Madsen MH, Rothe C, Andreasen AM, Lundstrøm LH, Lange KHW.** The effect of ropivacaine concentration on common peroneal nerve block duration using a fixed dose. *European Journal of Anaesthesiology* 2019;36(9):673–679. DOI `10.1097/EJA.0000000000001112`. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** Dose held at 10 mg, concentration varied eightfold: sensory block 10.4–13.4 h with no significant difference; **motor block fell from 8.5 to 4.0 h with dilution.** Limits: 60 healthy volunteers; peroneal nerve block; range 0.5–4 mg/mL sits **below** 5 mg/mL. No editorial notices.
**Cederholm I, Anskär S, Bengtsson M.** Sensory, motor and sympathetic blockade during epidural analgesia with 0.5% and 0.75% ropivacaine with and without epinephrine. *Regional Anesthesia* 1994;19(1):18–33. DOI `10.1136/rapm-00115550-199419010-00005`. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** 48 male transurethral surgery patients, 20 mL. Onset at Th10 median 5.3–6.7 min; maximum segmental level median Th 2–3 (range Th5–C4); duration at Th10 median 258–264 min for 0.75% against 228–234 for 0.5%, described as a tendency; complete motor block in only about half; **majority with marked or complete sympathetic blockade in the lower limbs**; short-lasting mild hypotension in 40–70% responding to ephedrine; **adrenaline provided no significant prolongation and did not alter the sympathetic blockade.** Note the contrast with the intradermal result, where adrenaline lengthened everything — a route difference.
**Breuninger H, Nogova L, Hobbach PS et al.** Ropivacain, ein vorteilhaftes Anästhetikum für die subkutane Infusionsanästhesie. *Hautarzt* 2000;51. DOI `10.1007/s001050051210`. — **`ABSTRACT`****, in German.** Plasma levels in 10 volunteers at 300 mg with epinephrine 1:1,000,000; effect comparison in 30 volunteers with 30 mL each of lidocaine alone, lidocaine+ropivacaine, and ropivacaine alone; and **4,670 operations in 3,015 patients, ages 0.5–95, median 54.5, no patient excluded.** Low plasma levels with protracted release from the subcutis; ropivacaine significantly longer-acting than lidocaine (p \\< 0.001); low rebleeding rate; pain freedom lasting many hours; **only finger and penis blocks done without epinephrine.**
**Breuninger H et al.** Ropivacaine: an important anesthetic agent for slow infusion and other forms of tumescent anesthesia. *Dermatologic Surgery* 1999. — **`CITATION ONLY`****.** Cited in the project for 30 volunteers plus **5,220 procedures in 3,270 patients**, with epinephrine 1:1,000,000, reported as longer-lasting than lidocaine or prilocaine. **Accrual figures differ from the 2000 Hautarzt paper; do not conflate them.** Not read.
**Ropivacaine hydrochloride injection, prescribing information.** United States label, via DailyMed. — **`ABSTRACT`****-equivalent.** Local infiltration and field block: 2 mg/mL in 1–100 mL, or 5 mg/mL in 1–40 mL, in both cases **2–200 mg**; onset 1–15 min; duration 2–6 h. Limits: the 2-to-6-hour duration is time to first request for analgesia after surgical infiltration.
**Markham A, Faulds D.** Ropivacaine: a review of its pharmacology and therapeutic use in regional anaesthesia. *Drugs* 1996;52(3):429–449. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** Single-enantiomer chemistry, pKa 8.07, protein binding around 94%, the biphasic vascular effects shared with the amide class, and greater block of pain fibres than motor fibres.
**McClellan KJ, Faulds D.** Ropivacaine: an update of its use in regional anaesthesia. *Drugs* 2000;60(5). DOI `10.2165/00003495-200060050-00007`. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** High pKa, low lipid solubility, blocks Aδ and C fibres more than Aβ; less cardiotoxic than equal concentrations of racemic bupivacaine but **more so than lidocaine** in vitro. `INFERENCE` **Note “more cardiotoxic than lidocaine” — the systemic safety advantage is over bupivacaine, not over lidocaine, which mirrors exactly the local toxicity picture in 6.2.**
**Thomas J, Schug SA.** Recent advances in the pharmacokinetics of local anaesthetics: long-acting amide enantiomers and continuous infusions. *Clinical Pharmacokinetics* 1999;36(1). DOI `10.2165/00003088-199936010-00005`. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** Ropivacaine versus racemic bupivacaine: lower lipid solubility, slightly higher plasma clearance, shorter elimination half-life, similar plasma protein binding.
## 15.3 Pharmacokinetics and dose
**Gill AM, Scott NB, Abbas M et al.** Ropivacaine plasma levels following local infiltration analgesia for primary total hip arthroplasty. *Anaesthesia* 2014;69. DOI `10.1111/anae.12559`. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** 19 patients ≥65, 180 mL of 0.2% (360 mg). Total 0.081–1.707 µg/mL, mean 0.953 ± 0.323; free 0.000–0.053, mean 0.024 ± 0.011. One patient had episodes of complete heart block on ECG.
**Gromov K, Grassin-Delyle S, Foss NB et al.** Population pharmacokinetics of ropivacaine used for local infiltration anaesthesia during primary total unilateral and simultaneous bilateral knee arthroplasty. *British Journal of Anaesthesia* 2021. DOI `10.1016/j.bja.2020.11.038`. NCT04702282. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** 400 mg per knee. Peak free 0.030 vs 0.095 µg/mL and peak total 0.756 vs 1.695 µg/mL for unilateral vs bilateral. One-compartment model, first-order absorption; main covariates **protein binding**, allometrically scaled weight on clearance and volume, and unilateral/bilateral on volume.
**Hübler M, Planitz MC, Vicent O.** Early pharmacokinetics of ropivacaine without epinephrine after injection into the psoas compartment. *British Journal of Anaesthesia* 2015. DOI `10.1093/bja/aeu363`. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** 11 patients, 150 mg, nine sampling points over 30 min. **Peak within 10 minutes in all but two patients.** Total 422–3905 ng/mL, free 5–186 ng/mL, correlation 0.96, **maximum 5% unbound.** No relationship found between plasma protein concentrations and ropivacaine concentrations.
**Riff C, Guilhaumou R, Marsot A et al.** Ropivacaine wound infiltration for pain management after breast cancer mastectomy: a population pharmacokinetic analysis. *Clinical Pharmacology in Drug Development* 2018. DOI `10.1002/cpdd.452` — **`ABSTRACT`****.** 40-hour continuous wound infusion; model-predicted Cmax 1.45 ± 0.80 µg/mL occurring at hour 42.4. Relevant only as an upper reference for sustained exposure.
**Matsota P, Karalis V, Saranteas T.** Ropivacaine pharmacokinetics in the arterial and venous pools after ultrasound-guided continuous thoracic paravertebral nerve block. *Journal of Anaesthesiology Clinical Pharmacology* 2023. DOI `10.4103/joacp.joacp_353_22`. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** 18 patients, 800 mg/day. Gender affected clearance, females lower.
**Emanuelsson B-M, Persson J, Sandin S.** Intraindividual and interindividual variability in the disposition of the local anesthetic ropivacaine in healthy subjects. *Therapeutic Drug Monitoring* 1997;19(2). DOI `10.1097/00007691-199704000-00002`. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** Whether ropivacaine’s pharmacokinetics are non-linear in healthy subjects. Relevant only above this protocol’s doses.
**Gaudreault F, Drolet P, Fallaha M.** Modeling the anesthetic effect of ropivacaine after a femoral nerve block. *Anesthesiology* 2015. DOI `10.1097/ALN.0000000000000631`. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** 20 patients, 20 mL of 0.5%, sensory thresholds followed to 4 days. Effect-site amount producing 90% of maximum effect **AE90 = 20.2 mg**; at 2 × AE90 predicted onset 23.4 min and duration 22.9 h; **interindividual variability in AE50 49%**, ke0 half-life 34.7 min. `INFERENCE` The 49% interindividual variability is the useful number: it quantifies how much person-to-person variation to expect, and it is large.
**Ekström G, Gunnarsson UB.** Ropivacaine, a new amide-type local anesthetic agent, is metabolized by cytochromes P450 1A and 3A in human liver microsomes. *Drug Metabolism and Disposition* 1996;24(9):955–961. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** The metabolic route, and therefore the CYP1A2 interaction.
## 15.4 Neurotoxicity
**Malet A, Faure M-O, Deletage N et al.** The comparative cytotoxic effects of different local anesthetics on a human neuroblastoma cell line. *Anesthesia and Analgesia* 2015;120(3):589–596. DOI `10.1213/ANE.0000000000000562`. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** LD₅₀ after 20 minutes’ exposure. **The individual millimolar values recorded in the project (bupivacaine 0.95, lidocaine 3.35, prilocaine 4.32, mepivacaine 4.84, articaine 8.98, ropivacaine 13.43) were taken from the published table and could not be re-read at source.** Only the three-band grouping is confirmed from the abstract. Standing: 78 citing publications, 57 classified statements — 6 supporting, 0 contrasting, no editorial notices. **But note that “0 contrasting” refers to classified citation statements, not to the existence of studies reaching the opposite ranking, of which there are four (6.1).**
**Pérez-Castro R, Patel S, Garavito-Aguilar ZV et al.** Cytotoxicity of local anesthetics in human neuronal cells. *Anesthesia and Analgesia* 2009. DOI `10.1213/ANE.0b013e31819385e1`. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** Same human nerve-derived cell line as Malet, **10 minutes of exposure instead of 20**, and it ranks ropivacaine **second harshest of six**, behind only bupivacaine. **Directly contradicts Malet in the same cell line. Name the disagreement; do not resolve it.**
**Werdehausen R, Fazeli S, Braun S et al.** Apoptosis induction by different local anaesthetics in a neuroblastoma cell line. *British Journal of Anaesthesia* 2009. DOI `10.1093/bja/aep236`. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** SHEP cells, eight anaesthetics, at least five concentrations each, 24 h, annexin V and 7-AAD flow cytometry. Order from high to low toxicity: **tetracaine \\> bupivacaine \\> prilocaine = mepivacaine = ropivacaine \\> lidocaine \\> procaine = articaine.** Toxicity correlated with octanol/buffer coefficients and with experimental potency, **unrelated to ester or amide structure.** All commonly used local anaesthetics induced neuronal apoptosis at clinically used concentrations.
**Werdehausen R, Braun S, Fazeli S et al.** Lipophilicity but not stereospecificity is a major determinant of local anaesthetic-induced cytotoxicity in human T-lymphoma cells. *European Journal of Anaesthesiology* 2012;29(1):35–41. DOI `10.1097/EJA.0b013e32834cd6c4`. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** Jurkat cells, eight anaesthetics. Decreasing toxicity: **tetracaine, bupivacaine, ropivacaine, prilocaine, procaine, lidocaine, articaine, mepivacaine.** Correlated with octanol/buffer coefficients, independent of ester/amide linkage. **No effect of stereoisomerism** on apoptosis or necrosis; S(−) enantiomers advantageous for systemic but not local cytotoxicity. Note their own caveat: correlations in non-neuronal cells are weaker than previously reported for neuronal cells.
**Onizuka S, Yonaha T, Tsuneyoshi I.** Local anesthetics with high lipophilicity are toxic, while local anesthetics with low pKa induce more apoptosis in human leukemia cells. *Journal of Anesthesia and Clinical Research* 2011. DOI `10.4172/2155-6148.1000116`. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** HL-60 cells. LD₅₀ order **dibucaine \\> tetracaine \\> bupivacaine \\> ropivacaine \\> mepivacaine \\> lidocaine \\> procaine \\> QX-314**, correlated with logP. Apoptosis-versus-necrosis correlated with pKa, lidocaine and mepivacaine inducing the most apoptosis and significant DNA fragmentation.
**Radwan IAM, Saito S, Goto F.** The neurotoxicity of local anesthetics on growing neurons: a comparative study of lidocaine, bupivacaine, mepivacaine, and ropivacaine. *Anesthesia and Analgesia* 2002. DOI `10.1097/00000539-200202000-00016`. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** Chick embryo dorsal root ganglion neurons, growth cone collapse assay. **IC₅₀ at 15 min: lidocaine 10⁻²·⁸ M, bupivacaine 10⁻²·⁶, ropivacaine 10⁻²·⁵, mepivacaine 10⁻¹·⁶** — so lidocaine most toxic, mepivacaine safest by an order of magnitude. **Reversibility: at 20 h after washout, bupivacaine and ropivacaine were insignificant against control while lidocaine and mepivacaine remained significantly elevated.** Larger nerve growth factor concentrations did not improve reversibility. Limit: growing embryonic neurons, not mature ones.
**Kasaba T et al.** Procaine and mepivacaine have less toxicity in vitro than other clinically used local anesthetics. *Anesthesia and Analgesia* 2003. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** Cultured snail neurons. Increasing toxicity: procaine = mepivacaine, then ropivacaine = bupivacaine, then lidocaine, then tetracaine, then dibucaine.
**Yamashita A et al.** A comparison of the neurotoxic effects on the spinal cord of tetracaine, lidocaine, bupivacaine, and ropivacaine administered intrathecally in rabbits. *Anesthesia and Analgesia* 2003. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** Tissue damage ran lidocaine = tetracaine, then bupivacaine, then ropivacaine; **margin of safety appeared smallest with lidocaine.** Limit: rabbits, intrathecal, at 10% lidocaine and 2% ropivacaine.
**Yang S et al.** Local anesthetic Schwann cell toxicity is time and concentration-dependent. *Regional Anesthesia and Pain Medicine* 2011. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** At an intermediate concentration **ropivacaine and bupivacaine killed more Schwann cells than the other agents; at low concentrations none did.** Also the general finding that damage scales with concentration and exposure time.
**Lakhin RE et al.** A double-blind randomized study on the toxicity of lidocaine and ropivacaine on sciatic nerve and biceps muscle of rats. *Messenger of Anesthesiology and Resuscitation* 2019. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** Every concentration of both drugs produced inflammation and tissue damage against saline, rising with concentration. Limit: rats, perineural, small study.
**Sen O, Sayilgan N, Tutuncu AC et al.** Evaluation of sciatic nerve damage following intraneural injection of bupivacaine, levobupivacaine and lidocaine in rats. *Rev Bras Anestesiol* 2016. DOI `10.1016/j.bjane.2014.09.012`. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** 60 Wistar rats, 0.2 mL injected **intraneurally** under magnification, histology at day 7. Severe axonal degeneration in bupivacaine 0.5% 20%, levobupivacaine 5% 15%, lidocaine 2% 30% — **no statistical difference between groups.** Histopathological damage frequency and severity exceeded motor deficiency in all groups. `INFERENCE` The last point is worth keeping: **damage can be present without functional deficit.**
**Kalichman MW, Powell HC, Myers RR.** Quantitative histologic analysis of local anesthetic-induced injury to rat sciatic nerve. *Journal of Pharmacology and Experimental Therapeutics* 1989. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** Rat sciatic, 48 h, three concentrations each of 2-chloroprocaine, procaine, etidocaine, lidocaine; morphometry plus quantitative electron microscopy. All four produced a **concentration-dependent increase in every measure of injury.** Injury predominantly subperineurial. **The Schwann cells of unmyelinated fibres were more likely to undergo lysis; those of myelinated fibres to accumulate cytoplasmic lipid droplets.** The finding most specific to small fibre neuropathy in the whole collection — but **ropivacaine was not tested.**
**Kalichman MW, Powell HC, Myers RR.** Pathology of local anesthetic-induced nerve injury. *Acta Neuropathologica* 1988;75. DOI `10.1007/BF00686203`. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** 2-chloroprocaine, tetracaine, procaine, etidocaine, mepivacaine perisciatic. Perineurial barrier **not mechanically damaged** but permeability increased at 48 h; neutrophils and eosinophils in the endoneurium indicating blood-nerve barrier disruption; endoneurial edema; axonal degeneration and demyelination with large lipid droplets in Schwann cells; mast cell degranulation, fibroblast proliferation, macrophage activity.
**Kroin JS, Buvanendran A, Williams DK, Wagenaar B, Moric M, Tuman KJ, Kerns JM.** Local anesthetic sciatic nerve block and nerve fiber damage in diabetic rats. *Regional Anesthesia and Pain Medicine* 2010;35(4):343–350. DOI `10.1097/AAP.0b013e3181e82df0`. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** In streptozotocin-diabetic rats, **ropivacaine alone produced significantly more abnormal myelinated axon profiles than the same drug in non-diabetic rats; plain lidocaine did not, and produced the effect only when clonidine was added to prolong its block. Block duration was longer in the diabetic animals for every drug tested.** Bounds: rats; perineural sciatic at 0.5%; two-day histology; **no nerve exceeded 3% fibre degeneration**; a streptozotocin model is not small fibre neuropathy. Standing: 93 citing publications, 110 classified statements — 10 supporting, 0 contrasting, no editorial notices.
**Yu ZY, Geng J, Li ZQ et al.** Dexmedetomidine enhances ropivacaine-induced sciatic nerve injury in diabetic rats. *British Journal of Anaesthesia* 2019. DOI `10.1016/j.bja.2018.08.022`. — **`ABSTRACT`****. The independent replication of Kroin, and it was not in the previous version of this page.** Streptozotocin diabetic Sprague-Dawley rats and controls; ropivacaine 0.5%, dexmedetomidine 20 µg/kg, the combination, or saline, all in 0.2 mL, perisciatic, n = 8 per group; nerves harvested day 7. **No nerve fibre damage from any treatment in non-diabetic controls. In diabetic rats ropivacaine induced significant nerve fibre damage**, enhanced by dexmedetomidine — slowed motor nerve conduction velocity, decreased axon density, decreased G ratio, with demyelination, axon disappearance and empty vacuoles on electron microscopy, and increased nerve IL-1β and TNF-α. Their own caveat: the dexmedetomidine dose was considerably higher than clinical practice.
**ten Hoope W, Hollmann MW, de Bruin K et al.** Pharmacodynamics and pharmacokinetics of lidocaine in a rodent model of diabetic neuropathy. *Anesthesiology* 2017. DOI `10.1097/ALN.0000000000002035`. **DOI verified at source.** `INFERENCE` **Note for anyone cross-checking: an independent audit of this page screened “ten Hoope 2017” under DOI 10.1097/ACO.0000000000000506, which is a different paper in *Current Opinion in Anaesthesiology*. The Anesthesiology original research paper is the one cited here, and its editorial-notice screen should be re-run against the correct DOI.** — **`ABSTRACT`****.** 25 Zucker Diabetic Fatty rats at 20 weeks with neurophysiologically confirmed neuropathy, plus controls. **Minimum local anaesthetic dose of lidocaine for sciatic motor block 0.9% in diabetic against 1.4% in control; intraneural ¹⁴C-lidocaine higher in diabetic at 60 min; lower inhibitory concentration for blocking sodium currents in neuropathic dorsal root ganglion neurons on patch clamp.** Conclusion: both pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic mechanisms prolong block duration in neuropathy.
**Kalichman MW, Calcutt NA.** Local anesthetic-induced conduction block and nerve fiber injury in streptozotocin-diabetic rats. *Anesthesiology* 1992. DOI `10.1097/00000542-199211000-00017`. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** 500 µL of 1% lidocaine or procaine perisciatic at midthigh in control and 4-week diabetic rats. In controls procaine was much less effective than lidocaine; lidocaine’s block did not differ between groups; **procaine’s block was sufficiently enhanced in diabetic rats to become comparable to lidocaine in control nerves.** At 2 days, lidocaine induced edema in both, but **4% lidocaine induced significantly more edema in diabetic nerves.** No fibre injury in saline-treated nerves.
**Umek N.** The influence of diabetic peripheral neuropathy on the duration of sciatic nerve block with 1.3% liposomal bupivacaine and 0.25% bupivacaine hydrochloride in a mouse model. *Pharmaceutics* 2022;14(9):1824. DOI `10.3390/pharmaceutics14091824`. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** 24 female C57BL/6J-OlaHsd mice. Plain bupivacaine sensory block 106 min control vs 117.1 min diabetic; **liposomal 118 min control vs above the 270-minute study limit in diabetic.**
**Wehrfritz A, Namer B, Ihmsen H, Mueller C, Filitz J, Koppert W, Leffler A.** Differential effects on sensory functions and measures of epidermal nerve fiber density after application of a lidocaine patch (5%) on healthy human skin. *European Journal of Pain* 2011;15(9):907–912. DOI `10.1016/j.ejpain.2011.03.011`. — **`ABSTRACT`****. The only human measurement.** 42 days of 5% lidocaine patches on healthy skin produced a moderate but significant fall in epidermal nerve fibre density in skin blister roofs, with no fall under placebo. **Sensory thresholds returned to baseline within two days of stopping; the fibre count did not.** Limits: topical lidocaine, not injected ropivacaine; six weeks; a patch on intact skin, not a wheal; healthy volunteers with no fibre loss to begin with. Standing: 41 citing publications, 32 classified statements — 5 supporting, 0 contrasting.
**Cederholm I, Briheim G, Rutberg H, Dahlgren C.** Effects of five amino-amide local anaesthetic agents on human polymorphonuclear leukocytes measured by chemiluminescence. *Acta Anaesthesiologica Scandinavica* 1994;38:704–710. — **`CITATION ONLY`**, via the thesis abstract. Contributes the possible local anti-inflammatory effect (6.8). Not read.
**Aps JKM, Badr N.** Narrative review: the evidence for neurotoxicity of dental local anesthetics. *Journal of Dental Anesthesia and Pain Medicine* 2020;20(2):63. DOI `10.17245/jdapm.2020.20.2.63`. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** 2,802 hits screened, 23 eligible: 17 in vitro studies, 3 reviews, 3 national-inquiry audits. **“All dental local anesthetics are potentially neurotoxic in a concentration and/or exposure time fashion”** and **“there is no unanimity in the publications about which local anesthetic is the least or the most neurotoxic.”** Notes there is no consensus on which cell lines to use, which makes comparison between studies ambiguous. **This is the honest summary of section 6.1 and should be quoted to the human when the toxicity ranking comes up.**
**Kang D-K, Zhao L-Y, Wang H-L.** Cytotoxic effects of local anesthesia through lidocaine/ropivacaine on human melanoma cell lines. *Rev Bras Anestesiol* 2016. DOI `10.1016/j.bjane.2015.04.002`. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** A375 and Hs294T melanoma cells. At 72 h: lidocaine 2% → 10 ± 2% / 14 ± 2% viability; ropivacaine 0.75% → 15 ± 3% / 25 ± 3%; the combination → 10 ± 2% / 18 ± 2%; melphalan control → 8 ± 1% / 12 ± 2%. Caspase-3 and caspase-8 mediated. **The combination was not worse than lidocaine alone, so this does not support Zhao 2014.** Melanoma cells, not neurons.
**Popitz-Bergez FA, Leeson S, Strichartz GR.** Relation between functional deficit and intraneural local anesthetic during peripheral nerve block: a study in the rat sciatic nerve. *Anesthesiology* 1995. DOI `10.1097/00000542-199509000-00018`. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** 0.1 mL of 1% ¹⁴C-lidocaine. Total intraneural drug near steady state by 3 min at \\~14.3 nmol/mg wet tissue, stable \\~12 min, zero by 70 min. **1.6% of the injected dose intraneurally at full block, 0.3% when deep pain returned, 0.065% at full recovery, with the longitudinal distribution constant throughout.** Intraneural concentrations at full block corresponded to equilibration in **0.7–0.9 mM** and at partial recovery 0.2–0.3 mM. Independently establishes that tissue concentration during a working block is three orders of magnitude below the syringe.
## 15.5 Mixing
**Kim JM, Goto H, Arakawa K.** Duration of bupivacaine intradermal anesthesia when the bupivacaine is mixed with chloroprocaine. *Anesthesia and Analgesia* 1979. DOI `10.1213/00000539-197909000-00003`. — **`ABSTRACT`****. The most transferable mixing result: human, intradermal, double-blind, 20 volunteers.** Bupivacaine 0.5 mg alone **139 ± 78 min**; with chloroprocaine 3 mg **36 ± 16 min**; chloroprocaine 3 mg alone **24 ± 11 min.** Proposed mechanism: competition for nonspecific receptors at the biophase and in surrounding nerve tissue.
**Corke BC, Carlson CG, Dettbarn W-D.** The influence of 2-chloroprocaine on the subsequent analgesic potency of bupivacaine. *Anesthesiology* 1984. DOI `10.1097/00000542-198401000-00006`. — **`ABSTRACT`****. The finding that changes the practical advice.** Isolated rat sciatic nerve. Bupivacaine alone: 50% CAP recovery in 50 ± 4 min. **After 2-chloroprocaine pretreatment allowed to fully recover: 25 ± 5 min. After the chloroprocaine metabolite 4-amino-2-chlorobenzoic acid, which produces no block at all: 22 ± 5 min.** Neither pretreatment affected the rate of recovery once amplitude became measurable. **The interference survives full washout and is caused by the metabolite.**
**Galindo A, Witcher T.** Mixtures of local anesthetics: bupivacaine-chloroprocaine. *Anesthesia and Analgesia* 1980. DOI `10.1213/00000539-198009000-00009`. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** Rat sciatic. Equal parts commercial chloroprocaine 2% and bupivacaine 0.5% gave a **chloroprocaine-like** block; **changing the mixture pH from 3.60 to 5.56 made it bupivacaine-like.** Mixtures of commercial solutions are unpredictable and depend on the pH as much as on the drugs.
**Yousef S et al.** The effect of combining lidocaine and ropivacaine on the duration and onset time of an ultrasound-guided infraclavicular brachial plexus nerve block. *European Journal of Anaesthesiology* 2025;42(12):1046–1055. PMID 40859881. DOI `10.1097/EJA.0000000000002261`. NCT06381622. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** 847 min for ropivacaine alone against 536 and 671 for the two mixtures; onset unchanged at 17–18 min. **The full-dose mixture contained the identical 150 mg of ropivacaine as the plain arm and still lost 177 minutes**, so the shortening is the mixing and not the dose. Both mixtures carried epinephrine 1:200,000 and came out shorter anyway. Limits: brachial plexus block, not skin; 78 patients across three arms; the difference between the two mixtures lost significance after adjustment for multiple testing. Standing: published Sept 2025, one citing publication, no classified statements yet.
**Gricourt Y, Occéan BV, Favrelle A et al.** Mixture of lidocaine and ropivacaine as a local anesthetic in WALANT surgery. *Journal of Hand Surgery (American)* 2025;50(7):790–796. DOI `10.1016/j.jhsa.2025.03.010`. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** 91 patients; 20 mL of 1% lidocaine with epinephrine against 18 mL of the same plus 2 mL of 0.75% ropivacaine. **Median analgesia 8 hours in both arms**; recovery scores, rescue analgesia, seven-day pain scores and chronic pain at three months all unchanged. Limits: tests the **reverse** direction of mixing; median with a 4-to-12-hour interquartile range is blunt; hand surgery, not leg skin. Standing: published July 2025, no classified statements yet.
**Zhao G et al.** Intrathecal lidocaine neurotoxicity: combination with bupivacaine and ropivacaine and effect of nerve growth factor. *Life Sciences* 2014. — **`ABSTRACT`****.** **Lidocaine’s neurotoxicity was significantly increased by combining it with ropivacaine**; combining with bupivacaine at up to one-to-one was not. Limits: rats, intrathecal, at 5% lidocaine and 1.5% ropivacaine — far above anything used in skin.
## 15.6 Availability in Mexico
**COFEPRIS-registered ropivacaine presentations**, via Vademecum Mexico, Medicamentos PLM and Actuamed; **retail prices checked September 2026** at Farmacia La Paz, Vitau, Farmalisto, Sanorim and MercadoLibre. — **`UNSOURCED`**** aggregators and listings, not verified at a primary regulatory source.** See 11.1 for the detail and the caveats.
## 15.7a Sources added by the independent gap audit
**These were absent from every earlier version of this page.** Two of them overturned a central claim.
### Local tissue pharmacokinetics — the papers that refuted “never measured”
**Kimi H, Yamashiro M, Hashimoto S.** The local pharmacokinetics of ³H-ropivacaine and ¹⁴C-lidocaine after maxillary infiltration anesthesia in rats. *Anesthesia Progress* 2012;59(2):75–81. DOI `10.2344/11-14.1`. PMC3403585. — **`FULL TEXT HELD`**** (open access). The most consequential addition to this document.** Contributes: **the first tissue concentration–time curve for infiltrated ropivacaine**, showing an eighteen-fold fall within 30 seconds and passage out of the constricting window by 20–60 minutes (4.5); **the head-to-head ropivacaine-versus-lidocaine retention comparison** that partly resolves the open problem in 4.9a (7.0% versus 1.0% of maximum at 30 minutes); the **equilibrium-dialysis tissue affinities** — ropivacaine about 2× lidocaine for mucosa and about 12× for nerve, higher for mucosa, nerve, brain, liver and serum but not bone; the **maxillary nerve reversal**, where ropivacaine collapsed to 10.4% by 2 minutes while lidocaine held at 79.7%; and TLC confirmation that 91.0% of mucosal radioactivity at 60 minutes was unmetabolised drug. Limits: **rat palatal mucosa is close to the worst available proxy for leg dermis** — thin, keratinised, bound to bone, and sitting over bone foramina that act as a diffusion sink; **20 µL against a 50–100 µL wheal**; and the figures are **tissue-homogenate averages over 150–200 mg specimens**, so the depot centre is understated by roughly 7–10× and the concentration gradient is flattened.
**Yamashiro M, Hashimoto S, Yasuda A, Sunada K.** Epinephrine affects pharmacokinetics of ropivacaine infiltrated into palate. *Anesthesia Progress* 2016;63(2):71–79. DOI `10.2344/0003-3006-63.2.71`. PMC4896045. — **`FULL TEXT HELD`**** (open access).** Same group and model, **with laser Doppler blood flow measured alongside tissue concentration — the closest anyone has come to the causal chain** (4.5, 4.11 item 3). Contributes: plain ropivacaine peaking at 219.5 ± 37.8 ng/mg at 2 minutes and sitting at about 10% of maximum from 20 to 50 minutes; **flow indistinguishable from saline until 2.5 minutes, then falling progressively to 77% of control at 45 minutes, significant from 40 to 60 minutes** — i.e. **constriction appearing as the drug descends into the window, not at peak concentration**; epinephrine retaining 3–5× more drug from 10 to 60 minutes and reaching the contralateral side; autoradiography showing 13% of peak still present at 60 minutes with epinephrine; serum radioactivity holding at about 30% of peak from 2 to 6 hours; and the human metabolite note (2,6-pipecoloxylidide and 3-hydroxyropivacaine, with the former accumulating in advanced renal disease). **Also the one study that contradicts the epinephrine-blunting finding** (4.8): 0.5% ropivacaine significantly **augmented** and prolonged epinephrine’s flow reduction, with the divergence at 50 and 60 minutes. Limits: n = 4 for the flow arm; **flow recorded at the lip while concentration was measured at the palate**; rat; epinephrine at 10 µg/mL, twice Cederholm’s and Kopacz’s.
### The pig conflict
**Guinard JP, Carpenter RL, Owens BD, Nadir B.** Ropivacaine versus bupivacaine after subcutaneous injection in pigs. *Regional Anaesthesia* 1991;16:268–271. DOI `10.1136/rapm-00115550-199116050-00006`. — **`PAYWALLED`****; abstract only.** 7 piglets 12.2–20.4 kg, **10 mL per site** subcutaneously, 0.25% ropivacaine or bupivacaine each ± epinephrine 5 µg/mL, plus saline and an untreated site; laser Doppler at baseline and 10 minutes; 5 cm incision with blood loss over 10 minutes. **Result: no difference between ropivacaine and bupivacaine; epinephrine reduced flow (P \\< 0.05).** Two corrections follow (4.5): **the authors themselves rejected large volume as the explanation for the conflict with Kopacz, because saline alone did not show the same effect** — so Nakamura’s volume reconciliation is a contested hypothesis, not the authors’ account; and **the deeper conflict is that Kopacz separated the two drugs decisively where Guinard found them indistinguishable.**
### Claim provenance
**Ghali S et al. 2008** (already in 15.1) — **the 207% figure is now traced to this paper and it means “to 207% of baseline,” i.e. +107%**, from 0.5 mL subdermal forearm injections in five volunteers, against 133% for phosphate-buffered saline, with flow still elevated beyond 40 minutes (section 14).
**Breuninger H et al.** Langenbeck’s Archives of Surgery 2000. PMID 10958513. — **`ABSTRACT-ONLY`****.** The third paper in the series: **5,020 operations in 3,270 patients.** With the 1999 and 2000 Hautarzt papers the counts do not increase monotonically, so these are **separate publications with likely overlapping experience**, not successive cutoffs (10.4).
### Flow, drug and block measured together
**Bernards CM, Kopacz DJ.** *1999.* PMID 10519498. — **`ABSTRACT-ONLY`****. The only human study measuring all three links.** Superficial peroneal nerve, 8 volunteers, 1% lidocaine ± epinephrine 2.5 µg/mL; **microdialysis lidocaine every 5 minutes, laser Doppler flow and sensory block every 10 minutes, for 5 hours.** Epinephrine lowered flow, slowed clearance and prolonged block, with a two-compartment model. Perineural, not dermal; lidocaine, not ropivacaine.
**Liu S, Carpenter RL, Chiu AA, McGill TJ, Mantell SA.** *Reg Anesth* 1995;20:378–384. PMID 8519713. — **`ABSTRACT-ONLY`****. Previously listed as “worth getting”; now obtained, and weaker than assumed.** Ten 0.2 mL subcutaneous forearm wheals for pinprick with matched abdominal sites for flow; 1% lidocaine or 0.25% bupivacaine with epinephrine from none to 1:3,200,000; flow for 6 hours. **Epinephrine prolonged analgesia dose-dependently, but flow returned to baseline by 30 minutes while the duration benefit persisted, and duration correlated with vasoconstriction at only one timepoint — 15 minutes, r = 0.53 lidocaine and r = 0.57 bupivacaine** (5.8.3).
### Neuropathic skin — the LDIflare literature
**Krishnan STM, Rayman G.** *Diabetes Care* 2004;27(12):2930. DOI `10.2337/DIACARE.27.12.2930`. — **`ABSTRACT-ONLY`****.** Flare areas: healthy 5.5 cm²; healthy under topical anaesthetic 1.62 cm² (from 5.2); diabetes without neuropathy 2.8 cm²; **diabetic neuropathy 1.3 cm².**
**Krishnan STM et al.** *Diabetes Care* 2009. DOI `10.2337/dc08-1453`. PMC2646027. — **`ABSTRACT-ONLY`**** for this document.** The full text is open access and an independent audit confirmed the abstract values against it, but **this document has not read the paper.** LDIflare **1.59 ± 0.41 cm² painful and 1.51 ± 0.56 cm² painless diabetic neuropathy against 4.38 ± 1.40 cm² controls, P \\< 0.001**; nerve fibre densities 307.6 ± 164.5, 205.8 ± 165.3 and 424.9 ± 176.3 fibres/mm²; flare and density correlated r = 0.57, P \\< 0.0001. **Painful and painless neuropathy are indistinguishable on this measure.**
**Abraham A et al.** 2016. PMC5100917. — **`ABSTRACT-ONLY`**** for this document; full text is open access and unread here.** LDIflare sensitivity 64% in suspected small fibre neuropathy, 64% in mixed neuropathy, 86% in diabetic mixed neuropathy.
**Nabavi Nouri M et al.** 2012. DOI `10.1371/journal.pone.0034807`. — **`CITATION ONLY`****.** Third LDIflare entry point named by the audit; not obtained.
### Ectopic firing — the concentration separation
**Tanelian DL, MacIver MB.** *Anesthesiology* 1991. DOI `10.1097/00000542-199105000-00020`. — **`ABSTRACT-ONLY`****. The hard number behind argument 1 for 0.25%.** In **injured** rabbit corneal fibres, lidocaine suppressed tonic Aδ and C-fibre discharge at **1–20 µg/mL, ED₅₀ 5.7 µg/mL (\\~0.00057% w/v)**, while **blocking evoked conduction required \\>250 µg/mL (\\~0.025% w/v)** — a roughly **44-fold separation** (5.6).
**Dong XW et al.** 2008. PMID 17705679. — **`ABSTRACT-ONLY`****.** In compressed rat DRG neurons, **10 µM lidocaine suppressed the persistent sodium current and bursting; 5 mM was needed to block single spikes** — a 500-fold separation.
**Yatziv S-L, Devor M.** 2019. — **`ABSTRACT-ONLY`****.** Selective silencing of DRG ectopia suppresses neuropathic pain. The behavioural counterpart.
**Koplovitch P, Devor M.** Dilute lidocaine suppresses ectopic neuropathic discharge in dorsal root ganglia without blocking axonal propagation: a new approach to selective pain control. *Pain* 2018. DOI `10.1097/J.PAIN.0000000000001205`. — **`ABSTRACT-ONLY`****. The only paper in this collection giving the separation as a percentage.** In vivo rat, epidural application to the DRG surface: **0.02% or 0.2% lidocaine substantially suppressed DRG electrogenesis with only a modest effect on impulse propagation through the foramen.** Topical opiates and GABA blocked neither. Proposed as a strategy for post-herpetic neuralgia, trigeminal neuralgia, phantom limb pain, CRPS and radicular low back pain. `INFERENCE` **0.02% is twelve times more dilute than 0.25%** (5.6).
**Kirillova I, Teliban A, Gorodetskaya N et al.** Effect of local and intravenous lidocaine on ongoing activity in injured afferent nerve fibers. *Pain* 2011. DOI `10.1016/J.PAIN.2011.02.046`. — **`ABSTRACT-ONLY`****. The proper dose-response, and it carries the key caveat.** Rat sural and lateral gastrocnemius-soleus nerves, 4–11 days after crush; ectopic activity recorded from 75 A-fibres and 69 C-fibres; lidocaine locally at **1 to 1000 µg/mL** or intravenously. Local and intravenous lidocaine both depressed ectopic activity dose-dependently, and responses to heat or mechanical stimulation were **not** suppressed even at the highest concentrations. **“Depression was weaker in C- than in A-fibres.”** `INFERENCE` **Since small fibre neuropathy is a C-fibre condition, this is the most important qualification on argument 1 in 10.4.**
**Xiao WH, Bennett GJ.** C-fiber spontaneous discharge evoked by chronic inflammation is suppressed by a long-term infusion of lidocaine yielding nanogram per milliliter plasma levels. *Pain* 2008. DOI `10.1016/J.PAIN.2008.02.018`. — **`ABSTRACT-ONLY`****.** A 7-day infusion at a plasma level of **210 ng/mL completely inhibited muscle C-fibre spontaneous discharge and suppressed cutaneous C-fibre discharge by 50%**, with no effect on A-fibre spontaneous discharge; reduced mechano-hyperalgesia but not allodynia or heat-hyperalgesia.
**Revenko SV, Borovikova LV, Ermishkin VV.** Stimulus-dependent effects in the actions of sodium channel blockers on sensory C-units. *Neuroscience and Behavioral Physiology* 2000. DOI `10.1007/BF02471788`. — **`ABSTRACT-ONLY`****.** Proposes **stimulus-dependent inhibition** of cutaneous C-fibre mechanothermosensitive units as the explanation for “the paradoxical analgesic effect of local anesthetics at low concentrations, insufficient to block axonal conduction.”
**Woolf CJ, Wiesenfeld-Hallin Z.** The systemic administration of local anaesthetics produces a selective depression of C-afferent fibre evoked activity in the spinal cord. *Pain* 1985. DOI `10.1016/0304-3959(85)90006-5`. — **`ABSTRACT-ONLY`****.** Systemic lidocaine up to 10 mg/kg and tocainide up to 100 mg/kg produced **no** conduction block in Aβ, Aδ or C primary afferents, yet both significantly suppressed the C-fibre-evoked polysynaptic reflex. **So the analgesia is not a weak conduction block.**
**Kankel J et al. 2012**, “Differential effects of low dose lidocaine on C-fiber classes in humans”; and **Miclescu A et al. 2015**, DOI `10.1016/j.sjpain.2015.04.026`. — **`CITATION ONLY`****, neither obtained.** Named by an independent audit as **the human microneurography entry points** and the closest available approach to the missing experiment: recording a cutaneous C-fibre during a local anaesthetic wheal in a person. **Worth obtaining if the endpoint argument in 5.6 ever needs to rest on human data.**
### Fibre selectivity — the contested premise
**Gissen AJ, Covino BG, Gregus J.** Differential sensitivities of mammalian nerve fibers to local anesthetic agents. *Anesthesiology* 1980. DOI `10.1097/00000542-198012000-00006`. — **`ABSTRACT-ONLY`****.** For lidocaine, tetracaine, etidocaine and bupivacaine, **large fast A fibres were blocked at the lowest concentration, B fibres at a higher one, and C fibres required the HIGHEST concentration** for conduction blockade; procaine, chloroprocaine, cocaine, tetrodotoxin and saxitoxin behaved similarly on B and C fibres. **Directly contrary to the clinical differential-block account this page has repeated** (section 8).
**Fink BR, Cairns AM.** Differential slowing and block of conduction by lidocaine in individual afferent myelinated and unmyelinated axons. *Anesthesiology* 1984. DOI `10.1097/00000542-198402000-00006`. — **`ABSTRACT-ONLY`****.** Single-axon recording in rabbit vagus: mean blocking concentration **0.43 ± 0.15 mM for myelinated axons against 0.63 ± 0.14 mM for unmyelinated** (P \\< 0.01), though myelinated axons showed significantly more conduction slowing before block.
**Torebjörk HE, Hallin RG.** Perceptual changes accompanying controlled preferential blocking of A and C fibre responses in intact human skin nerves. *Experimental Brain Research* 1973. DOI `10.1007/BF00233334`. — **`ABSTRACT-ONLY`****. Human microneurography, and it disagrees with the two above.** After injecting **a low concentration of lidocaine, the C waves were abolished BEFORE the A deflections**; prolonged pain disappeared while tactile sensation to weak shocks remained.
**Liu S, Kopacz DJ, Carpenter RL.** Quantitative assessment of differential sensory nerve block after lidocaine spinal anesthesia. *Anesthesiology* 1995. DOI `10.1097/00000542-199501000-00009`. — **`ABSTRACT-ONLY`****.** Quantitative, selective measurement of Aβ, Aδ and C fibre transmission over the course of a lidocaine spinal. Same authors as Liu 1995 on epinephrine and Kopacz 1989 on pig skin.
### Local anaesthetic infiltration for neuropathic pain
**Amjad M, Mashhood AA.** 2005. PMID 16300701. — **`ABSTRACT-ONLY`****.** Randomised post-herpetic neuralgia trial, **three local infiltrations at two-week intervals**: lidocaine alone gave complete relief in **16.6% at 6 weeks and 6.6% at 12 weeks**; triamcinolone plus lidocaine gave **63.3% and 83.3%.** Route depth, volume per site and concentration absent from the abstract; the steroid arm carries most of the effect (section 13 item 1).
**Cui J et al.** 2016. PMID 27777201. — **`ABSTRACT-ONLY`****.** Intradermal lidocaine plus valaciclovir against methylene blue plus lidocaine plus valaciclovir in acute zoster. **Does not isolate the anaesthetic effect**; representative of a literature where intradermal lidocaine appears only as a co-intervention.
**Ferguson L et al.** 2021. PMID 34187295. — **`ABSTRACT-ONLY`****.** Retrospective series of 18 subcutaneous lidocaine **infusions** in palliative care; patients considered **13 of 18 effective**, dose and duration varying. **Systemic delivery, not a local wheal.**
### The procedural analogue
**Bertram et al. 2026**, onabotulinumtoxinA in refractory post-herpetic neuralgia. — **`CITATION ONLY`****.** Named by the audit as representative of post-mid-2025 work; a case report, and it supplies no wheal concentration-time, neuropathic-skin perfusion or repeated-site IENFD data.
**Ghasemi M et al.** 2014. PMC3999594. — **`AUDIT-READ`**** — the independent audit read the full text; this document has not.** 40 patients with painful diabetic neuropathy randomised to intradermal botulinum toxin A or saline. **Twelve sites in a 3 × 4 grid over one foot dorsum, 100 U total at \\~8–10 U per site, insulin syringe.** At 3 weeks **6/20 versus 0/20 reported no pain on VAS, P = 0.01.** Does **not** report volume per site, needle gauge or dermal depth (12.3a). Companion entry points: **Yuan RY et al. 2009** (18-person randomised crossover, diabetic neuropathy) and **Xiao L et al. 2010** (post-herpetic neuralgia, subcutaneous botulinum toxin A 5 U/mL against 0.5% lidocaine and saline).
### IENFD measurement noise
**Selim MM et al.** 2010. PMID 20851518. — **`ABSTRACT-ONLY`****. The number that sets the detection threshold.** 36 healthy volunteers followed 24 weeks with no intervention: **mean fall of 4.18 epidermal nerve fibres/mm**, with large unsystematic within-person variation in a subset; **sex difference 12.2 fibres/mm** (6.7). Further variability work: **Lauria G et al. 2015**, DOI `10.1212/WNL.0000000000001666`; **Smith AG et al. 2005**.
### Adjacent literatures on repeated or prolonged exposure
All `ABSTRACT-ONLY`, all covered in 6.8a with transfer limits stated: **Pogrel 2000**, **Hillerup 2011** and **Gaffen & Haas 2009** on dental paraesthesia; **Bianconi 2004** and later mastectomy work on continuous wound infiltration at 0.2% for 48–55 hours; **Plachouri 2019** and **Mammucari 2011** on mesotherapy complications; **Klein 1990**, **Rubin 1999** and **Kenkel 2004** on tumescent pharmacokinetics with systemic peaks at 4–14 hours; **Abimussi 2014** and **Credie 2013** on canine ropivacaine at 0.05% versus 0.1% giving similar return of sensitivity at about 7 hours; **Thiem 2021**, DOI `10.3390/jcm10153411`, hyperspectral imaging for 120 minutes after subcutaneous 4% articaine.
### Editorial-notice screen
`INFERENCE` Fourteen of this document’s load-bearing papers were screened on **12 September 2026** against Scite editorial notices, the Crossref REST `update-to` and `updated-by` fields (which incorporate Retraction Watch records), and exact-DOI PubPeer searches: Malet 2015, Kroin 2010, Yu 2019, ten Hoope 2017, Wehrfritz 2011, Cederholm 1991/1992/1994, Kopacz 1989, Moffitt 2001, Ginosar 2016, Keramidas 2007, Yousef 2025, Gricourt 2025. **No retraction, correction, expression of concern or PubPeer record was found for any of them.** **This is not evidence that their methods are sound**, and per 0.1 it is a different measurement from “no contrasting citations.”
## 15.7b What was searched, and what it would cost to close the main gap
`INFERENCE` **This subsection exists so that the negative claims in section 13 are checkable rather than merely asserted.** A negative result is only usable if the search behind it is known.
**Databases and archives searched in the independent gap audit (12 September 2026):** PubMed and PMC; Scite; Undermind deep literature search; Consensus; Crossref and Retraction Watch metadata; PubPeer exact-DOI search; the BMJ/RAPM archive; ScienceDirect; Wiley, IOS and Sage publisher pages; the Linköping DiVA repository; the Internet Archive; ResearchGate; and broad web and institutional-repository search. Post-mid-2025 coverage combined `(ropivacaine OR local anesthetic OR botulinum toxin)` with `(intradermal OR subcutaneous OR skin perfusion OR neuropathic pain)`, narrowed by each gap’s concepts, through 12 September 2026.
**Representative search strings, retained per gap so the negatives can be reproduced or overturned:**

`INFERENCE` **Two of these negatives are worth re-testing periodically**, because a single new paper would change the document: **gap 14** (anything injected into neuropathic human skin with a vascular measurement) and **gap 1** (an anaesthetic-only wheal with a neuropathic-pain endpoint and full procedural reporting).
**What the paywalled documents cost, as quoted on 12 September 2026.** Recorded because it is actionable, not as a recommendation. **Cederholm 1992 — US\\$70.95.** Guinard 1992 — US\\$70.95. Pérez-Castro 2009 — US\\$32.45. Ordering all thirteen unobtained items was quoted at about **US\\$544.85**; one of the fourteen screened was already accessible.
`INFERENCE` **Cederholm 1992 is the only one worth considering on its own.** Three of this document’s claims are waiting on it (10.7), it is the sole source for the 0.5%, 0.375% and 0.125% points on the concentration ladder, and it is the only place a ropivacaine-versus-untreated statistical test might exist. **Nothing else on the list would change a conclusion.**
