## Revisions
2026-09-12 — The page now sets out the vessel, duration, comparative and neurotoxicity evidence for intradermal ropivacaine with a provenance tag on every claim, and recommends a single split-leg session comparing 0.75% against 0.25% rather than a fixed concentration.<br>was: 0.5% recommended outright; the neurotoxicity ranking stated as settled with ropivacaine the gentlest agent; vessel narrowing given as a principal reason for its duration; 8–13 hours given as the planning range; local tissue concentration said never to have been measured.<br>because: Kimi 2012 (10.2344/11-14.1) measured tissue ropivacaine at 279.0 ± 23.3 ng/mg 30 s after a 20 µL 0.5% injection, an 18-fold fall from the injected 15.2 mM, and 5.4% of maximum by 20 min; Yamashiro 2016 (10.2344/0003-3006-63.2.71) measured concentration and blood flow in the same preparation, flow falling to 77% of control at 45 min as the drug descended into the window; Sohn 2012 (10.1155/2012/170958) put ropivacaine’s constricting peak at 3 × 10⁻⁴ M and showed lipid solubility to be the dominant determinant of vasoconstriction potency, standardised coefficient −0.4701; Werdehausen 2012 (10.1097/EJA.0b013e32834cd6c4) and Onizuka 2011 place ropivacaine above lidocaine in local cytotoxicity; Yu 2019 (10.1016/j.bja.2018.08.022) found ropivacaine 0.5% damaged diabetic rat nerve with no damage in non-diabetic controls; Willatts & Reynolds 1985 (10.1093/bja/57.10.1006) found procaine never constricts human skin at any concentration, and mepivacaine the most consistent constrictor of six drugs; Cederholm 1994 read in full gives median complete analgesia of 30, 60, 120 and 60 minutes at 0.25, 0.5, 0.75 and 1%.<br>set aside: Malet 2015’s ranking, not followed as settled because four studies in human cell lines reach the opposite order and Pérez-Castro 2009 contradicts it in the same SH-SY5Y line; Moffitt’s \\>773 minutes, not used as a planning figure because the volume was 0.5 mL rather than 0.1 mL and the figures are right-censored at the 960-minute study limit; Nakamura’s volume reconciliation of the two pig studies, because Guinard’s own authors rejected that explanation on the grounds that saline alone did not show the effect; the 207% lidocaine flow figure, traced to Ghali 2008 and meaning 207% of baseline rather than a rise of 207%; the 375 mg ceiling, traced to Pettersson 1998 as a studied dose rather than a label maximum. Also considered and left alone: the page title, which names a topic rather than stating a claim, so the replacement does not falsify it.
2026-09-19 — Session 1 is recorded as 18 mL of the 0.75% ampoule, 135 mg, and the blood-level estimate, the dose table and the interaction note now work from 135 mg.<br>was: approximately 18 mg total (3.1, 7.2, 7.3, 7.4); a peak plasma of about 0.15 µg/mL total, about 7% of the 2.2 µg/mL toxic threshold, with a free fraction of about 0.008 µg/mL (7.2); 18 mg, 13% of the 140 mg allowance, with the lidocaine-equivalent cells blank (7.3); “None of this is likely to matter at 18 mg per session with a peak plasma around 7% of threshold. All of it matters if the sessions get larger.” (7.4)<br>because: the human, in chat on 2026-09-19: “On September 12th, it was 18 ml of 0.75 mg”, so the journal’s “0.75 mg/ml, around 18 mg total” meant 18 mL of the 7.5 mg/mL ampoule, and 18 × 7.5 = 135 mg; 7.2’s own linear scaling gives 135/12.5 × 0.1 µg/mL = 1.08 µg/mL; 135/140 = 96%; 18 mL of 1% lidocaine is 180 mg, 37% of 490 mg.<br>set aside: nothing pointed the other way. Left as they stood, for the catch-up: 7.2’s “3.6% of the ceiling” framing sentence, whose basis the page does not state; 7.3’s “At the volumes in use the ceiling does not bind”, still true at 96% but with little room; 10.3’s supply arithmetic, built on a twenty-wheal 2 mL session; and 3.3’s and 10.2’s reading of session 1. Sessions 2 (2026-09-15, 12 mL of 0.75%, 90 mg) and 3 (2026-09-18, 12 mL of 0.5% made with 4 mL of Heel products, 60 mg) are recorded in Stack & Experience History and not yet in this page’s session log.
2026-09-19 — 11.2 now carries a third caution: the ropivacaine label’s warning that it may precipitate when mixed with alkaline solutions, and so a check of any mixture for cloudiness or particles before injecting.<br>was: 11.2 named two cautions, pH and sterility; the page mentioned precipitation only as a laboratory detail, ropivacaine precipitating in Krebs’ bicarbonate buffer (Nakamura 1993, in 4.9).<br>because: US Naropin label, section 2.3, read in its own wording on 2026-09-19: solubility limited above pH 6, precipitation possible if mixed with alkaline solutions, and solutions showing particles or discolouration not to be given; the human had mixed Heel ampoules into ropivacaine on 2026-09-18.<br>set aside: the pH of the Heel ampoules, which is not known here and was not guessed.
2026-09-24 — Sections 0.0, 0.0.2, 0.1, 1, 2 and 3: the user’s own sessions, decisions and observations are taken out — two navigation rows and a phrase in a third, two operating instructions, a clause in a third and the block on the missing session-1 readings, the OWN provenance tag, the strength he injected, and the whole of section 2, Decisions closed, and section 3, Session log.<br>was: navigation rows on his white spots; instructions resting on his decisions and his own measurements; the session-1 readings table; the OWN tag; the strength he injected; his closed decisions; his session-1 log, readings and conclusions and the lidocaine-cream confound.<br>because: the record holds research and market facts, not the user's own use.<br>set aside: the questions, teaching preferences and framing that address him or his condition without recording his use, which stay; the 2026-09-19 entries above, which quote his sessions and are not rewritten.
2026-09-24 — Parts I and II, 4.2 to 5.7: statements of the user’s own sessions and observations, and the analysis resting on them, are taken out — clauses setting his wheal volume, strength and past epinephrine beside the literature, the readings of his pallor, redness, relief and onset against published figures in 4.2, 4.5, 4.6, 4.7, 4.8, 4.9, 5.1, 5.2 and 5.6, the whole of 5.6a, and the mentions of the drugs he uses in 5.7; volumes are now set against a 50–100 µL or a 0.1 mL wheal.<br>was: clauses and paragraphs on his wheal volume, his injected strength, his former epinephrine, his late pallor, his early redness, his relief at 0.75% and his onset, with the subsection 4.6 Consequences and the section 5.6a built on them.<br>because: the record holds research and market facts, not the user's own use.<br>set aside: nothing pointed the other way. Left alone: sentences about his condition, his endpoint and his question, which record no use.
2026-09-24 — Parts III to XII: the remaining statements of the user’s own use are taken out — in 6.5, 6.8, 7.4, 8, the openings of Parts VI and VI-A, 9a.3 to 9a.5, 10.2, 10.4, 10.6, 10.7, 10.8, 11.2, 12.2, 12.4, items 5, 7 and 15 of section 13, the epinephrine row of section 14, and the Kimi entry in 15.7a; 12.4 now names one addition.<br>was: the drugs and mixtures he uses, his first session, his protective-sensation choices, his own legs as evidence, a check he declined, the lidocaine-cream test on his sites, his diluent of 2026-09-18, his pallor timing and his own blanching.<br>because: the record holds research and market facts, not the user's own use.<br>set aside: nothing pointed the other way. Left alone: the split-leg test and the other recommendations, which describe a design rather than record a use.
2026-09-24 — Part IV, the Description and wherever a limit was used: the dose-ceiling material is taken out — 7.1, 7.2 and 7.3 whole, a navigation row, a clause in 11.1, the recommended maximum in 5.5, a threshold comparison in 5.4, the plasma-arithmetic reminder in 6.8a, the headroom argument in 10.4, whose list now counts six, the 375 mg row in section 14, the maximum and threshold sentences in the label, McClellan, Gill and Matsota entries, and the Rosenberg, University of Iowa and Pettersson entries; the Description no longer lists safe dose.<br>was: maximum doses and a regulatory ceiling, toxic plasma thresholds, a session’s exposure and share of an allowance set against them, and the sources given for those figures; 'Evidence page on intradermal ropivacaine wheals … tissue concentration after injection, safe dose, mixing…'.<br>because: the user ruled dose ceilings, antidotes and overdose arithmetic out of the record, keeping only harms intrinsic to a product.<br>set aside: nothing pointed the other way. Left alone: the label’s infiltration directions, session volumes and amounts, concentrations, the CYP1A2 and metabolite interactions, and harms intrinsic to the drug; the Substances / Topics terms safe dose and maximum dose, which this run does not edit; the entries above, which are not rewritten.
2026-09-24 — 12.3a, item 1 of section 13, 5.6 and 0.1: the statements that no wheal protocol for neuropathic pain with a local anaesthetic has been published are narrowed to the one published protocol with a pain outcome, lidocaine wheals in notalgia paresthetica, none being in small fibre neuropathy.<br>was: 'Nobody has published a wheal protocol for neuropathic pain with a local anaesthetic.'; 'What has never been done is an anaesthetic-only wheal with per-wheal volume, spacing, depth…'; 'See gap 1 in section 13 for the three studies that do exist'; 'neuropathic relief from a wheal'.<br>because: the pain-evidence audit found one intradermal wheal protocol with a pain outcome, 1 mL of 2% lidocaine in 5 mL of saline in 0.2–0.3 mL wheals 1 cm apart, three sessions two weeks apart, pain lower at 2 and 4 weeks and at 3 months in 20 people with notalgia paresthetica, uncontrolled (Mülkoğlu 2020, PMID 32416719).<br>set aside: nothing pointed the other way. Left alone: the statement that nobody has measured neuropathic relief from a ropivacaine wheal, which still holds.
⁠2026-09-24 — Substances / Topics: 'safe dose / maximum dose' taken out; 'late pallor / white spots' kept.<br>because: the user ruled dose ceilings, antidotes and overdose arithmetic out of the record, keeping only harms intrinsic to a product.
⁠2026-09-26 — Three passages still stating claims this page reversed on 2026-09-12 brought into line with its own corrections: the heading of 4.5, the third paragraph of 10.2 and item 3 of section 13.<br>was: '4.5 The unifying mechanism — the constricting window, and why concentration sets timing rather than depth'; 'What concentration controls is when.'; 'Local tissue concentration over time at a wheal has never been MEASURED — but it has now been calculated'; 'whether 0.25% and 0.75% differ in anything but timing'.<br>because: the page’s own 4.5, 4.11 and 10.7 already retract both claims on Kimi 2012 (10.2344/11-14.1), which measured tissue ropivacaine falling eighteen-fold within 30 seconds in rat oral mucosa, and Yamashiro 2016 (10.2344/0003-3006-63.2.71); the three passages had been left saying the opposite.<br>set aside: nothing pointed the other way. Left alone: the maximum ropivacaine dose that two Breuninger papers report, in 10.4 item 5, which waits on the user’s ruling on the injection-safety pages; the entries above, which are not rewritten.
⁠2026-09-26 — 11.2: the pH of the Heel ampoules is now recorded as stated on no Heel label found, with Traumeel S’s product information as the one checked.<br>was: 'the pH of the Heel ampoules, which is not known here and was not guessed' (the set aside of the 2026-09-19 entry above).<br>because: the Traumeel S Fachinformation, March 2023, read 2026-09-26, gives water for injection and sodium chloride as excipients, no pH, and incompatibilities as not applicable.<br>set aside: the two pages calling the Heel ampoules pH-neutral (Why It’s Better To Do Cerebrum, Coenzyme, and Ubichinon SubQ; In A Nutshell), left as they stand; the other Heel products’ product information, not read.
